=== sql2-01 | Joining three tables
--- teach
At the end of the basics you joined two tables: each row of `requests` found its user in `users` through `user_id`. You also grouped rows, kept users with LEFT JOIN, changed data and named steps with `WITH`. This course asks harder questions of a bigger database. This first lesson lets you join three or four tables in one query.

Along the way, the course also teaches the traps that make a report quietly wrong: NULLs (empty values), joins that multiply rows, and text that looks equal but is not.

### The shop

The course uses a small online shop. Its database has six **tables**. A table is a grid: each **row** is one thing (one customer, one order), and each **column** is one fact about it (a name, a price).

- `customers`: `id`, `name`, `email`, `city`, `referred_by` (another customer's id, or NULL), `joined`
- `products`: `id`, `name`, `category`, `price`
- `orders`: `id`, `customer_id`, `placed` (the moment it was ordered, written as a `'YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS'` [[timestamp|timestamp]]), `status` (`paid`, `shipped`, `canceled` or `refunded`), `coupon`
- `order_items`: one row per product in an order: `order_id`, `product_id`, `qty` (how many), `unit_price` ([[what was actually charged|unit-price]] for one, which can differ from today's price)
- `payments`: `id`, `order_id`, `amount` (an order can have several payments; refunds are negative)
- `newsletter`: `email`, `signed_up`

Look at the columns that end in `_id`. `customer_id` in `orders` holds the `id` of a row in `customers`. A column that points at a row in another table is a **[[foreign key|foreign-key]]**: a link from one table to another.

### A receipt, followed back

Picture a paper receipt. Each line names a product and a quantity. The receipt belongs to one order, and the order belongs to one customer. To read one line in full, "Ada bought 3 Notebooks", you [[follow the links|join-chain]]: from the line to its order, from the order to its customer, and from the line to its product.

In the database, one line of a receipt is one row of `order_items`. On its own it holds only numbers. Here are its first three rows:

| order_id | product_id | qty |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1 | 3 |
| 1 | 4 | 1 |
| 2 | 5 | 1 |

### One link at a time

You follow each link with one `JOIN`, and each `JOIN` gets its own `ON`. The `ON` says which rows belong together. Start with one link, from items to orders:

```sql
SELECT oi.order_id, o.customer_id, oi.qty
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id;
```

`oi` and `o` are the short nicknames (aliases) you met in the basics. `ON o.id = oi.order_id` means "pair each item with the order whose `id` equals the item's `order_id`". Now each item knows its customer's id:

| order_id | customer_id | qty |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1 | 3 |
| 1 | 1 | 1 |
| 2 | 1 | 1 |

Add a second `JOIN` to turn that id into a name:

```sql
SELECT oi.order_id, c.name, oi.qty
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id;
```

The new `ON` uses `o.customer_id`, a column that only arrived with the join before it. That is why the joins read as a chain, one link after another.

| order_id | name | qty |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Ada | 3 |
| 1 | Ada | 1 |
| 2 | Ada | 1 |

A third `JOIN` brings in the product. Here is the whole chain:

```sql
SELECT o.id, c.name, p.name AS product
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN products p  ON p.id = oi.product_id;
```

Read it as a chain: every item belongs to one order, every order to one customer, and every item names one product.

### Where to start

Put in `FROM` the table you want [[one row per result row|one-row-per]]. Here you want one row per item, so start from `order_items`. Then walk out along the foreign keys. Each item has exactly one order, one customer and one product, so these joins add columns but never add rows: 13 items go in, 13 rows come out.

### Two habits

First, give every table a short alias and put it in front of **every** column: `c.name`, `p.name`. Both `customers` and `products` have a column called `name`. Without the prefix, the database cannot tell which one you mean, and it [[stops with an error|ambiguous-column]].

Second, when two columns in the result would share a name, rename one with `AS`. `AS` means "call this column something else in the result". Above, `p.name AS product` keeps the product's name apart from the customer's `name`.

**Watch out:** each `ON` must pair the right two columns. `JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.id` runs without any error, but it matches customer 1 to order 1, customer 2 to order 2, and so on, which means nothing. Read every `ON` aloud: "this table's `id` equals the column in the other table that points at it".

::: context timestamp Dates written as text
A timestamp is a date and a time together, like `'2024-03-20 18:40:00'`: year, month, day, then hours, minutes and seconds. This shop stores them as text. The biggest part (the year) comes first and every part has a fixed width, with a zero in front where needed (`03`, not `3`). Because of that, sorting the text A-to-Z also sorts the moments from earliest to latest. This layout is an international standard called ISO 8601. The lesson "Dates and times" shows the functions that do arithmetic with these dates.
:::

::: context unit-price Why an order stores its own price
Prices change. If an order only pointed at `products.price`, then raising the price of Headphones tomorrow would change what Ada paid last March, and every old report would shift. So the shop copies the price into `order_items` at the moment of sale. You can see the difference in this data: Headphones cost 89.00 today, but Ada's order 2 charged 80.10, because that order used the `SPRING10` coupon (10% off). Receipts, invoices and flight logs all work this way: record what happened, as it happened.
:::

::: context foreign-key Links between tables
You met these in the basics, where `requests.user_id` pointed at `users.id` and `REFERENCES users(id)` in `CREATE TABLE` declared the link. A foreign key is only a column of numbers, but it is what makes joins possible: the `ON` of almost every join pairs a foreign key with the `id` it points at. In this shop, `orders.customer_id`, `order_items.order_id`, `order_items.product_id` and `payments.order_id` are all foreign keys. The advanced course has a whole lesson on making the database enforce them.
:::

::: context join-chain The shop as a chain of links
Each arrow below is a foreign key. A query that starts at `order_items` can reach every other table by following arrows, one `JOIN` per arrow.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="arrow" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <rect x="8" y="66" width="104" height="36" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="60" y="89" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">order_items</text>
  <rect x="146" y="14" width="84" height="36" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="188" y="37" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">orders</text>
  <rect x="262" y="14" width="92" height="36" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="308" y="37" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">customers</text>
  <rect x="146" y="118" width="84" height="36" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="188" y="141" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">products</text>
  <line x1="112" y1="76" x2="145" y2="44" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arrow)"/>
  <text x="64" y="50" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">order_id</text>
  <line x1="230" y1="32" x2="261" y2="32" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arrow)"/>
  <text x="250" y="66" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">customer_id</text>
  <line x1="112" y1="92" x2="145" y2="126" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arrow)"/>
  <text x="56" y="128" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">product_id</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context one-row-per The grain of a query
Engineers call "what one row stands for" the **grain** of a result. Here the grain is one order item. Deciding the grain first tells you which table goes in `FROM`. It also gives you a free test: count the rows before and after each join. If a join you expected to only add columns suddenly adds rows, a link is not one-to-one, and your totals will come out too big. The lesson "Debugging: the join that double-counts" is a whole bug hunt built on exactly that.
:::

::: context ambiguous-column The error you get without a prefix
In SQLite, `SELECT name FROM order_items oi JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id;` stops with `ambiguous column name: name`. "Ambiguous" means "could mean more than one thing". This error is a friendly one: it stops the query instead of guessing. Prefixing every column, even the ones that are not ambiguous today, keeps the query working if someone later adds a `name` column to another table.
:::
--- task
For every item in an order whose `status` is `'shipped'`, return four columns: the order id, the customer's name, the product's name and the quantity (`qty`). You will need `order_items`, `orders`, `customers` and `products`. Sort by order id, then by product name.
--- starter
SELECT oi.order_id, oi.product_id, oi.qty
FROM order_items oi;
--- solution
SELECT o.id AS order_id, c.name AS customer, p.name AS product, oi.qty
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN products p  ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE o.status = 'shipped'
ORDER BY o.id, p.name;
--- hint
Start from `order_items oi`, one row per item, and add one `JOIN` for each link: first `orders`, then `customers`, then `products`.
--- hint
The links are `JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id`, then `JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id`, then `JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id`. Keep only shipped orders with `WHERE o.status = 'shipped'`.
--- hint
Select `o.id, c.name, p.name AS product, oi.qty`, and finish with `ORDER BY o.id, p.name`.
--- check result | Seven items from orders 1, 2, 4 and 6, in order
ordered
[[1, "Ada", "Mug", 1], [1, "Ada", "Notebook", 3], [2, "Ada", "Headphones", 1], [4, "Ben", "Desk lamp", 1], [4, "Ben", "USB cable", 2], [6, "Dev", "Headphones", 1], [6, "Dev", "Mug", 2]]

+++ practice | Who made each payment
--- task
For every row of `payments`, return four columns:

- `id`: the payment's id
- `customer`: the name of the customer whose order it paid for
- `status`: that order's `status`
- `amount`

A payment points at an order through `order_id`, and the order points at a customer through `customer_id`. Sort by the payment's id.
--- starter
SELECT p.id, p.order_id, p.amount
FROM payments p;
--- solution
SELECT p.id, c.name AS customer, o.status, p.amount
FROM payments p
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = p.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
ORDER BY p.id;
--- hint
Start from `payments`, because you want one row per payment. Then follow the links: first to `orders`, then to `customers`.
--- hint
The two joins are `JOIN orders o ON o.id = p.order_id` and `JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id`.
--- check result | Nine payments; Eve's refund of -45 is the eighth
ordered
[[1, "Ada", "shipped", 22.5], [2, "Ada", "shipped", 80.1], [3, "Ben", "shipped", 30.0], [4, "Ben", "shipped", 28.0], [5, "Cleo", "paid", 37.8], [6, "Dev", "shipped", 107.0], [7, "Eve", "refunded", 45.0], [8, "Eve", "refunded", -45.0], [9, "Ada", "paid", 25.0]]
--- check source | Joins the tables instead of typing the names
[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not type any customer's name
'(Ada|Ben|Cleo|Dev|Eve)'

+++ practice | Who bought notebooks
--- task
Find every time a product named `'Notebook'` was ordered, in any order status. Return three columns:

- `customer`: the customer's name
- `order_id`: the order it was in
- `qty`: how many notebooks that line held

You will need `products`, `order_items`, `orders` and `customers`. Pick the product by its name, not by its id. Sort by `order_id`.
--- starter
SELECT oi.order_id, oi.qty
FROM order_items oi
WHERE oi.product_id = 1;
--- solution
SELECT c.name AS customer, oi.order_id, oi.qty
FROM products p
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.product_id = p.id
JOIN orders o       ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c    ON c.id = o.customer_id
WHERE p.name = 'Notebook'
ORDER BY oi.order_id;
--- hint
This time the question starts from a product. You can start `FROM products p` and walk the links the other way: product to its order lines, lines to their order, order to its customer.
--- hint
The joins are `JOIN order_items oi ON oi.product_id = p.id`, `JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id` and `JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id`. Filter with `WHERE p.name = 'Notebook'`.
--- check result | Ada twice (orders 1 and 8) and Cleo once (order 5)
ordered
[["Ada", 1, 3], ["Cleo", 5, 2], ["Ada", 8, 1]]
--- check source | Picks the product by its name
'Notebook'
--- check source absent | Does not pick the product by its id
product_id\s*=\s*1\b

+++ practice | Stationery units per customer
--- task
For each customer who has bought anything in the `'stationery'` category (any order status), return two columns:

- `name`: the customer's name
- `units`: the total `qty` of stationery items they ordered

Sort by `units`, highest first, then by `name`. Group by the customer's `id` as well as the name, so two customers who shared a name would stay apart.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY units DESC, c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN products p  ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE p.category = 'stationery'
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY units DESC, c.name;
--- hint
The starter adds up every item. The category lives in `products`, so you need one more join.
--- hint
Add `JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id`, then keep only `WHERE p.category = 'stationery'` before the `GROUP BY`.
--- check result | Ada 4, Cleo 3, Ben 1
ordered
[["Ada", 4], ["Cleo", 3], ["Ben", 1]]
--- check source | Filters on the category, not on product ids
'stationery'
--- check source | Adds up with SUM
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(

+++ practice | Two astronauts called Kim
--- schema
CREATE TABLE astronauts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO astronauts (id, name) VALUES
  (1, 'Kim'), (2, 'Ravi'), (3, 'Kim'), (4, 'Lena'), (5, 'Omar');
CREATE TABLE missions (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  year INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO missions (id, name, year) VALUES
  (1, 'Artemis II', 2025), (2, 'Gateway 1', 2027), (3, 'Lunar Base', 2029);
CREATE TABLE crew (
  mission_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES missions(id),
  astronaut_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES astronauts(id),
  role TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO crew (mission_id, astronaut_id, role) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'commander'), (1, 2, 'pilot'), (1, 3, 'specialist'),
  (2, 3, 'commander'), (2, 4, 'pilot'),
  (3, 1, 'pilot'), (3, 3, 'specialist'), (3, 4, 'commander');
--- task
This problem has its own database. `astronauts` has `id` and `name`. `missions` has `id`, `name` and `year`. `crew` has one row per seat on a mission: `mission_id`, `astronaut_id` and `role`.

For every astronaut who flies on at least one mission, return four columns:

- `id` and `name`
- `missions`: how many missions they fly
- `first_year`: the `year` of their earliest mission

Two different astronauts are both called Kim (ids 1 and 3). They are different people, so each needs their own row. Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT a.name, COUNT(*) AS missions, MIN(m.year) AS first_year
FROM crew cr
JOIN astronauts a ON a.id = cr.astronaut_id
JOIN missions m   ON m.id = cr.mission_id
GROUP BY a.name;
--- solution
SELECT a.id, a.name, COUNT(*) AS missions, MIN(m.year) AS first_year
FROM crew cr
JOIN astronauts a ON a.id = cr.astronaut_id
JOIN missions m   ON m.id = cr.mission_id
GROUP BY a.id, a.name
ORDER BY a.id;
--- hint
Run the starter: one Kim flies 5 missions, but there are only 3 missions. The two Kims were put in one group.
--- hint
Group by what tells people apart: the astronaut's `id`. Add `a.id` to both the `SELECT` and the `GROUP BY`, and sort by it.
--- check result | Kim (id 1) flies 2, Kim (id 3) flies 3; Omar, on no mission, is left out
ordered
[[1, "Kim", 2, 2025], [2, "Ravi", 1, 2025], [3, "Kim", 3, 2025], [4, "Lena", 2, 2027]]
--- check source | Groups by the astronaut's id
[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy][^;]*\ba\.id\b|[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy][^;]*astronaut_id
--- check source absent | Does not type the counts
\b[23]\s+AS\s+missions

+++ practice | Ben never bought a mug
--- task
This query should list every item Ben ordered, in any status: the order id, `customer`, `product` and `qty`, sorted by order id and then product name. It runs without an error, but it says Ben bought a Mug twice, and Ben never bought a mug.

Find the join that pairs the wrong columns and fix it. The correct result has three rows.
--- starter
SELECT oi.order_id, c.name AS customer, p.name AS product, oi.qty
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN products p  ON p.id = oi.order_id
WHERE c.name = 'Ben'
ORDER BY oi.order_id, p.name;
--- solution
SELECT oi.order_id, c.name AS customer, p.name AS product, oi.qty
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN products p  ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE c.name = 'Ben'
ORDER BY oi.order_id, p.name;
--- hint
Read each `ON` aloud: "this table's `id` equals the column that points at it". One of them points at the wrong table.
--- hint
The products join matches a product id with an order id. An item names its product in `oi.product_id`.
--- check result | Fountain pen in order 3, Desk lamp and USB cable in order 4
ordered
[[3, "Ben", "Fountain pen", 1], [4, "Ben", "Desk lamp", 1], [4, "Ben", "USB cable", 2]]
--- check source | Links products through product_id
[Pp]\.id\s*=\s*oi\.product_id|oi\.product_id\s*=\s*[Pp]\.id
--- check source absent | No join matches a product id with an order id
[Pp]\.id\s*=\s*oi\.order_id|oi\.order_id\s*=\s*[Pp]\.id

+++ practice | London's shipped revenue by category
--- task
The shop wants to know what its London customers spend on. Using only customers whose `city` is `'London'` and only orders whose `status` is `'shipped'`, return one row per product `category` with three columns:

- `category`
- `lines`: how many order items (rows of `order_items`) fall in it
- `revenue`: the sum of `qty * unit_price`, rounded to 2 decimals with `ROUND(..., 2)`

Sort by `revenue`, highest first.
--- starter
SELECT p.category, COUNT(*) AS lines
FROM order_items oi
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.category;
--- solution
SELECT p.category,
       COUNT(*) AS lines,
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS revenue
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o    ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN products p  ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE c.city = 'London' AND o.status = 'shipped'
GROUP BY p.category
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
--- hint
You need all four tables: the city is in `customers`, the status in `orders`, the category in `products`, and the money in `order_items`.
--- hint
Start from `order_items oi` and join `orders`, `customers` and `products`. Both conditions go in one `WHERE`, joined with `AND`.
--- hint
Group by `p.category`, select `COUNT(*) AS lines` and `ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS revenue`, then `ORDER BY revenue DESC`.
--- check result | electronics 169.1, home 27.0, stationery 13.5
ordered
[["electronics", 2, 169.1], ["home", 2, 27.0], ["stationery", 1, 13.5]]
--- check source | Filters on the city
'London'
--- check source absent | Does not type the revenue
169\.1|27\.0|13\.5

=== sql2-02 | LEFT JOIN: where the filter goes
--- teach
Last lesson every join was an inner `JOIN`: it keeps only rows that find a match. In the basics you also met `LEFT JOIN`, which keeps every row of the [[left table|left-table]] and fills the other side with NULL when nothing matches. This lesson shows where to put a filter in a LEFT JOIN. There is a catch here that trips up almost everyone once.

### The picture

A teacher wants a list of every student with the number of homeworks they handed in on time, 0 for anyone with none. The teacher writes every student down, with each homework next to the name. Then every line where the homework was not on time gets crossed out. But a student with no homework at all has a blank on that line, and a blank is not "on time", so that line is crossed out too. Now they are missing from a list that was meant to include everyone.

### The LEFT JOIN keeps everyone

Here is every customer with their orders:

```sql
SELECT c.name, o.id, o.status
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
ORDER BY c.name, o.id;
```

The last four rows:

| name | id | status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dev | 6 | shipped |
| Eve | 7 | refunded |
| Finn | NULL | NULL |
| Gus | NULL | NULL |

Finn and Gus have no orders, so the order columns are **NULL**, which means "no value here".

### The query that looks right

You want every customer with their number of *live* orders: orders whose status is `paid` or `shipped`. `IN ('paid', 'shipped')` means "is one of these values". This looks right:

```sql
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS live_orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name;
```

It returns only four rows: Ada 3, Ben 1, Cleo 1, Dev 1. Eve, Finn and Gus are gone.

### Why they vanish

A **clause** is one part of a query that starts with a keyword: the `FROM` clause, the `WHERE` clause. The database does the `FROM` and `JOIN`s first, and the `WHERE` [[runs after the join|run-order]].

So Finn reaches the `WHERE` with `o.status` = NULL. `NULL IN ('paid', 'shipped')` is not true. It is [[unknown|unknown]], and `WHERE` keeps only rows where the condition is true. Finn is thrown away. Gus goes the same way. Eve's only order was refunded, and `'refunded' IN ('paid', 'shipped')` is false, so Eve goes too. The LEFT JOIN kept them; the WHERE undid it.

### The fix: put it in ON

Put conditions about the **right-hand** table (the one after `LEFT JOIN`) inside its `ON`, joined with `AND`:

```sql
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
```

Now the condition decides which orders *match*. It no longer removes customers. Eve's refunded order does not match, so Eve [[stays with NULLs|on-never-drops]], like Finn and Gus:

| name | id | status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dev | 6 | shipped |
| Eve | NULL | NULL |
| Finn | NULL | NULL |
| Gus | NULL | NULL |

`COUNT(o.id)` skips NULLs, so those three [[count as 0|count-column]].

The rule of thumb: in a LEFT JOIN, conditions about the right table go in `ON`; conditions about the left table go in `WHERE`. For example, `WHERE c.city = 'London'` is fine in `WHERE`: it picks which customers you want.

**Watch out:** the wrong version gives no error and plausible numbers. It is only missing people. Count the rows you expect before you trust a result: 7 customers should give 7 rows.

::: context left-table Which table is "left"
The left table is the one written first, before the words `LEFT JOIN`. In `FROM customers c LEFT JOIN orders o`, `customers` is on the left and every customer is kept; `orders` is on the right and only fills in where it matches. Swap them and the promise swaps too. Read `LEFT JOIN` as "keep everything I already have, and add what matches".
:::

::: context run-order Written order and running order
You write `SELECT` first, but the database works out the rows starting from `FROM`. It builds the joined rows, then filters them with `WHERE`, then groups them. The lesson "GROUP BY in depth" lists the whole order step by step. For this lesson, the key point is that `WHERE` only ever sees rows the joins have already built, NULL-filled ones included, and it can throw any of them away.
:::

::: context unknown True, false, and unknown
In SQL a comparison has three possible results: true, false, or unknown. Any comparison with NULL is unknown, because the database cannot say whether a missing value equals something. Even `NULL = NULL` is unknown. You can see it in SQLite: `SELECT NULL IN ('paid', 'shipped');` returns NULL, not 0 or 1. `WHERE` keeps a row only when its condition is true, so both false and unknown rows are dropped. The lesson "Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear" is a debugging lesson built entirely on this.
:::

::: context on-never-drops ON matches, WHERE removes
The two clauses do different jobs. `ON` answers "which right-hand rows go with this left-hand row?" If the answer is "none", a LEFT JOIN still keeps the left row, with NULLs. `WHERE` answers "which finished rows stay?", and it can remove anything.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="90" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">condition in WHERE</text>
  <text x="270" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">condition in ON</text>
  <line x1="180" y1="8" x2="180" y2="146" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <rect x="20" y="30" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="30" y="46" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Dev  6  shipped</text>
  <rect x="20" y="58" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="30" y="74" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">Eve  7  refunded</text>
  <line x1="24" y1="70" x2="156" y2="70" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <rect x="20" y="86" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="30" y="102" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">Finn  NULL  NULL</text>
  <line x1="24" y1="98" x2="156" y2="98" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <rect x="20" y="114" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="30" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">Gus  NULL  NULL</text>
  <line x1="24" y1="126" x2="156" y2="126" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <rect x="200" y="30" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="210" y="46" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Dev  6  shipped</text>
  <rect x="200" y="58" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="210" y="74" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Eve  NULL  NULL</text>
  <rect x="200" y="86" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="210" y="102" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Finn  NULL  NULL</text>
  <rect x="200" y="114" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="210" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Gus  NULL  NULL</text>
</svg>
```

Putting a right-table condition in `WHERE` quietly turns your LEFT JOIN back into an inner join. The lesson "Design: choosing the joins for a report" lists this among the checks for any report.
:::

::: context count-column Why COUNT(o.id) and not COUNT(*)
You saw this in the basics. `COUNT(*)` counts rows, and Finn still has one row, full of NULLs, so `COUNT(*)` would give Finn 1. `COUNT(o.id)` counts only the rows where `o.id` is not NULL, so Finn gets 0. After a LEFT JOIN, count a column from the right-hand table, ideally its `id`, which is never NULL in a real match.
:::
--- task
Return **every** customer's name and their number of orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, as a column named `live_orders` (0 for customers with none). Use `customers` and `orders`. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS live_orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS live_orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o
  ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Eve, Finn and Gus are missing. Which clause removed them?
--- hint
The status condition is about the right-hand table, `orders`. Move it out of `WHERE` and into the `ON`.
--- hint
Delete the `WHERE` line and make the join read `LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')`.
--- check result | All seven customers; Eve, Finn and Gus have 0
ordered
[["Ada", 3], ["Ben", 1], ["Cleo", 1], ["Dev", 1], ["Eve", 0], ["Finn", 0], ["Gus", 0]]

+++ practice | Shipped orders for everyone
--- task
Return **every** customer's `name` and their number of orders whose `status` is `'shipped'`, as a column named `shipped`. A customer with no shipped orders shows `0`. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS shipped
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o
  ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status = 'shipped'
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
You need every customer, matched or not, so join `orders` with a `LEFT JOIN` from `customers`.
--- hint
The status is about the right-hand table, so it goes in the `ON`, after `AND`. Count `o.id`, not `*`, so an empty match counts as 0.
--- check result | All seven customers; Cleo, Eve, Finn and Gus have 0
ordered

+++ practice | Products bought in bulk
--- task
Return **every** product's `name` and the number of its order lines (rows of `order_items`) with a `qty` of 2 or more, as a column named `bulk_lines`. A product with no such line shows `0`. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT p.name
FROM products p
ORDER BY p.name;
--- solution
SELECT p.name, COUNT(oi.product_id) AS bulk_lines
FROM products p
LEFT JOIN order_items oi
  ON oi.product_id = p.id AND oi.qty >= 2
GROUP BY p.id, p.name
ORDER BY p.name;
--- hint
Start from `products` so every product stays, and `LEFT JOIN order_items` onto it.
--- hint
The rule "qty of 2 or more" is about the right-hand table. Put it in the `ON` with `AND oi.qty >= 2`, and count a column of `order_items`.
--- check result | Notebook 2, USB cable 2, Mug 1, the other five 0

+++ practice | March orders in two cities
--- task
Return every customer whose `city` is `'London'` or `'Paris'`, with two columns:

- `name`
- `march_orders`: how many orders they placed in March 2024 (a `placed` value starting `'2024-03'`), any status, or `0` if none

Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS march_orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.placed LIKE '2024-03%'
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS march_orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o
  ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.placed LIKE '2024-03%'
WHERE c.city IN ('London', 'Paris')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Two filters here, one per table. Which one picks the customers you want, and which one decides which orders match?
--- hint
The March rule is about `orders`, the right-hand table: move it into the `ON`. The city rule is about `customers`, the left table: it belongs in `WHERE`.

+++ practice | Hot readings at every station
--- schema
CREATE TABLE stations (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO stations (id, name) VALUES
  (1, 'Atacama'), (2, 'Kiruna'), (3, 'Svalbard'), (4, 'Hobart'), (5, 'Mauna Kea');
CREATE TABLE readings (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  station_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES stations(id),
  temp REAL
);
INSERT INTO readings (id, station_id, temp) VALUES
  (1, 1, 31.5), (2, 1, 29.0), (3, 1, 35.2), (4, 1, NULL),
  (5, 2, 12.0), (6, 2, NULL),
  (7, 4, 30.0), (8, 4, 30.5),
  (9, 5, NULL), (10, 5, NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: `stations` (`id`, `name`) and `readings` (`id`, `station_id`, `temp`). A `temp` can be NULL when the sensor failed.

Return **every** station, with three columns:

- `name`
- `hot`: how many of its readings are **above** 30 (a reading of exactly 30 is not hot)
- `hottest`: the highest of those hot readings, or NULL if it has none

Svalbard has no readings at all, and Mauna Kea has only NULL ones. Both must still appear, with `0` and NULL. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT s.name
FROM stations s
ORDER BY s.name;
--- solution
SELECT s.name, COUNT(r.id) AS hot, MAX(r.temp) AS hottest
FROM stations s
LEFT JOIN readings r
  ON r.station_id = s.id AND r.temp > 30
GROUP BY s.id, s.name
ORDER BY s.name;
--- hint
Keep every station with a `LEFT JOIN readings r` from `stations`. "Above 30" is `> 30`, and it is a rule about the right-hand table.
--- hint
Put `r.temp > 30` in the `ON`. A NULL temp is not above 30, so it never matches either.
--- hint
Count `r.id`, which is NULL when nothing matched; `COUNT(*)` would give 1. `MAX(r.temp)` is already NULL for a station with no match.
--- check result | Atacama 2 (35.2), Hobart 1 (30.5, not the 30.0), the other three 0 and NULL
ordered
[["Atacama", 2, 35.2], ["Hobart", 1, 30.5], ["Kiruna", 0, null], ["Mauna Kea", 0, null], ["Svalbard", 0, null]]

+++ practice | The products that vanished from the paid report
--- task
This report should show **every** product with the number of its order lines that belong to a `'paid'` order, as `paid_lines`, sorted by name. It shows only three products. The other five have no paid lines, and they should appear with `0`.

The two `LEFT JOIN`s are right. Find the line that undoes them and fix it.
--- starter
SELECT p.name, COUNT(o.id) AS paid_lines
FROM products p
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.product_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN orders o       ON o.id = oi.order_id
WHERE o.status = 'paid'
GROUP BY p.id, p.name
ORDER BY p.name;
--- solution
SELECT p.name, COUNT(o.id) AS paid_lines
FROM products p
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.product_id = p.id
LEFT JOIN orders o       ON o.id = oi.order_id AND o.status = 'paid'
GROUP BY p.id, p.name
ORDER BY p.name;
--- hint
Which clause runs after the joins and throws away every row where `o.status` is NULL?
--- hint
The status is about `orders`, so the condition belongs in that join's `ON`. Then an order that is not paid simply does not match, and `COUNT(o.id)` skips it.
--- check result | All eight products: Notebook 2, Fountain pen 1, USB cable 1, the rest 0
ordered
[["Bookends", 0], ["Desk lamp", 0], ["Fountain pen", 1], ["Headphones", 0], ["Mug", 0], ["Notebook", 2], ["Planner", 0], ["USB cable", 1]]

+++ practice | Critical alerts this month
--- schema
CREATE TABLE satellites (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  active INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO satellites (id, name, active) VALUES
  (1, 'Aurora', 1), (2, 'Borealis', 1), (3, 'Cygnus', 0), (4, 'Draco', 1);
CREATE TABLE alerts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sat_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES satellites(id),
  level TEXT NOT NULL,
  at TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO alerts (id, sat_id, level, at) VALUES
  (1, 1, 'critical', '2024-06-02 04:10:00'),
  (2, 1, 'warning',  '2024-06-03 11:00:00'),
  (3, 1, 'critical', '2024-06-05 09:12:00'),
  (4, 2, 'warning',  '2024-06-04 18:30:00'),
  (5, 3, 'critical', '2024-06-06 07:45:00'),
  (6, 4, 'critical', '2024-05-20 22:05:00');
--- task
This problem has its own database. `satellites` has `id`, `name` and `active` (1 for a satellite still flying, 0 for one switched off). `alerts` has `id`, `sat_id`, `level` (`'critical'` or `'warning'`) and `at`, a timestamp like `'2024-06-02 04:10:00'`.

Mission control wants a board of **every active** satellite, with three columns:

- `name`
- `critical`: how many `'critical'` alerts it raised on or after `'2024-06-01'`, or `0`
- `last_critical`: the `at` of the latest of those alerts, or NULL if none

Leave out satellites that are switched off. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT s.name, COUNT(a.id) AS critical, MAX(a.at) AS last_critical
FROM satellites s
LEFT JOIN alerts a ON a.sat_id = s.id
GROUP BY s.id, s.name
ORDER BY s.name;
--- solution
SELECT s.name, COUNT(a.id) AS critical, MAX(a.at) AS last_critical
FROM satellites s
LEFT JOIN alerts a
  ON a.sat_id = s.id
 AND a.level = 'critical'
 AND a.at >= '2024-06-01'
WHERE s.active = 1
GROUP BY s.id, s.name
ORDER BY s.name;
--- hint
Sort the three conditions by table. Which are about `alerts`, the right-hand table, and which is about `satellites`?
--- hint
The level and the date decide which alerts match, so both go in the `ON`. "Active" decides which satellites are on the board, so it goes in `WHERE`.
--- hint
The join becomes `LEFT JOIN alerts a ON a.sat_id = s.id AND a.level = 'critical' AND a.at >= '2024-06-01'`, followed by `WHERE s.active = 1`.
--- check result | Aurora 2 (last on 5 June), Borealis 0, Draco 0 (its critical alert is from May); Cygnus is off
ordered
[["Aurora", 2, "2024-06-05 09:12:00"], ["Borealis", 0, null], ["Draco", 0, null]]
--- check source | Uses LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not type a satellite's name
'(Aurora|Borealis|Cygnus|Draco)'

=== sql2-03 | Self-joins
--- teach
Last lesson you kept every customer with a LEFT JOIN, even when no order matched. So far every join has linked two *different* tables. This lesson shows how to join a table to itself, for when a table [[points at itself|referral-tree]].

### The picture

Picture a class list where each student has a "buddy" column holding another student's number. To find out who Sam's buddy is by name, you read Sam's buddy number, then run your finger back up the *same* list to find that number. One list, used twice: once to read the student, once to look up the buddy.

### A table that points at itself

In `customers`, `referred_by` holds the id of the customer who brought this one in: someone who recommended the shop to them. That is a **[[referral|referral]]**.

| id | name | referred_by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Ada | NULL |
| 2 | Ben | 1 |
| 3 | Cleo | 1 |
| 4 | Dev | 2 |
| 5 | Eve | NULL |
| 6 | Finn | 4 |
| 7 | Gus | NULL |

Ben's `referred_by` is 1, so Ada referred Ben. To show the referrer's *name* instead of a number, join `customers` to itself. This is a **self-join**: a table joined to itself.

### Two aliases, two parts

Give the table two different aliases, so SQL knows [[which copy you mean|two-aliases]]:

```sql
SELECT c.name, r.name AS referrer
FROM customers c
JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by;
```

`c` is "the customer", `r` is "the referrer": the same table, playing two parts. Read the `ON` aloud: "the referrer's `id` equals this customer's `referred_by`". The result:

| name | referrer |
| --- | --- |
| Ben | Ada |
| Cleo | Ada |
| Dev | Ben |
| Finn | Dev |

Employees and their managers, categories and their parent categories, replies and the message they answer: these are all self-joins.

### Who goes missing

As with any inner join, rows with no match are dropped. Ada, Eve and Gus have `referred_by` = NULL, and [[NULL matches nothing|null-no-match]], so they disappear. Whether that is right depends on the question:

- "Who referred whom?" The inner join is fine.
- "List every customer with their referrer." Use `LEFT JOIN`. The customers nobody referred come back, with `referrer` = NULL.

**Watch out:** the direction of the `ON` matters. `ON c.id = r.referred_by` also runs, but it answers a different question: "whom did each customer refer?" It gives Ada twice (for Ben and for Cleo) and never mentions Eve. Say the `ON` in words before you run it.

::: context referral-tree The referral chain as a picture
Each arrow goes from the referrer to the customer they brought in. Ada started a chain three links long; Eve and Gus found the shop on their own.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="head" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <rect x="10" y="44" width="56" height="28" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="38" y="63" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Ada</text>
  <rect x="100" y="14" width="56" height="28" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="128" y="33" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Ben</text>
  <rect x="100" y="74" width="56" height="28" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="128" y="93" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Cleo</text>
  <rect x="190" y="14" width="56" height="28" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="218" y="33" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Dev</text>
  <rect x="280" y="14" width="56" height="28" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="308" y="33" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Finn</text>
  <line x1="66" y1="52" x2="99" y2="32" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#head)"/>
  <line x1="66" y1="66" x2="99" y2="86" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#head)"/>
  <line x1="156" y1="28" x2="189" y2="28" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#head)"/>
  <line x1="246" y1="28" x2="279" y2="28" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#head)"/>
  <rect x="190" y="104" width="56" height="28" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="218" y="123" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">Eve</text>
  <rect x="280" y="104" width="56" height="28" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="308" y="123" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">Gus</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context referral How apps use referrals
Many apps reward you for inviting a friend: you both get credit when they sign up. To pay out that reward, the app stores who invited whom, usually exactly like this: a column on the users table holding another user's id. The same shape answers questions such as "which customers brought in the most new customers?" by joining the table to itself and grouping by the referrer.
:::

::: context two-aliases Why the aliases are required
With two copies of the same table, every column name exists twice. In SQLite, `SELECT name FROM customers JOIN customers ON customers.id = customers.referred_by;` stops with `ambiguous column name: name`, because both copies are called `customers`. The aliases give each copy its own name. Pick aliases that say the part each copy plays, like `c` for customer and `r` for referrer, or `e` for employee and `m` for manager. Then the `ON` reads almost like a sentence.
:::

::: context null-no-match Why NULL never matches
An inner join keeps a pair of rows only when its `ON` is true. For Ada, the `ON` asks whether `r.id = NULL`. As you saw last lesson, any comparison with NULL is unknown, never true, so no row of the second copy matches Ada's row. A LEFT JOIN keeps that row anyway and fills the referrer's columns with NULL. The same holds for any foreign key that can be empty: an employee with no manager, a top-level category with no parent.
:::
--- task
Return **every** customer's name and the name of the customer who referred them, as a column named `referrer` (NULL when nobody did). Use `customers` twice, linking `referred_by` to `id`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, r.name AS referrer
FROM customers c
JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, r.name AS referrer
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by;
--- hint
Run the starter: Ada, Eve and Gus are missing. Look at their `referred_by`. Why can no row match it?
--- hint
You need a join that keeps every customer, even without a match. You met it last lesson.
--- hint
Change `JOIN customers r` to `LEFT JOIN customers r`. Keep the same `ON`.
--- check result | Seven customers, NULL for the three nobody referred
[["Ada", null], ["Ben", "Ada"], ["Cleo", "Ada"], ["Dev", "Ben"], ["Eve", null], ["Finn", "Dev"], ["Gus", null]]

+++ practice | Where the referrer lives
--- task
For every customer who **was** referred by another customer, return three columns:

- `name`: the customer's name
- `city`: the customer's city
- `referrer_city`: the city of the customer who referred them

Customers nobody referred are left out. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, c.city, c.referred_by
FROM customers c
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, c.city, r.city AS referrer_city
FROM customers c
JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Use `customers` twice, with a different alias for each part: `c` for the customer and `r` for the referrer.
--- hint
The link is "the referrer's `id` equals this customer's `referred_by`": `JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by`. Then select `r.city`.
--- check result | Ben, Cleo, Dev and Finn; Cleo and Finn have no city of their own
ordered
[["Ben", "Paris", "London"], ["Cleo", null, "London"], ["Dev", "London", "Paris"], ["Finn", null, "London"]]
--- check source | Uses the customers table twice
[Cc][Uu][Ss][Tt][Oo][Mm][Ee][Rr][Ss]\s+\w+[\s\S]*[Cc][Uu][Ss][Tt][Oo][Mm][Ee][Rr][Ss]\s+\w+
--- check source absent | Does not type a city
'(London|Paris|Berlin)'

+++ practice | Who brought in the most customers
--- task
For every customer who referred at least one other customer, return two columns:

- `referrer`: the referrer's name
- `referrals`: how many customers they referred

Sort by `referrals`, highest first, then by `referrer`.
--- starter
SELECT referred_by, COUNT(*) AS referrals
FROM customers
GROUP BY referred_by;
--- solution
SELECT r.name AS referrer, COUNT(*) AS referrals
FROM customers c
JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
GROUP BY r.id, r.name
ORDER BY referrals DESC, r.name;
--- hint
Join every referred customer `c` to their referrer `r`, as in the lesson. Now group the rows by the referrer.
--- hint
Group by `r.id, r.name` and count the rows. The inner join already drops customers nobody referred, so there is no NULL group.
--- check result | Ada 2, then Ben 1 and Dev 1
ordered
[["Ada", 2], ["Ben", 1], ["Dev", 1]]
--- check source | Groups the rows
[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy]
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Dev)'

+++ practice | Orders from referred customers
--- task
For every order placed by a customer who was referred, return three columns:

- `order_id`
- `customer`: the name of the customer who placed it
- `referrer`: the name of the customer who referred them

Orders from customers nobody referred are left out. Sort by `order_id`.
--- starter
SELECT o.id AS order_id, c.name AS customer
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
ORDER BY o.id;
--- solution
SELECT o.id AS order_id, c.name AS customer, r.name AS referrer
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
ORDER BY o.id;
--- hint
The starter already finds each order's customer. Add one more join: the customer's referrer, from `customers` again with a new alias.
--- hint
The new join is `JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by`. An inner join leaves out customers nobody referred, which is what the task asks.
--- check result | Orders 3, 4, 5 and 6; Ada referred Ben and Cleo, Ben referred Dev
ordered
[[3, "Ben", "Ada"], [4, "Ben", "Ada"], [5, "Cleo", "Ada"], [6, "Dev", "Ben"]]
--- check source | Joins customers a second time
[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]\s+[Cc][Uu][Ss][Tt][Oo][Mm][Ee][Rr][Ss][\s\S]*[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]\s+[Cc][Uu][Ss][Tt][Oo][Mm][Ee][Rr][Ss]
--- check source absent | Does not type any name

+++ practice | Every employee and their manager
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  manager_id INTEGER,
  salary INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name, manager_id, salary) VALUES
  (1, 'Mara', NULL, 150000),
  (2, 'Theo', 1,    110000),
  (3, 'Iris', 1,    115000),
  (4, 'Sam',  2,     90000),
  (5, 'Sam',  3,    120000),
  (6, 'Juno', 9,     70000),
  (7, 'Pia',  2,     95000);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `staff`, with `id`, `name`, `manager_id` (the `id` of their manager) and `salary`.

Return **every** employee, with four columns:

- `id` and `name`
- `manager`: their manager's name
- `gap`: the manager's salary minus the employee's salary

Watch the awkward rows. Mara has no manager (`manager_id` is NULL). Juno's manager, id 9, has left the company and is no longer in the table. Both must still appear, with NULL in `manager` and `gap`. Two different people are called Sam, and one of them earns more than their manager, so their `gap` is negative. Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT e.id, e.name, m.name AS manager
FROM staff e
JOIN staff m ON m.id = e.manager_id
ORDER BY e.id;
--- solution
SELECT e.id, e.name, m.name AS manager, m.salary - e.salary AS gap
FROM staff e
LEFT JOIN staff m ON m.id = e.manager_id
ORDER BY e.id;
--- hint
Run the starter: Mara and Juno are missing. Neither of their `manager_id`s finds a row, so an inner join drops them.
--- hint
Use `LEFT JOIN staff m`. Then `m.salary - e.salary` is NULL on its own for the two rows with no manager, because arithmetic with NULL gives NULL.
--- check result | Seven rows; Mara and Juno have NULLs, the second Sam has a gap of -5000
ordered
[[1, "Mara", null, null], [2, "Theo", "Mara", 40000], [3, "Iris", "Mara", 35000], [4, "Sam", "Theo", 20000], [5, "Sam", "Iris", -5000], [6, "Juno", null, null], [7, "Pia", "Theo", 15000]]
--- check source | Uses LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not type a salary
\d{5}

+++ practice | Paid more than the boss
--- schema
CREATE TABLE staff (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  manager_id INTEGER,
  salary INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO staff (id, name, manager_id, salary) VALUES
  (1, 'Mara', NULL, 150000),
  (2, 'Theo', 1,    110000),
  (3, 'Iris', 1,    115000),
  (4, 'Sam',  2,     90000),
  (5, 'Omar', 3,    120000),
  (6, 'Juno', 3,     70000),
  (7, 'Pia',  2,    112000);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `staff`, with `id`, `name`, `manager_id` (the `id` of their manager) and `salary`.

This query should find the employees who earn more than their own manager, with four columns: `name`, `salary`, `manager` and `manager_salary`, sorted by name. It returns managers instead, and none of them earns more than their boss. The right answer is Omar and Pia.

The bug is in one line. Say each `ON` aloud to find it.
--- starter
SELECT e.name, e.salary, m.name AS manager, m.salary AS manager_salary
FROM staff e
JOIN staff m ON e.id = m.manager_id
WHERE e.salary > m.salary
ORDER BY e.name;
--- solution
SELECT e.name, e.salary, m.name AS manager, m.salary AS manager_salary
FROM staff e
JOIN staff m ON m.id = e.manager_id
WHERE e.salary > m.salary
ORDER BY e.name;
--- hint
`e` should be the employee and `m` their manager. Read the `ON` in words: whose `id` should equal whose `manager_id`?
--- hint
The manager's `id` equals the employee's `manager_id`: `ON m.id = e.manager_id`. The starter has the two sides the other way round.
--- check result | Omar (120000, under Iris at 115000) and Pia (112000, under Theo at 110000)
ordered
[["Omar", 120000, "Iris", 115000], ["Pia", 112000, "Theo", 110000]]
--- check source | Links the manager's id to the employee's manager_id
m\.id\s*=\s*e\.manager_id|e\.manager_id\s*=\s*m\.id
--- check source absent | Does not type a name
'(Omar|Pia|Iris|Theo)'

+++ practice | Crew who share a base
--- schema
CREATE TABLE people (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  base TEXT
);
INSERT INTO people (id, name, base) VALUES
  (1, 'Ana', 'Houston'), (2, 'Ben', 'Houston'), (3, 'Cai', 'Houston'),
  (4, 'Dee', 'Cologne'), (5, 'Eli', 'Cologne'),
  (6, 'Fay', NULL), (7, 'Gil', NULL),
  (8, 'Hal', 'Tsukuba');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `people`, with `id`, `name` and `base` (the city they work from, or NULL if not assigned yet).

List every **pair** of people who work from the same base, with three columns:

- `first`: the name of the person with the smaller `id`
- `second`: the name of the person with the larger `id`
- `base`

Each pair appears once: Ana with Ben, never also Ben with Ana, and nobody is paired with themselves. People with no base are not paired, even with each other. Sort by `base`, then `first`, then `second`.
--- starter
SELECT a.name AS first, b.name AS second, a.base
FROM people a
JOIN people b ON b.base = a.base
ORDER BY a.base, a.name, b.name;
--- solution
SELECT a.name AS first, b.name AS second, a.base
FROM people a
JOIN people b ON b.base = a.base AND a.id < b.id
ORDER BY a.base, first, second;
--- hint
Run the starter: every person is paired with themselves, and every pair shows up twice, once in each order.
--- hint
Both problems go away with one extra condition in the `ON`: keep a pair only when the first person's `id` is smaller than the second's.
--- hint
Add `AND a.id < b.id` to the `ON`. The NULL bases need nothing extra: `NULL = NULL` is not true, so they never match.
--- check result | Three Houston pairs and one Cologne pair; Fay, Gil and Hal have no partner
ordered
[["Dee", "Eli", "Cologne"], ["Ana", "Ben", "Houston"], ["Ana", "Cai", "Houston"], ["Ben", "Cai", "Houston"]]
--- check source | Compares the two ids to keep each pair once
\w+\.id\s*[<>]\s*\w+\.id
--- check source absent | Does not type a name
'(Ana|Ben|Cai|Dee|Eli)'

=== sql2-04 | GROUP BY in depth
--- teach
Last lesson you joined a table to itself. In the basics you met `GROUP BY` and `HAVING`. This lesson opens them up: you will see the order the database runs a grouped query in, which tells you where each filter goes, and how to count correctly after a join. The next lesson adds a few more grouping tools.

### The picture

Picture a pile of paper orders on a desk, and a summary to write. First you throw out the canceled ones. Then you sort the rest into piles, one per customer. Then you count each pile. Then you put away the piles that are too small. Only then do you write the summary sheet, and put its lines in order.

### The order the database works in

A grouped query runs [[in the same order|run-order]]:

1. `FROM` and the `JOIN`s build one wide set of rows.
2. `WHERE` drops rows.
3. `GROUP BY` sorts the rows that are left into buckets, one bucket per value.
4. The **aggregates** run once per bucket. An aggregate is a function that turns many rows into one value: `COUNT`, `SUM`, `AVG`, `MIN`, `MAX`.
5. `HAVING` drops buckets.
6. `SELECT` shapes the output, then `ORDER BY` sorts it.

Here it is on `orders` alone. `<>` means "not equal".

```sql
SELECT customer_id, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
WHERE status <> 'canceled'
GROUP BY customer_id
HAVING COUNT(*) >= 2;
```

- `WHERE` drops order 3, Ben's canceled one. Seven orders are left.
- `GROUP BY` makes five buckets: customer 1 has orders 1, 2 and 8; customers 2, 3, 4 and 5 have one order each.
- `COUNT(*)` counts each bucket: 3, 1, 1, 1, 1.
- `HAVING` keeps buckets with at least 2.

| customer_id | orders |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | 3 |

### Where each filter goes

That order explains the rules. `WHERE` cannot use `SUM(...)` or `COUNT(...)`, because at step 2 the sums [[do not exist yet|misuse-aggregate]]. `HAVING` runs at step 5, after they exist, so it can.

A condition that does not need an aggregate, such as `category = 'home'`, could go in either place. Put it in `WHERE`, where it [[throws rows away early|filter-early]].

### Counting different values

`COUNT(DISTINCT x)` counts the *different* values of `x`. `DISTINCT` means "each value once".

This matters after a join. Once `order_items` is joined in, there is one row per order *item*, so `COUNT(*)` counts items. That is rarely the number you want to call "orders" or "customers". Here are the buyers per category, over every order:

```sql
SELECT p.category, COUNT(*) AS item_rows, COUNT(DISTINCT o.customer_id) AS buyers
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.category;
```

| category | item_rows | buyers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| electronics | 4 | 3 |
| home | 4 | 4 |
| stationery | 5 | 3 |

Ada bought two different electronics items, so Ada makes [[two rows but one buyer|count-distinct]].

**Watch out:** after a join, `COUNT(*)` counts joined rows. It gives no error; the number is only too big. Ask "one row of what?" and use `COUNT(DISTINCT ...)` on the id you really mean.

::: context run-order Why it runs in that order
You write `SELECT` first because it reads well, not because it happens first. The order in this lesson is the *logical* order: the answer always comes out as if the steps ran like that. Inside, a real database may do things in a cleverer order to go faster, but it never changes the answer. When a query surprises you, walking these six steps with pencil and paper nearly always finds the reason.
:::

::: context misuse-aggregate The error you get
Try `SELECT customer_id FROM orders WHERE COUNT(*) > 1 GROUP BY customer_id;` in SQLite. It stops with `misuse of aggregate: COUNT()`. The database is telling you it cannot count at the `WHERE` step, because the buckets have not been made yet. The fix is to move the condition to `HAVING`, which goes after `GROUP BY`.
:::

::: context filter-early Why WHERE is the better place
Every row that `WHERE` drops is a row that never has to be sorted into a bucket. On this shop's 13 items that makes no difference. On a table of flight telemetry, with millions of readings, it does. A condition in `WHERE` can also let the database use shortcuts (indexes, which you meet in the advanced course) to skip unwanted rows without reading them at all. `HAVING` only sees the buckets after all that work is done.
:::

::: context count-distinct Rows, and different values
The four electronics rows come from three people. `COUNT(*)` counts every row; `COUNT(DISTINCT o.customer_id)` counts each customer once.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="80" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">electronics rows</text>
  <rect x="10" y="28" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="20" y="44" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Ada · Headphones</text>
  <rect x="10" y="56" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="20" y="72" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Ben · USB cable</text>
  <rect x="10" y="84" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="20" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Dev · Headphones</text>
  <rect x="10" y="112" width="140" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="20" y="128" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">Ada · USB cable</text>
  <text x="80" y="154" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">COUNT(*) = 4</text>
  <line x1="152" y1="40" x2="218" y2="54" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="152" y1="124" x2="218" y2="58" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="152" y1="68" x2="218" y2="86" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="152" y1="96" x2="218" y2="114" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="280" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">different buyers</text>
  <rect x="220" y="44" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="280" y="60" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Ada</text>
  <rect x="220" y="74" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="280" y="90" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Ben</text>
  <rect x="220" y="104" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="280" y="120" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Dev</text>
  <text x="280" y="154" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">COUNT(DISTINCT) = 3</text>
</svg>
```

Rows multiplying under a join is called fan-out. The lesson "Dates and times" counts orders with `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)` for this reason, and the lesson "Debugging: the join that double-counts" hunts down a report that fan-out made wrong.
:::
--- task
Using only orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, return one row per product `category` with four columns:

- `category`
- `buyers`: the number of **different customers** who bought from it
- `units`: the total of `qty`
- `revenue`: the sum of `qty * unit_price`, rounded to 2 decimals with `ROUND(..., 2)`

You will need `order_items`, `orders` and `products`. Keep only categories with revenue of at least 60, and sort by revenue, highest first.
--- starter
SELECT p.category, COUNT(*) AS buyers, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM order_items oi
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.category;
--- solution
SELECT p.category,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.customer_id) AS buyers,
       SUM(oi.qty) AS units,
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS revenue
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY p.category
HAVING SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) >= 60
ORDER BY revenue DESC;
--- hint
You need `orders` in the join too: for the status filter, and for `customer_id`. Add `JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id`.
--- hint
Buyers is `COUNT(DISTINCT o.customer_id)`. The status filter works on rows, so it goes in `WHERE`. The revenue threshold needs a `SUM`, so it goes in `HAVING`.
--- hint
Add `ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS revenue` to the `SELECT`, then `HAVING SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) >= 60` after `GROUP BY`, and finish with `ORDER BY revenue DESC`.
--- check result | electronics then home; stationery (56.80) is left out
ordered
[["electronics", 3, 7, 201.6], ["home", 3, 4, 72.0]]

+++ practice | Products people buy in bulk
--- task
Over every order, whatever its status, return one row per product with three columns:

- `name`: the product's name
- `buyers`: how many **different** customers bought it
- `units`: the total `qty` sold

Keep only products with at least 3 units sold. Sort by `units`, highest first.
--- starter
SELECT p.name, COUNT(*) AS buyers, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM order_items oi
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.id, p.name;
--- solution
SELECT p.name,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.customer_id) AS buyers,
       SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.id, p.name
HAVING SUM(oi.qty) >= 3
ORDER BY units DESC;
--- hint
The customer is on the order, so join `orders` too. Then count each customer once with `COUNT(DISTINCT o.customer_id)`.
--- hint
"At least 3 units" needs the `SUM`, which only exists after grouping. Put it in `HAVING`, not `WHERE`.
--- check result | Notebook 6 units from 2 buyers, USB cable 5, Mug 3
ordered
[["Notebook", 2, 6], ["USB cable", 2, 5], ["Mug", 2, 3]]
--- check source | Counts different customers
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source | Filters the groups with HAVING
[Hh][Aa][Vv][Ii][Nn][Gg]

+++ practice | Orders paid in more than one go
--- task
Join `payments` to `orders` and return one row per order `status`, with three columns:

- `status`
- `payments`: how many payments were made on orders with that status
- `orders`: how many **different** orders those payments were for

Keep only the statuses where there are more payments than orders, which means some order was paid in more than one go. Sort by `status`.
--- starter
SELECT o.status, COUNT(*) AS payments
FROM payments p
JOIN orders o ON o.id = p.order_id
GROUP BY o.status;
--- solution
SELECT o.status,
       COUNT(*) AS payments,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders
FROM payments p
JOIN orders o ON o.id = p.order_id
GROUP BY o.status
HAVING COUNT(*) > COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)
ORDER BY o.status;
--- hint
After the join there is one row per payment. `COUNT(*)` counts payments; `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)` counts each order once.
--- hint
The condition compares two aggregates, so it goes in `HAVING`: `HAVING COUNT(*) > COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)`.
--- check result | refunded (2 payments, 1 order) and shipped (5 payments, 4 orders); paid is left out
ordered
[["refunded", 2, 1], ["shipped", 5, 4]]
--- check source | Counts different orders
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source absent | Does not type a status
'(refunded|shipped)'

+++ practice | Customers who shop across categories
--- task
Using only orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, find the customers who bought from **at least 2 different** product categories. Return two columns:

- `name`: the customer's name
- `categories`: how many different categories they bought from

Sort by `categories`, highest first, then by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COUNT(*) AS categories
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COUNT(DISTINCT p.category) AS categories
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT p.category) >= 2
ORDER BY categories DESC, c.name;
--- hint
Walk from customers to their orders, to the order items, to the products. The status rule works on rows, so it goes in `WHERE`.
--- hint
Count categories with `COUNT(DISTINCT p.category)`. The "at least 2" rule needs that count, so it goes in `HAVING`.
--- check result | Ada 3, Ben 2, Dev 2; Cleo only bought stationery
ordered
[["Ada", 3], ["Ben", 2], ["Dev", 2]]
--- check source | Counts different categories
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]\s+p\.category|[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]\s+category
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Dev|Cleo)'

+++ practice | Sensors per spacecraft
--- schema
CREATE TABLE readings (
  craft TEXT NOT NULL,
  sensor TEXT,
  value REAL
);
INSERT INTO readings (craft, sensor, value) VALUES
  ('Hermes', 'temp', 21.0), ('Hermes', 'temp', 22.5), ('Hermes', 'volt', NULL), ('Hermes', NULL, 3.3),
  ('Iris',   'temp', 19.0), ('Iris',   'temp', 19.5), ('Iris',   'temp', NULL),
  ('Juno',   'gyro', 0.1),  ('Juno',   'volt', 28.0), ('Juno',   'temp', NULL), ('Juno', 'volt', 28.2),
  ('Kepler', NULL,   1.0),  ('Kepler', NULL,   2.0);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `readings`, with `craft`, `sensor` and `value`. A `sensor` is NULL when the reading came in with no label, and a `value` is NULL when the sensor sent nothing usable.

Return one row per craft with four columns:

- `craft`
- `readings`: how many rows it has
- `sensors`: how many **different** named sensors it has (a NULL sensor is not a sensor)
- `valid`: how many of its readings have a `value`

Keep only crafts with at least 2 different named sensors. Iris has three readings but only one sensor, so it is left out. Sort by `craft`.
--- starter
SELECT craft, COUNT(*) AS readings, COUNT(*) AS sensors, COUNT(*) AS valid
FROM readings
GROUP BY craft
HAVING COUNT(*) >= 2
ORDER BY craft;
--- solution
SELECT craft,
       COUNT(*) AS readings,
       COUNT(DISTINCT sensor) AS sensors,
       COUNT(value) AS valid
FROM readings
GROUP BY craft
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT sensor) >= 2
ORDER BY craft;
--- hint
Three different counts: `COUNT(*)` counts rows, `COUNT(value)` skips NULL values, and `COUNT(DISTINCT sensor)` counts each named sensor once.
--- hint
`COUNT(DISTINCT ...)` skips NULL too, so Kepler has 0 sensors. Use the same count in `HAVING`.
--- check result | Hermes (4 rows, 2 sensors, 3 valid) and Juno (4 rows, 3 sensors, 3 valid)
ordered
[["Hermes", 4, 2, 3], ["Juno", 4, 3, 3]]
--- check source | Counts different sensors
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]\s+sensor
--- check source absent | Does not type a craft's name
'(Hermes|Iris|Juno|Kepler)'

+++ practice | Too many orders
--- task
This report should show, for each customer who has placed an order (any status), their `name`, their number of `orders`, and the total `units` (the sum of `qty`), sorted by name. The units are right, but the order counts are too big: it says Ada placed 5 orders, and Ada placed 3.

Fix the count.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COUNT(*) AS orders, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Ask "one row of what?". After the join with `order_items`, each row is one item, not one order.
--- hint
Count each order once: `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)`.
--- check result | Ada 3 orders and 9 units, Ben 2 and 4, Cleo 1 and 3, Dev 1 and 3, Eve 1 and 1
ordered
[["Ada", 3, 9], ["Ben", 2, 4], ["Cleo", 1, 3], ["Dev", 1, 3], ["Eve", 1, 1]]
--- check source | Counts different orders
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]\s+o\.id
--- check source | Still adds up the units
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(\s*oi\.qty\s*\)

  (19, 'Delta',    'test',   'Cato', '2024-05-01 12:00:00');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `events`, a mission log with `id`, `mission`, `kind`, `crew` (who logged it, or NULL for an automatic entry) and `at`.

Events of kind `'test'` are rehearsals and do not count at all. Using only the other events, return one row per mission with four columns:

- `mission`
- `events`: how many events it has, automatic ones included
- `crew_members`: how many **different** crew members logged them
- `first_at`: the earliest `at`

Keep only missions where at least 3 different crew members logged something. Sort by `crew_members`, highest first, then by `mission`.
--- starter
SELECT mission, COUNT(*) AS events
FROM events
GROUP BY mission;
--- solution
SELECT mission,
       COUNT(*) AS events,
       COUNT(DISTINCT crew) AS crew_members,
       MIN(at) AS first_at
FROM events
WHERE kind <> 'test'
GROUP BY mission
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT crew) >= 3
ORDER BY crew_members DESC, mission;
--- hint
Throw the rehearsals away before grouping: the `'test'` rule is about single rows, so it goes in `WHERE`. If it only went in `HAVING`, their crew would still be counted.
--- hint
Count people with `COUNT(DISTINCT crew)`, which skips the automatic NULL entries. Keep missions with `HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT crew) >= 3`.
--- hint
Delta has only rehearsals, so after the `WHERE` it has no rows at all and cannot appear.
--- check result | Corvid (6 events, 4 crew) then Apollo-X (4 events, 3 crew); Borei and Delta are left out

=== sql2-04b | GROUP BY: expressions, NULLs and extra columns
--- teach
Last lesson you grouped by a column, `category`, and learned where `WHERE` and `HAVING` go. This lesson adds three more things about grouping: grouping by a value you work out, where NULLs land, and which extra columns you may put in the `SELECT`.

### The picture

Picture sorting a year of receipts into monthly envelopes. You do not care about the exact day or time on each receipt. You only read the month from the date, and that decides the envelope.

### Grouping by an expression

An **expression** is anything that works out a value: a column, a sum like `qty * unit_price`, or a function call. You can group by an expression, not only by a column.

`substr(placed, 1, 10)` takes a piece of the text in `placed`: starting at character 1, 10 characters long. `substr` is short for "substring", a piece of a string (text). From `'2024-04-07 20:10:00'` it gives `'2024-04-07'`, the day. The lesson "Working with text" covers text functions properly.

```sql
SELECT substr(placed, 1, 10) AS day, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY substr(placed, 1, 10);
```

Every order placed on 7 April lands in one bucket, whatever its time:

| day | orders |
| --- | --- |
| 2024-03-02 | 1 |
| 2024-03-20 | 1 |
| 2024-03-21 | 1 |
| 2024-04-07 | 2 |
| 2024-05-15 | 1 |
| 2024-05-28 | 1 |
| 2024-06-01 | 1 |

Notice that you [[repeat the expression|group-alias]] in `GROUP BY`. Take the first 7 characters instead and you get the month, like `'2024-03'`.

### NULLs get one group of their own

Some customers have no city. When you group by `city`, [[all the NULLs fall into one group|null-group]]:

```sql
SELECT city, COUNT(*) AS customers
FROM customers
GROUP BY city;
```

| city | customers |
| --- | --- |
| NULL | 2 |
| Berlin | 1 |
| London | 2 |
| Paris | 2 |

The NULL group is Cleo and Finn. If a report should not show it, filter it out with `WHERE city IS NOT NULL`.

### Which extra columns you may select

The rule from the basics: every column in the `SELECT` is either in the `GROUP BY` or inside an aggregate. Here is one refinement.

A **primary key** is the column that tells every row apart, like `customers.id`: no two customers share one. Grouping by a table's primary key also lets you select that table's other columns:

```sql
SELECT c.id, c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS orders
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id;
```

`c.name` is not grouped or aggregated, but this is fine. Each group is exactly one customer, so it has exactly one name. [[PostgreSQL accepts this too|functional-dependency]].

SQLite goes further, and this part is a trap. It lets you select *any* column that is neither grouped nor aggregated, and it [[silently picks a value|bare-columns]] from some row in the group:

```sql
SELECT city, name, COUNT(*) FROM customers GROUP BY city;
```

The London group holds Ada and Dev, but only one name comes back, and nothing tells you which. Other databases reject this query, and so should you. When in doubt, add the column to the `GROUP BY`.

**Watch out:** group by the piece you mean. `GROUP BY placed` makes one group per exact timestamp, and here every order has a different one, so every group holds a single order. To count per month, group by the [[month piece|month-buckets]].

::: context group-alias Grouping by the name you gave it
In this lesson the whole expression is written again in `GROUP BY`. That works in every database. SQLite and PostgreSQL also let you group by the name you gave the column with `AS`, so `GROUP BY day` does the same job. You will see that shortcut in later lessons, such as `GROUP BY domain` in the lesson "Working with text". Some databases, such as Microsoft SQL Server, do not allow it, so writing the expression out is the safe habit.
:::

::: context null-group Why the NULLs stick together
You learned that `NULL = NULL` is unknown, not true. So why do the NULLs share one group? Because the SQL standard makes a special rule for grouping: `GROUP BY` and `DISTINCT` treat all NULLs as the same value. Otherwise every NULL would get a group of its own, which would be useless. The NULL group is often a sign of missing data worth a look. In the lesson "Working with text" a missing email turns into exactly this kind of NULL group.
:::

::: context functional-dependency Why the primary key is enough
If you know a customer's `id`, you know their `name`, because only one row has that id. Database people say `name` is *functionally dependent* on `id`: one determines the other. The SQL standard allows selecting such columns, and PostgreSQL has accepted them since version 9.1 (2011). It only works for columns of the table whose primary key you grouped by; `o.status` would still be refused, because one customer can have many orders.
:::

::: context bare-columns SQLite's "bare columns"
A column in a grouped query that is neither grouped nor aggregated is called a bare column. SQLite's documentation says a bare column takes its value from an arbitrary row of the group. There is one exception: with `MIN(...)` or `MAX(...)`, the bare columns come from the row that holds the minimum or maximum. So `SELECT city, name, MAX(joined) FROM customers GROUP BY city;` gives the newest customer in each city. That is handy, but it only works in SQLite and it is easy to misread. MySQL rejects bare columns by default, and PostgreSQL always does.
:::

::: context month-buckets Timestamps into months
This is the task's grouping as a picture. Each order's timestamp is cut to its first 7 characters, and orders with the same piece share a bucket.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="22" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-03-02 10:15</text>
  <text x="10" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-03-20 18:40</text>
  <text x="10" y="66" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-03-21 09:05</text>
  <text x="10" y="88" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-04-07 14:30</text>
  <text x="10" y="110" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-04-07 20:10</text>
  <text x="10" y="132" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-05-15 11:00</text>
  <text x="10" y="154" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-05-28 16:45</text>
  <text x="10" y="176" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">2024-06-01 08:30</text>
  <line x1="118" y1="18" x2="228" y2="40" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>

  <text x="290" y="89" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2024-04 · 2</text>

  <text x="290" y="133" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2024-05 · 2</text>
  <rect x="230" y="158" width="120" height="28" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="290" y="177" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2024-06 · 1</text>

```

The lesson "Dates and times" does the same with a date function, `strftime('%Y-%m', placed)`.
:::
--- task
Return one row per month of orders, using every order whatever its status, with three columns:

- `month`: the first 7 characters of `placed`, like `'2024-03'` (use `substr`)
- `orders`: how many orders were placed that month
- `customers`: how many **different** customers ordered that month

Sort by month.
--- starter
SELECT placed, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY placed;
--- solution
SELECT substr(placed, 1, 7) AS month,
       COUNT(*) AS orders,
       COUNT(DISTINCT customer_id) AS customers
FROM orders
GROUP BY substr(placed, 1, 7)
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
Run the starter: every row has a count of 1, because every timestamp is different. Group by the month piece instead of the whole `placed`.
--- hint
`substr(placed, 1, 7)` gives the month. Use it in the `SELECT` (with `AS month`) and in the `GROUP BY`.
--- hint
Add `COUNT(*) AS orders` and `COUNT(DISTINCT customer_id) AS customers`, then finish with `ORDER BY month`.
--- check result | Four months: March 3 orders from 2 customers, then April, May, June
ordered
[["2024-03", 3, 2], ["2024-04", 2, 2], ["2024-05", 2, 2], ["2024-06", 1, 1]]
--- check source | Works the month out of placed
[Ss][Uu][Bb][Ss][Tt][Rr]|[Ss][Tt][Rr][Ff][Tt][Ii][Mm][Ee]

+++ practice | Cities, and the customers with none
--- task
Return one row per `city` of `customers`, with three columns:

- `city`
- `customers`: how many customers live there
- `newest`: the most recent `joined` date among them

The customers with no city form one group of their own, with NULL as its `city`, and it must be in the result. Sort by `city`; the NULL group comes first.
--- starter

FROM customers
WHERE city IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY city
ORDER BY city;

FROM customers
GROUP BY city
ORDER BY city;
--- hint
The starter throws the customers with no city away. This time they should stay, as their own group.
--- hint
