+++ practice | Orders by city
--- task
For every order, whatever its status, look up the city of the customer who placed it. Return one row per city with three columns:

- `city`
- `buyers`: how many **different** customers from that city placed orders
- `orders`: how many orders came from that city

Leave out orders from customers with no city. Sort by `orders`, highest first.
--- starter
SELECT c.city, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
GROUP BY c.city;
--- solution
SELECT c.city,
       COUNT(DISTINCT c.id) AS buyers,
       COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
WHERE c.city IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY c.city
ORDER BY orders DESC;
--- hint
Run the starter: there is a NULL group, from Cleo's order. Drop it with a `WHERE` before grouping.
--- hint
Each row is one order, so `COUNT(*)` counts orders. Count buyers with `COUNT(DISTINCT c.id)`.
--- check result | London 2 buyers and 4 orders, Paris 1 and 2, Berlin 1 and 1
ordered
[["London", 2, 4], ["Paris", 1, 2], ["Berlin", 1, 1]]
--- check source | Counts different customers

+++ practice | Log entries per day
--- schema
CREATE TABLE logs (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  craft TEXT NOT NULL,
  at TEXT
);
INSERT INTO logs (id, craft, at) VALUES
  (1, 'Vega', '2024-03-31 23:59:59'),
  (2, 'Vega', '2024-04-01 00:00:00'),
  (3, 'Rho',  '2024-04-01 12:30:00'),
  (4, 'Vega', '2024-04-01 18:00:00'),

  (6, 'Tau',  '2024-04-02 09:00:00'),
  (7, 'Tau',  NULL),
  (8, 'Rho',  '2024-04-02 23:00:00'),
  (9, 'Rho',  '2024-04-02 23:30:00');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `logs`, with `id`, `craft` and `at`, a timestamp like `'2024-04-01 12:30:00'`. Some entries lost their timestamp, so `at` is NULL.

Return one row per day with three columns:

- `day`: the first 10 characters of `at`, like `'2024-04-01'`
- `entries`: how many log entries were made that day
- `crafts`: how many **different** crafts logged that day

Leave out the entries with no timestamp. One second before midnight and midnight itself are on different days. Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT at AS day, COUNT(*) AS entries
FROM logs
GROUP BY at
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
SELECT substr(at, 1, 10) AS day,
       COUNT(*) AS entries,
       COUNT(DISTINCT craft) AS crafts
FROM logs
WHERE at IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY substr(at, 1, 10)
ORDER BY day;
--- hint
Group by the day piece, `substr(at, 1, 10)`, not by the whole timestamp.
--- hint
Without a filter the NULL timestamps form a group of their own. Add `WHERE at IS NOT NULL`, and count crafts with `COUNT(DISTINCT craft)`.
--- check result | 31 March 1 entry, 1 April 3 entries from 2 crafts, 2 April 3 from 2; no NULL day

+++ practice | The latest date in each city
--- task
This report should show each city with its number of `customers`, `first_joined` (the earliest `joined` date in that city) and `last_joined` (the latest one), skipping customers with no city and sorted by city. SQLite runs it without complaint, but in every city `last_joined` is the same as `first_joined`. In London, Dev joined two months after Ada.

Find the column that is neither grouped nor aggregated, and fix it.
--- starter
SELECT city, COUNT(*) AS customers, MIN(joined) AS first_joined, joined AS last_joined
FROM customers
WHERE city IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY city
ORDER BY city;
--- solution
SELECT city, COUNT(*) AS customers, MIN(joined) AS first_joined, MAX(joined) AS last_joined
FROM customers
WHERE city IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY city
ORDER BY city;
--- hint
`joined AS last_joined` is not in the `GROUP BY` and not inside an aggregate. SQLite quietly takes it from one row of each group, and with a `MIN` in the query that row is the one holding the minimum.
--- hint
"The latest date in the group" is an aggregate too: `MAX(joined)`.
--- check result | London runs from 2024-01-15 to 2024-03-11, Paris from 2024-02-03 to 2024-05-02
ordered
[["Berlin", 1, "2024-04-01", "2024-04-01"], ["London", 2, "2024-01-15", "2024-03-11"], ["Paris", 2, "2024-02-03", "2024-05-02"]]
--- check source | Takes the latest date with MAX
[Mm][Aa][Xx]\s*\(\s*joined\s*\)
--- check source absent | No bare joined column left
,\s*joined\s+[Aa][Ss]

+++ practice | Temperature by the hour
--- schema
CREATE TABLE telemetry (
  craft TEXT NOT NULL,
  at TEXT NOT NULL,
  temp REAL
);
INSERT INTO telemetry (craft, at, temp) VALUES
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01 10:05:00', 20.1),
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01 10:40:00', 20.9),
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01 11:10:00', 21.4),
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01 11:55:00', 22.0),
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01 11:59:59', 21.8),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-01 10:30:00', 15.0),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-01 12:00:00', 15.5),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-01 12:01:00', 16.1),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-02 10:15:00', 14.9),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-02 10:45:00', NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `telemetry`, with `craft`, `at` (a timestamp like `'2024-05-01 10:05:00'`) and `temp`, which is NULL when a reading was lost.

Return one row per craft **and** hour, with five columns:

- `craft`
- `hour`: the first 13 characters of `at`, like `'2024-05-01 10'`, so the same hour on two different days stays apart
- `readings`: how many rows fall in that hour, lost readings included
- `low` and `high`: the lowest and highest `temp` in that hour

Keep only the hours with at least 2 rows. Sort by `craft`, then `hour`.
--- starter
SELECT craft, substr(at, 12, 2) AS hour, COUNT(*) AS readings
FROM telemetry
GROUP BY craft, substr(at, 12, 2);
--- solution
SELECT craft,
       substr(at, 1, 13) AS hour,
       COUNT(*) AS readings,
       MIN(temp) AS low,
       MAX(temp) AS high
FROM telemetry
GROUP BY craft, substr(at, 1, 13)
HAVING COUNT(*) >= 2
ORDER BY craft, hour;
--- hint
The starter's hour is only the two digits, so 10 o'clock on 1 May and on 2 May land in one group. Take the first 13 characters instead.
--- hint
Group by two things at once: `GROUP BY craft, substr(at, 1, 13)`. `MIN` and `MAX` skip the NULL reading, while `COUNT(*)` still counts its row.
--- hint
The "at least 2 rows" rule needs the count, so it goes in `HAVING COUNT(*) >= 2`.
--- check result | Rho at noon on 1 May and 10:00 on 2 May, Vega at 10:00 and 11:00 on 1 May
ordered
[["Rho", "2024-05-01 12", 2, 15.5, 16.1], ["Rho", "2024-05-02 10", 2, 14.9, 14.9], ["Vega", "2024-05-01 10", 2, 20.1, 20.9], ["Vega", "2024-05-01 11", 3, 21.4, 22.0]]
--- check source | Groups by the craft and the hour
[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy]\s+craft\s*,|[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy][^;]*,\s*craft\b
--- check source | Keeps the busy hours with HAVING
[Hh][Aa][Vv][Ii][Nn][Gg]

=== sql2-05 | Subqueries: a value, or a list
--- teach
Last lesson you sorted orders into monthly buckets. This lesson goes back to an idea from the end of the basics, the subquery, and gives it a second shape. Before, a subquery gave back one value. Now it can give back a whole list, and you can ask "is this row's id on that list?".

### The picture

Picture a party with a guest list at the door. The person at the door does not care how many times your name was written on the list. They only ask one question: is your name on it, yes or no? If yes, you go in, once.

### A quick reminder: one value

A **subquery** is a whole `SELECT` in round brackets, used inside another query. What it gives back decides where it can go.

A subquery that gives back one row with one column is a **[[scalar|scalar-word]] subquery**: a single value. It can sit anywhere a single value can, even in the `SELECT` list:

```sql
SELECT name, price,
       price - (SELECT AVG(price) FROM products) AS above_avg
FROM products;
```

The inner query works out the average price, 26.625, once. Then every product is compared with it:

| name | price | above_avg |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Notebook | 4.5 | -22.125 |
| Fountain pen | 32.0 | 5.375 |
| Headphones | 89.0 | 62.375 |

(Five more rows follow, one for each other product.)

### The new shape: a list

A subquery that gives back **one column of many rows** is a list. Run this one on its own first. It gives the customer id of every shipped order:

```sql
SELECT customer_id FROM orders WHERE status = 'shipped';
```

It returns `1`, `1`, `2`, `4`. Customer 1, Ada, shipped two orders, so 1 appears twice.

A list pairs with `IN`. You met `IN ('paid', 'shipped')` in the basics: it means "matches any value in this list". Now the list is a subquery:

```sql
SELECT name FROM customers
WHERE id IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders WHERE status = 'shipped');
```

| name |
| --- |
| Ada |
| Ben |
| Dev |

For each customer, `IN` asks a yes-or-no question: is this customer's `id` [[on that list|membership]]? Ada's id, 1, is on it, so Ada is in the answer, once. It does not matter that 1 is on the list twice.

### Why not a join?

You could get the same names with a join. But a join multiplies:

```sql
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status = 'shipped';
```

This gives Ada, Ada, Ben, Dev. A join makes one row per *matching order*, so a customer with two shipped orders [[appears twice|join-fanout]]. You could bolt on `DISTINCT`, which drops repeated rows (the lesson DISTINCT, UNION and UNION ALL covers it), to undo the damage. `IN` never makes the copies in the first place.

### Subqueries inside subqueries

The list can be built from anything: a join, or another subquery. SQL does not mind how deep it goes. Who bought the headphones, product 5?

```sql
SELECT name FROM customers
WHERE id IN (
  SELECT customer_id FROM orders
  WHERE id IN (SELECT order_id FROM order_items WHERE product_id = 5)
);
```

Read it from the inside out. The innermost query finds the orders that contain product 5 (orders 2 and 6). The middle one turns those orders into customer ids (1 and 4). The outer one turns ids into names: Ada and Dev. A query built like this often reads [[close to the question you were asked|real-questions]].

**Watch out:** the list must hold the *same kind* of id as the column you test. `WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM orders ...)` compares customer ids with **order** ids. SQLite gives no error; it quietly returns the wrong customers. Run the inner query on its own and ask: are these customer ids? A subquery in `IN` must also give back exactly one column. `IN (SELECT * FROM orders)` stops with the error `sub-select returns 5 columns - expected 1`.

You may be wondering about [[the opposite, NOT IN|not-in-later]], for "not on the list". It exists, and it has a trap of its own.

::: context scalar-word Where "scalar" comes from
In maths and physics, a **scalar** is a single number, like a temperature or a mass. It is the opposite of a vector, which has several parts, like a velocity with a direction. The word comes from "scale": a scalar only makes things bigger or smaller. Databases borrowed it for "one single value". A scalar subquery is one that promises one row with one column. If it finds no rows at all, its value is NULL.
:::

::: context membership A list, not a count
`IN` only checks membership: is the value there, yes or no. Duplicates on the list change nothing, and the list's order changes nothing.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="70" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">customers</text>
  <rect x="20" y="28" width="100" height="22" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="70" y="43" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1 Ada</text>
  <rect x="20" y="54" width="100" height="22" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="70" y="69" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2 Ben</text>
  <rect x="20" y="80" width="100" height="22" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
  <text x="70" y="95" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">3 Cleo</text>
  <rect x="20" y="106" width="100" height="22" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="70" y="121" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4 Dev</text>
  <text x="280" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">the list</text>
  <rect x="240" y="28" width="80" height="100" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="280" y="50" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="280" y="72" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="280" y="94" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2</text>
  <text x="280" y="116" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4</text>
  <text x="180" y="62" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">on it?</text>
  <line x1="124" y1="70" x2="234" y2="70" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="146" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">yes: Ada, Ben, Dev once each · no: Cleo</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context join-fanout Rows that multiply
A join pairs each row on the left with every matching row on the right. One customer with two matching orders becomes two rows. This is called **fan-out**, and it is behind many wrong reports: sums counted twice, customers listed twice. The lesson Debugging: the join that double-counts hunts down one of these. Whenever you only want to know *whether* a match exists, not *what* it is, a subquery with `IN` (or `EXISTS`, next lesson) avoids the problem.
:::

::: context real-questions How engineers use it
Mission teams ask questions shaped exactly like this. "Which spacecraft had a critical alert this week?" is: spacecraft whose id is in the list of craft ids from this week's critical alerts. "Which users hit an error today?" is: users whose id is in the list of user ids from today's error log. Inside, databases like PostgreSQL often turn an `IN (subquery)` into a special join, called a semi-join, that stops at the first match. You write the clear version, and the database finds a fast way to run it.
:::

::: context not-in-later The opposite has a trap
`NOT IN` keeps the rows whose value is *not* on the list. It works, until the list contains a NULL. Then it quietly returns no rows at all. The lesson Problem solving: finding what is missing shows why, and gives a safer way to ask.
:::
--- task
Return the `name` of every customer who has bought at least one product in the `'electronics'` category (any order status), **each name once**, in alphabetical order.

Use `WHERE id IN (...)` on `customers`. Inside the brackets, build the list of customer ids from `orders`, `order_items` and `products`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE p.category = 'electronics'
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT name
FROM customers
WHERE id IN (
  SELECT o.customer_id
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE p.category = 'electronics'
)
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Ada appears twice, because Ada bought electronics in two different orders. That is the join multiplying rows.
--- hint
Keep the starter's joins, but use them only to build a list. Change them into a subquery that selects `o.customer_id`, starting `FROM orders o`.
--- hint
The shape is `SELECT name FROM customers WHERE id IN (SELECT o.customer_id FROM orders o JOIN ... WHERE p.category = 'electronics') ORDER BY name;`.
--- check result | Ada, Ben and Dev, once each
ordered
[["Ada"], ["Ben"], ["Dev"]]
--- check source | Uses IN with a subquery
\b[Ii][Nn]\s*\(\s*[Ss][Ee][Ll][Ee][Cc][Tt]

+++ practice | Products bought two at a time
--- task
Return the `name` of every product that appears in at least one order line with a `qty` of 2 or more, in any order. Sort by name.

Use `WHERE id IN (...)` on `products`, with a subquery on `order_items` inside the brackets.
--- starter
SELECT p.name
FROM products p
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.product_id = p.id
WHERE oi.qty >= 2
ORDER BY p.name;
--- solution

FROM products
WHERE id IN (SELECT product_id FROM order_items WHERE qty >= 2)
ORDER BY name;
--- hint

--- hint

+++ practice | Each price against the top price
--- task
For every product, return three columns:

- `name` and `price`
- `pct_of_top`: its price as a percentage of the most expensive product's price, rounded to 1 decimal with `ROUND(..., 1)`

Work the top price out with a subquery, not by typing it. The most expensive product itself shows `100.0`. Sort by `pct_of_top`, highest first, then by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT name, price
FROM products;
--- solution
SELECT name, price,
       ROUND(price * 100 / (SELECT MAX(price) FROM products), 1) AS pct_of_top
FROM products
ORDER BY pct_of_top DESC, name;
--- hint
`(SELECT MAX(price) FROM products)` is a single value, so it can sit in the middle of a calculation.
--- hint
The percentage is `price * 100 / (SELECT MAX(price) FROM products)`. Wrap it in `ROUND(..., 1)` and name it `AS pct_of_top`.

+++ practice | Customers who came back
--- task
Return the `name` and `city` of every customer who has placed **at least 2 orders**, of any status. Sort by name.

Use `WHERE id IN (...)` on `customers`. Build the list of customer ids inside the brackets with `GROUP BY` and `HAVING`.
--- starter

FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- solution

FROM customers

  FROM orders
  GROUP BY customer_id

+++ practice | The blue team's most flown
--- schema
CREATE TABLE crew (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  team TEXT NOT NULL,
  hours INTEGER
);
INSERT INTO crew (id, name, team, hours) VALUES
  (1, 'Ari', 'blue',  1200),
  (2, 'Bex', 'blue',  1500),
  (3, 'Cyd', 'blue',  1500),
  (4, 'Dov', 'red',   1500),
  (5, 'Eda', 'red',    900),
  (6, 'Fin', 'blue',  NULL),
  (7, 'Gus', 'green', NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `crew`, with `id`, `name`, `team` and `hours` (flight hours, NULL for someone still in training).

Return the `name` and `hours` of the member or members of the `'blue'` team with the most flight hours. If several blue members tie for the most, return all of them. Someone on another team with the same hours is not on the blue team. Sort by name.
--- starter

FROM crew
WHERE team = 'blue'
ORDER BY hours DESC
LIMIT 1;
--- solution
SELECT name, hours
FROM crew
WHERE team = 'blue'
  AND hours = (SELECT MAX(hours) FROM crew WHERE team = 'blue')
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
`LIMIT 1` keeps one row even when two people tie. Instead, find the top number of hours first, then keep everyone who has it.
--- hint
The top is `(SELECT MAX(hours) FROM crew WHERE team = 'blue')`. `MAX` skips the NULL hours.
--- hint
Keep the team filter in the outer query too, or Dov from the red team, who also has 1500, sneaks in.

+++ practice | Above-average spenders
--- task
Find the customers who spend more than the average customer. Count only orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, and a customer's spend is the sum of `qty * unit_price` over those orders.

The average is taken over the customers who spent anything; customers with no such orders are not part of it. Return two columns:

- `name`
- `spend`: that customer's spend, rounded to 2 decimals

Sort by `spend`, highest first. Name the per-customer spend as a step with `WITH`, then compare each customer with the average of that step.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS spend
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY spend DESC;
--- solution
WITH spend AS (
  SELECT o.customer_id, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) AS total
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
  GROUP BY o.customer_id
)
SELECT c.name, ROUND(s.total, 2) AS spend
FROM spend s
JOIN customers c ON c.id = s.customer_id
WHERE s.total > (SELECT AVG(total) FROM spend)
ORDER BY s.total DESC;
--- hint
The starter already works out every customer's spend. Move that work into a named step, `WITH spend AS (...)`, with one row per `customer_id`.
--- hint
The average spend is one value: `(SELECT AVG(total) FROM spend)`. Keep the rows of `spend` whose total is above it.
--- hint
Join the step to `customers` to turn each `customer_id` into a name. The average is 82.60.
--- check result | Ada 127.6 and Dev 107.0 (the average is 82.6)
ordered
[["Ada", 127.6], ["Dev", 107.0]]
--- check source | Names a step with WITH
[Ww][Ii][Tt][Hh]\s+\w+\s+[Aa][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source | Compares with the average
[Aa][Vv][Gg]\s*\(

=== sql2-06 | Correlated subqueries and EXISTS
--- teach
Last lesson a subquery built a list, and `IN` checked each customer against it. That subquery ran once and gave the same list to every row. This lesson meets a subquery that gives a *different* answer for each row, and a new word, `EXISTS`, that asks "is there at least one?".

### The picture

Picture a school where each class has its own average test score. To find the students who beat *their own class's* average, you cannot use one number for everyone. For each student you look up their class, work out that class's average, and compare. The scrap-paper step changes from row to row.

### Averages per category

Products come in three categories, and each has its own average price. You can see them with `GROUP BY`:

```sql
SELECT category, AVG(price) FROM products GROUP BY category;
```

| category | AVG(price) |
| --- | --- |
| electronics | 47.75 |
| home | 21.0 |
| stationery | 18.1666... |

### A subquery that looks at the outer row

Now find each product priced above the average *of its own category*:

```sql
SELECT p.name, p.price
FROM products p
WHERE p.price > (SELECT AVG(x.price) FROM products x WHERE x.category = p.category);
```

| name | price |
| --- | --- |
| Fountain pen | 32.0 |
| Desk lamp | 45.0 |
| Headphones | 89.0 |

The inner query mentions `p.category`, which belongs to the *outer* row. A subquery that reaches out to the outer row like this is a **[[correlated|correlated-word]] subquery**. It runs (in idea, at least) [[once for every outer row|per-row]].

Walk through two rows. For the Fountain pen, `p.category` is `'stationery'`. The inner query averages the stationery prices: 18.17. Is 32 bigger? Yes, so it stays. For the Mug, `p.category` is `'home'`. The home average is 21. Is 9 bigger? No, so it goes.

The two aliases matter. `products` appears twice, once outside as `p` and once inside as `x`. Without [[two different names|alias-clash]], SQL cannot tell the product being tested from the products being averaged.

### EXISTS: is there at least one?

Picture knocking on a door. You only want to know if anyone is home. You do not need to know who, or how many.

`EXISTS (subquery)` is true when the subquery returns **at least one row**. It never looks at *what* is in the rows, so people write [[SELECT 1|select-one]] inside it: the 1 is a placeholder that means "a row, any row".

```sql
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status = 'refunded');
```

For each customer, the inner query looks for a refunded order of theirs. Only Eve has one, so the answer is Eve.

`EXISTS` is nearly always correlated: here `o.customer_id = c.id` ties it to the outer customer. And it can stop at the first matching row, which makes it cheap.

### EXISTS as a yes/no column

In SQLite, `EXISTS (...)` is also a value: [[1 for yes, 0 for no|true-is-one]]. So it can go in the `SELECT` list and make a yes/no column:

```sql
SELECT c.name,
       EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) AS has_orders
FROM customers c;
```

Ada, Ben, Cleo, Dev and Eve get `1`. Finn and Gus have no orders, so they get `0`.

**Watch out:** the link to the outer row is what makes the subquery correlated. Leave out `o.customer_id = c.id` and the inner query no longer asks about *this* customer. It asks "is there a refunded order anywhere?", which is yes, so every customer comes back. When an `EXISTS` returns every row, check the link first.

::: context correlated-word What "correlated" means
To be correlated is to be connected: when one thing changes, the other changes with it. A correlated subquery is connected to the outer query: when the outer row changes, the inner query's answer changes too. The subqueries in the last lesson were not correlated. They could run on their own, pasted into an empty editor. A correlated one cannot, because `p.category` means nothing without an outer `p`. That is a quick test for which kind you have.
:::

::: context per-row Once for every row
In idea, the database runs the inner query again for each outer row, with that row's values filled in.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="70" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">outer row p</text>
  <rect x="10" y="28" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="70" y="44" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Fountain pen · 32</text>
  <rect x="10" y="62" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="70" y="78" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Mug · 9</text>
  <rect x="10" y="96" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="70" y="112" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Headphones · 89</text>
  <text x="265" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">inner AVG for p's category</text>
  <line x1="132" y1="40" x2="196" y2="40" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="132" y1="74" x2="196" y2="74" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="132" y1="108" x2="196" y2="108" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="200" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">stationery 18.17</text>
  <text x="310" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">keep</text>
  <text x="200" y="78" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">home 21.00</text>
  <text x="310" y="78" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">drop</text>
  <text x="200" y="112" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44">electronics 47.75</text>
  <text x="310" y="112" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1">keep</text>
  <text x="180" y="142" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">one inner run per outer row</text>
</svg>
```

A real database is free to find a quicker route, as long as the answer is the same.
:::

::: context alias-clash When the names clash
If you write `WHERE category = category` inside the subquery, SQL reads both names as the *inner* table's column, because a plain column name belongs to the nearest `FROM`. Every product equals itself, so the condition is always true and you get the average of all products, not of the category. No error warns you. Giving each copy its own alias, and prefixing every column with it, makes the link impossible to get wrong.
:::

::: context select-one Why SELECT 1
`EXISTS` only counts whether rows come back, and ignores their contents. `SELECT *`, `SELECT o.id` or `SELECT 'hello'` would all give the same answer. Writing `SELECT 1` tells the next person who reads your query: the columns here do not matter. It is a habit, not a rule, and you will see it in almost every codebase that uses `EXISTS`.
:::

::: context true-is-one Yes and no as numbers
SQLite has no separate true/false type. A condition that is true has the value 1, and false has the value 0. You can see it for yourself: `SELECT 5 > 3;` returns 1. That is why `EXISTS` can sit in the `SELECT` list. Its opposite, `NOT EXISTS`, comes back in the lesson Problem solving: finding what is missing, where it is the safest way to find rows with no match.
:::
--- task
Return every product priced **above the average price of its own category**, with four columns:

- `name`, `category` and `price`
- `sold_shipped`: `1` if the product appears in at least one `'shipped'` order, `0` if not

Use a correlated subquery for the category average, and `EXISTS` for `sold_shipped`. The `EXISTS` needs `order_items` joined to `orders`, so it can see each item's order status.
--- starter
SELECT name, category, price
FROM products
WHERE price > (SELECT AVG(price) FROM products);
--- solution
SELECT p.name, p.category, p.price,
       EXISTS (
         SELECT 1
         FROM order_items oi
         JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
         WHERE oi.product_id = p.id AND o.status = 'shipped'
       ) AS sold_shipped
FROM products p
WHERE p.price > (SELECT AVG(x.price) FROM products x WHERE x.category = p.category);
--- hint
The starter compares every product with the average of *all* products. Give the outer table the alias `p`, the inner one the alias `x`, and add `WHERE x.category = p.category` inside the brackets.
--- hint
For the flag, add a fourth column to the `SELECT`: `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM order_items oi JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id WHERE ...) AS sold_shipped`.
--- hint
Inside that `EXISTS`, link to the outer product and check the status: `WHERE oi.product_id = p.id AND o.status = 'shipped'`.
--- check result | Fountain pen (never shipped), Desk lamp and Headphones
[["Fountain pen", "stationery", 32.0, 0], ["Desk lamp", "home", 45.0, 1], ["Headphones", "electronics", 89.0, 1]]
--- check source | Uses EXISTS
\b[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(

+++ practice | Customers who bought for the home
--- task
Return the `name` of every customer who has at least one order (any status) containing a product from the `'home'` category. Sort by name.

Use `WHERE EXISTS (...)` on `customers`. Inside, join `orders`, `order_items` and `products`, and link the orders to the outer customer.
--- starter
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
WHERE EXISTS (
  SELECT 1
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
  WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND p.category = 'home'
)
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Inside the `EXISTS`, look for one row: an order item of this customer whose product is in the home category.
--- hint
The link to the outer row is `o.customer_id = c.id`. Put it in the inner `WHERE` together with `p.category = 'home'`.
--- check result | Ada, Ben, Dev and Eve
ordered
[["Ada"], ["Ben"], ["Dev"], ["Eve"]]
--- check source | Uses EXISTS
\b[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(

+++ practice | Each customer's orders, counted inside
--- task
For **every** customer, return three columns:

- `name`
- `orders`: how many orders they have placed, of any status
- `last_placed`: the `placed` value of their most recent order, or NULL if they have none

Work out `orders` and `last_placed` with correlated subqueries in the `SELECT` list, one for each. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name,
       (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) AS orders,
       (SELECT MAX(o.placed) FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) AS last_placed
FROM customers c
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
A subquery in the `SELECT` list can mention the outer row, `c`. It then gives a different answer for each customer.
--- hint
The count is `(SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id)`. `COUNT(*)` over no rows is 0, and `MAX` over no rows is NULL.
--- check result | Ada 3 orders, last on 1 June; Finn and Gus 0 and NULL
ordered
[["Ada", 3, "2024-06-01 08:30:00"], ["Ben", 2, "2024-04-07 14:30:00"], ["Cleo", 1, "2024-04-07 20:10:00"], ["Dev", 1, "2024-05-15 11:00:00"], ["Eve", 1, "2024-05-28 16:45:00"], ["Finn", 0, null], ["Gus", 0, null]]
--- check source | Uses a subquery linked to the customer

+++ practice | An order above the customer's usual
--- task
For each order, its total is the sum of `qty * unit_price` over its items. Find the orders whose total is **above the average order total of the same customer**. Return three columns:

- `order_id`
- `customer`: the customer's name
- `total`: the order's total, rounded to 2 decimals

A customer with a single order can never be above their own average. Sort by `order_id`.

Name the order totals as a step with `WITH`, one row per order with its `customer_id`, then compare each order with a correlated average over that step.
--- starter
SELECT o.id AS order_id, c.name AS customer,
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS total
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c    ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY o.id, c.name
ORDER BY o.id;
--- solution
WITH t AS (
  SELECT o.id, o.customer_id, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) AS total
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  GROUP BY o.id, o.customer_id
)
SELECT t.id AS order_id, c.name AS customer, ROUND(t.total, 2) AS total
FROM t
JOIN customers c ON c.id = t.customer_id
WHERE t.total > (SELECT AVG(x.total) FROM t x WHERE x.customer_id = t.customer_id)
ORDER BY t.id;
--- hint
Put the order totals in a step: `WITH t AS (SELECT o.id, o.customer_id, SUM(...) AS total FROM ... GROUP BY o.id, o.customer_id)`.
--- hint
Use the step twice, like the lesson used `products` twice: once outside as `t`, once inside the subquery as `x`. Link them with `x.customer_id = t.customer_id`.
--- hint
Ada's orders are 22.50, 80.10 and 25.00, so her average is 42.53 and only order 2 is above it.
--- check result | Order 2 (Ada, 80.1) and order 4 (Ben, 58.0)
ordered
[[2, "Ada", 80.1], [4, "Ben", 58.0]]
--- check source | Names a step with WITH
[Ww][Ii][Tt][Hh]\s+\w+\s+[Aa][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source | Compares with an average
[Aa][Vv][Gg]\s*\(

+++ practice | Heavier than its kind
--- schema
CREATE TABLE parts (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  kind TEXT NOT NULL,
  mass REAL
);
INSERT INTO parts (id, name, kind, mass) VALUES
  (1,  'Valve A', 'valve', 2.0),
  (2,  'Valve B', 'valve', 3.0),
  (3,  'Valve C', 'valve', 4.0),
  (4,  'Valve D', 'valve', NULL),
  (5,  'Pump',    'pump',  10.0),
  (6,  'Strut X', 'strut', 5.0),
  (7,  'Strut Y', 'strut', 5.0),
  (8,  'Tank T',  'tank',  NULL),
  (9,  'Tank U',  'tank',  50.0),
  (10, 'Panel 1', 'panel', 1.0),
  (11, 'Panel 2', 'panel', 1.5),
  (12, 'Panel 3', 'panel', 3.5);
CREATE TABLE stock (
  part_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES parts(id),
  qty INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO stock (part_id, qty) VALUES
  (3, 4), (9, 1), (12, 0);
--- task
This problem has its own database. `parts` has `id`, `name`, `kind` and `mass` (NULL when the part has not been weighed yet). `stock` has `part_id` and `qty`, the number on the shelf.

Return every part that is **strictly heavier** than the average mass of its own `kind`, with four columns:

- `name`, `kind` and `mass`
- `in_stock`: `1` if there is a `stock` row for it with `qty` above 0, `0` if not

Averages skip the parts that were never weighed. A part alone in its kind, or in a kind where every part weighs the same, is never heavier than its own average. Panel 3 has a stock row, but with `qty` 0, so it is not in stock. Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT name, kind, mass
FROM parts
WHERE mass > (SELECT AVG(mass) FROM parts);
--- solution
SELECT p.name, p.kind, p.mass,
       EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM stock s WHERE s.part_id = p.id AND s.qty > 0) AS in_stock
FROM parts p
WHERE p.mass > (SELECT AVG(x.mass) FROM parts x WHERE x.kind = p.kind)
ORDER BY p.name;
--- hint
The starter compares every part with the average of *all* parts. Give the outer table the alias `p`, the inner one `x`, and link them with `x.kind = p.kind`.
--- hint
For `in_stock`, add `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM stock s WHERE s.part_id = p.id AND ...)` to the `SELECT` list. A row with `qty` 0 should not count.
--- check result | Panel 3 (not in stock) and Valve C (in stock); the pump, struts and tanks are not above their kind
ordered
[["Panel 3", "panel", 3.5, 0], ["Valve C", "valve", 4.0, 1]]
--- check source | Uses EXISTS
\b[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a mass
\b(2\.0|3\.0|3\.5|4\.0)\b

This query should return the customers who have at least one order whose `status` is `'refunded'` or `'canceled'`, sorted by name. The right answer is Ben and Eve. It returns all seven customers.

+++ practice | Latest reading per sensor
--- schema
CREATE TABLE sensors (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  limit_c REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO sensors (id, name, limit_c) VALUES
  (1, 'cabin', 30.0), (2, 'battery', 45.0), (3, 'engine', 90.0), (4, 'spare', 50.0);
CREATE TABLE readings (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  sensor_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES sensors(id),
  at TEXT NOT NULL,
  value REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO readings (id, sensor_id, at, value) VALUES
  (1, 1, '2024-06-01 10:00:00', 22.0),
  (2, 1, '2024-06-01 11:00:00', 31.0),
  (3, 1, '2024-06-01 12:00:00', 24.5),
  (4, 2, '2024-06-01 10:00:00', 40.0),
  (5, 2, '2024-06-01 12:30:00', 44.9),
  (6, 3, '2024-06-01 09:00:00', 90.0),
  (7, 3, '2024-06-01 13:00:00', 88.0);
--- task
This problem has its own database. `sensors` has `id`, `name` and `limit_c`, the highest safe temperature. `readings` has `id`, `sensor_id`, `at` (a timestamp) and `value`.

For every sensor that has at least one reading, return four columns:

- `name`
- `latest_at` and `latest_value`: the time and value of its most recent reading
- `ever_over`: `1` if **any** of its readings was above its `limit_c`, `0` if not (a reading exactly at the limit is safe)

Sort by `name`. The spare sensor has no readings and is left out.
--- starter
SELECT s.name, r.at AS latest_at, r.value AS latest_value
FROM readings r
JOIN sensors s ON s.id = r.sensor_id
ORDER BY s.name;
--- solution
SELECT s.name, r.at AS latest_at, r.value AS latest_value,
       EXISTS (
         SELECT 1 FROM readings y
         WHERE y.sensor_id = s.id AND y.value > s.limit_c
       ) AS ever_over
FROM readings r
JOIN sensors s ON s.id = r.sensor_id
WHERE r.at = (SELECT MAX(x.at) FROM readings x WHERE x.sensor_id = r.sensor_id)
ORDER BY s.name;
--- hint
Keep only the reading whose `at` equals the latest `at` of the same sensor: a correlated `MAX` in the `WHERE`, linked with `x.sensor_id = r.sensor_id`.
--- hint
The latest reading is not always the one that went over. Ask about *any* reading of the sensor with a separate `EXISTS`, linked with `y.sensor_id = s.id`.
--- hint
Compare with the sensor's own limit inside the `EXISTS`: `y.value > s.limit_c`.
--- check result | battery 44.9 (never over), cabin 24.5 (was over at 11:00), engine 88.0 (90.0 was exactly the limit)
ordered
[["battery", "2024-06-01 12:30:00", 44.9, 0], ["cabin", "2024-06-01 12:00:00", 24.5, 1], ["engine", "2024-06-01 13:00:00", 88.0, 0]]
--- check source | Finds the latest with a correlated MAX
