--- check source | Uses EXISTS
\b[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(

=== sql2-07 | CASE: decisions inside a query
--- teach
Last lesson `EXISTS` gave you a yes/no column, 1 or 0. This lesson lets you choose *any* value from a condition: a label like `'premium'` or `'budget'`, worked out row by row.

### The picture

Picture a post office sorting parcels. The rule on the wall says: 20 kg or more goes to the heavy shelf; 5 kg or more goes to the medium shelf; everything else goes to the light shelf. The clerk reads the rules top to bottom and stops at the first one that fits.

### The CASE expression

`CASE` does the same inside a query. It checks conditions in order and gives back the value of the first one that is true:

```sql
SELECT name, price,
       CASE
         WHEN price >= 50 THEN 'premium'
         WHEN price >= 10 THEN 'standard'
         ELSE 'budget'
       END AS tier
FROM products;
```

Read each part in words:

- `CASE` starts the decision.
- `WHEN price >= 50 THEN 'premium'` means "if the price is 50 or more, the answer is `'premium'`".
- `ELSE 'budget'` is the answer when no `WHEN` was true.
- `END` closes the decision, and `AS tier` names the new column.

| name | price | tier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Notebook | 4.5 | budget |
| Fountain pen | 32.0 | standard |
| Headphones | 89.0 | premium |

(Five more products follow.) This form, with a full condition after each `WHEN`, is called the **[[searched|case-names]] CASE**.

### The first true WHEN wins

A price of 89 is `>= 50`, and it is also `>= 10`. Both are true, but `CASE` stops at the first. So order matters: put the [[narrowest condition first|if-else]]. Swap the two `WHEN`s and the headphones become `'standard'`, because `price >= 10` is now checked first.

### Leaving out ELSE

Without an `ELSE`, a row that matches no `WHEN` gets NULL:

```sql
SELECT name, CASE WHEN price >= 50 THEN 'premium' END AS tier
FROM products;
```

Only Headphones gets `'premium'`. The other seven get NULL. That is usually a sign you forgot a case, so write the `ELSE`.

### The short form

When you compare one expression with fixed values, there is a shorter form. The expression goes straight after `CASE`, and each `WHEN` holds a value to compare it with:

```sql
SELECT id, status,
       CASE status WHEN 'paid' THEN 'open' WHEN 'shipped' THEN 'done' ELSE 'closed' END AS stage
FROM orders;
```

Orders 5 and 8 are `'open'`, the shipped orders are `'done'`, and the canceled and refunded ones fall through to `'closed'`. The short form has [[one trap with NULL|case-null]].

### CASE on a group

`CASE` works anywhere an expression does: in `SELECT`, in `ORDER BY`, even inside `SUM(...)`. In a grouped query it can test an aggregate:

```sql
SELECT customer_id, COUNT(*) AS orders,
       CASE WHEN COUNT(*) >= 2 THEN 'regular' ELSE 'new' END AS kind
FROM orders
GROUP BY customer_id;
```

Customers 1 and 2 have 3 and 2 orders, so they are `'regular'`. The other three are `'new'`. Put `CASE` inside `SUM` and you can [[count only some rows|conditional-sum]].

### Edges on purpose

Bugs live on the edges. "Orders of 25 or more are medium" means `>= 25`. An order of exactly 25.00 must land in medium. Decide each [[edge|half-open]] on purpose, then find a row that sits on it and check where it lands.

**Watch out:** `>` and `>=` differ only on the edge, so a wrong one passes almost every test. `WHEN total > 25` sends an order of exactly 25.00 to the wrong bucket, and nothing warns you. Read the rule aloud: "25 or more" is `>= 25`; "more than 25" is `> 25`.

::: context case-names Searched and simple
The SQL standard names the two forms. The **searched** CASE has a full condition after each `WHEN`, so it can test anything: `price >= 50`, `city IS NULL`, `EXISTS (...)`. The **simple** CASE (the short form) names one expression after `CASE` and compares it with a value in each `WHEN`, using `=`. Every simple CASE can be written as a searched one, but not the other way round. When unsure, use the searched form.
:::

::: context if-else Like if, else if, else
If you have written Python or JavaScript, `CASE` is an `if`, `else if`, `else` chain. There is one difference. In those languages an `if` is a statement: it *does* things. `CASE` is an expression: it *is* a value, like `2 + 3` is a value. That is why it can sit inside `SELECT`, `ORDER BY` or `SUM(...)`. The rule "narrowest first" is the same in every language: `if score >= 50` before `if score >= 10`, or the second test swallows everything.
:::

::: context case-null The short form and NULL
The short form compares with `=`, and `NULL = NULL` is not true. So `CASE coupon WHEN NULL THEN 'none' ELSE coupon END` never gives `'none'`: for an order with no coupon, it falls through to `ELSE coupon`, which is NULL. Use the searched form for NULLs: `CASE WHEN coupon IS NULL THEN 'none' ELSE coupon END`. The lesson Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear digs into why NULL behaves this way.
:::

::: context conditional-sum Counting some rows
`SUM(CASE WHEN status = 'shipped' THEN 1 ELSE 0 END)` adds 1 for every shipped order and 0 for the rest. On this shop it gives 4. With one such column per status, a single query makes a whole summary row: shipped, paid, canceled, refunded side by side. Reports and dashboards are full of this pattern; people call it **conditional aggregation**. Engineers use it the same way, for example to count how many telemetry readings in each pass were in the warning range.
:::

::: context half-open Buckets without gaps
The task's sizes are buckets on a number line. Each one includes its lower edge and stops right before the next one.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="10" y1="50" x2="350" y2="50" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <rect x="10" y="38" width="90" height="24" fill="#f2b880"/>
  <rect x="100" y="38" width="160" height="24" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <rect x="260" y="38" width="90" height="24" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="55" y="30" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">small</text>
  <text x="180" y="30" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">medium</text>
  <text x="305" y="30" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">large</text>
  <circle cx="100" cy="50" r="5" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="260" cy="50" r="5" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="100" y="82" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">25</text>
  <text x="260" y="82" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">100</text>
  <text x="180" y="102" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">a filled dot belongs to the bucket on its right</text>
</svg>
```

Every value lands in exactly one bucket, with no gaps and no overlaps. Mathematicians call a range like "25 up to, but not including, 100" half-open.
:::
--- task
Return one row per order, from `order_items`, with three columns:

- `id`: the order id
- `total`: the sum of `qty * unit_price`, rounded to 2 decimals
- `size`: `'small'` under 25, `'medium'` from 25 up to (but not including) 100, and `'large'` at 100 or more

Sort by id. The starter already gives the first two columns.
--- starter
SELECT order_id AS id, ROUND(SUM(qty * unit_price), 2) AS total
FROM order_items
GROUP BY order_id
ORDER BY order_id;
--- solution
SELECT order_id AS id,
       ROUND(SUM(qty * unit_price), 2) AS total,
       CASE
         WHEN SUM(qty * unit_price) >= 100 THEN 'large'
         WHEN SUM(qty * unit_price) >= 25  THEN 'medium'
         ELSE 'small'
       END AS size
FROM order_items
GROUP BY order_id
ORDER BY order_id;
--- hint
Add a third column to the `SELECT`: `CASE WHEN ... THEN ... END AS size`. It can test `SUM(qty * unit_price)`, like the `COUNT(*)` example tested a group.
--- hint
Put the narrowest bucket first: `WHEN SUM(qty * unit_price) >= 100 THEN 'large'`.
--- hint
Then `WHEN SUM(qty * unit_price) >= 25 THEN 'medium'`, then `ELSE 'small'`, then `END AS size`. Order 8 totals exactly 25.00, so check it says medium.
--- check result | Eight orders sized, order 8 (exactly 25) is medium
ordered
[[1, 22.5, "small"], [2, 80.1, "medium"], [3, 32.0, "medium"], [4, 58.0, "medium"], [5, 37.8, "medium"], [6, 107.0, "large"], [7, 45.0, "medium"], [8, 25.0, "medium"]]

+++ practice | Kinds of payment
--- task
For every row of `payments`, return three columns:

- `id` and `amount`
- `kind`: `'refund'` when the amount is below 0, `'large'` when it is 50 or more, and `'normal'` otherwise

Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, amount
FROM payments
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id, amount,
       CASE
         WHEN amount < 0   THEN 'refund'
         WHEN amount >= 50 THEN 'large'
         ELSE 'normal'
       END AS kind
FROM payments
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Add a third column: `CASE WHEN ... THEN ... WHEN ... THEN ... ELSE ... END AS kind`.
--- hint
The rules do not overlap here, so any order of the two `WHEN`s works: `amount < 0` gives `'refund'`, `amount >= 50` gives `'large'`, and `ELSE 'normal'`.
--- check result | Payments 2 and 6 are large, payment 8 (-45) is a refund, the rest normal
ordered
[[1, 22.5, "normal"], [2, 80.1, "large"], [3, 30.0, "normal"], [4, 28.0, "normal"], [5, 37.8, "normal"], [6, 107.0, "large"], [7, 45.0, "normal"], [8, -45.0, "refund"], [9, 25.0, "normal"]]
--- check source | Uses CASE
\b[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]\b
--- check source absent | Does not pick rows by id
[Ii][Dd]\s*(=|[Ii][Nn])\s*\(?\s*\d

+++ practice | Open orders first
--- task
Return every order's `id` and `status`, sorted so that `'paid'` orders come first, then `'shipped'` ones, then every other status. Within each of those three groups, sort by `id`.

Use a `CASE` inside the `ORDER BY`.
--- starter
SELECT id, status
FROM orders
ORDER BY status, id;
--- solution
SELECT id, status
FROM orders
ORDER BY CASE status WHEN 'paid' THEN 1 WHEN 'shipped' THEN 2 ELSE 3 END,
         id;
--- hint
Sorting by `status` itself is alphabetical: canceled, paid, refunded, shipped. Sort by a number you choose instead.
--- hint
`CASE status WHEN 'paid' THEN 1 WHEN 'shipped' THEN 2 ELSE 3 END` turns each status into 1, 2 or 3. Put it first in the `ORDER BY`, then `id`.
--- check result | Orders 5 and 8, then 1, 2, 4 and 6, then 3 and 7
ordered
[[5, "paid"], [8, "paid"], [1, "shipped"], [2, "shipped"], [4, "shipped"], [6, "shipped"], [3, "canceled"], [7, "refunded"]]
--- check source | Uses CASE in the ORDER BY
[Oo][Rr][Dd][Ee][Rr]\s+[Bb][Yy]\s+[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]
--- check source absent | Does not list the ids
[Ii][Dd]\s*[Ii][Nn]\s*\(

+++ practice | Revenue kept and revenue lost
--- task
Return one row per product `category`, with three columns:

- `category`
- `live_revenue`: the sum of `qty * unit_price` over items in orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`
- `lost_revenue`: the same sum over items in orders whose `status` is `'canceled'` or `'refunded'`

Round both to 2 decimals. A category with nothing lost shows `0.0`, not NULL. Sort by `category`.
--- starter
SELECT p.category, ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS live_revenue
FROM order_items oi
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.category
ORDER BY p.category;
--- solution
SELECT p.category,
       ROUND(SUM(CASE WHEN o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped') THEN oi.qty * oi.unit_price ELSE 0 END), 2) AS live_revenue,
       ROUND(SUM(CASE WHEN o.status IN ('canceled', 'refunded') THEN oi.qty * oi.unit_price ELSE 0 END), 2) AS lost_revenue
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o   ON o.id = oi.order_id
JOIN products p ON p.id = oi.product_id
GROUP BY p.category
ORDER BY p.category;
--- hint
Join `orders` to see each item's status. One `WHERE` cannot give both columns, because each column needs different rows.
--- hint
Put a `CASE` inside each `SUM`: the item's value when its status fits the column, and `0` when it does not. The `ELSE 0` is what makes an empty column 0 instead of NULL.
--- check result | electronics 201.6 and 0.0, home 72.0 and 45.0, stationery 56.8 and 32.0
ordered
[["electronics", 201.6, 0.0], ["home", 72.0, 45.0], ["stationery", 56.8, 32.0]]
--- check source | Puts CASE inside SUM
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(\s*[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]
--- check source absent | Does not type the totals
201\.6|56\.8|72\.0

+++ practice | The fuel gauge
--- schema
CREATE TABLE tanks (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  craft TEXT NOT NULL,
  fuel_pct REAL
);
INSERT INTO tanks (id, craft, fuel_pct) VALUES
  (1, 'Aster',  -2.0),
  (2, 'Boreas',  0.0),
  (3, 'Ceres',  24.9),
  (4, 'Delos',  25.0),
  (5, 'Eos',    99.99),
  (6, 'Fornax', 100.0),
  (7, 'Gaia',   103.5),
  (8, 'Hydra',  NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `tanks`, with `id`, `craft` and `fuel_pct`, the fuel left as a percentage. The gauge is not perfect: it can read a little below 0 or above 100, and it reads NULL when it is broken.

Return `craft`, `fuel_pct` and a `level` for every tank:

- `'unknown'` when `fuel_pct` is NULL
- `'empty'` at 0 or below
- `'low'` above 0 and under 25
- `'ok'` from 25 up to, but not including, 100
- `'full'` at 100 or more

Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT craft, fuel_pct,
       CASE
         WHEN fuel_pct < 25 THEN 'low'
         WHEN fuel_pct < 100 THEN 'ok'
         ELSE 'full'
       END AS level
FROM tanks
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT craft, fuel_pct,
       CASE
         WHEN fuel_pct IS NULL THEN 'unknown'
         WHEN fuel_pct <= 0    THEN 'empty'
         WHEN fuel_pct < 25    THEN 'low'
         WHEN fuel_pct < 100   THEN 'ok'
         ELSE 'full'
       END AS level
FROM tanks
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Walk every edge through the starter: -2, exactly 0, exactly 25, exactly 100, and NULL. Where does each one land?
--- hint
A NULL fails every comparison, so it falls through to the `ELSE` and reads `'full'`. Test for it first, with `IS NULL`.
--- hint
Then `WHEN fuel_pct <= 0 THEN 'empty'`, before the `'low'` test catches it.
--- check result | Aster and Boreas empty, Ceres low, Delos ok (exactly 25), Fornax full (exactly 100), Hydra unknown
ordered
[["Aster", -2.0, "empty"], ["Boreas", 0.0, "empty"], ["Ceres", 24.9, "low"], ["Delos", 25.0, "ok"], ["Eos", 99.99, "ok"], ["Fornax", 100.0, "full"], ["Gaia", 103.5, "full"], ["Hydra", null, "unknown"]]
--- check source | Tests for NULL with IS NULL
[Ii][Ss]\s+[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll]
--- check source absent | Does not pick tanks by name
'(Aster|Boreas|Ceres|Delos|Eos|Fornax|Gaia|Hydra)'

+++ practice | Everyone is new
--- task
This query should label **every** customer by their number of orders (any status): `'regular'` for 3 or more, `'returning'` for exactly 2, `'new'` for exactly 1, and `'none'` for 0. It returns `name`, `orders` and `kind`, sorted by name.

The counts are right, but every customer with an order is labelled `'new'`, even Ada with 3. Fix the `CASE`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS orders,
       CASE
         WHEN COUNT(o.id) >= 1 THEN 'new'
         WHEN COUNT(o.id) >= 2 THEN 'returning'
         WHEN COUNT(o.id) >= 3 THEN 'regular'
         ELSE 'none'
       END AS kind
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS orders,
       CASE
         WHEN COUNT(o.id) >= 3 THEN 'regular'
         WHEN COUNT(o.id) >= 2 THEN 'returning'
         WHEN COUNT(o.id) >= 1 THEN 'new'
         ELSE 'none'
       END AS kind
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
`CASE` stops at the first `WHEN` that is true. For Ada, 3 is `>= 1`, so the first line wins.
--- hint
Put the narrowest condition first: `>= 3`, then `>= 2`, then `>= 1`.
--- check result | Ada regular, Ben returning, Cleo, Dev and Eve new, Finn and Gus none
ordered
[["Ada", 3, "regular"], ["Ben", 2, "returning"], ["Cleo", 1, "new"], ["Dev", 1, "new"], ["Eve", 1, "new"], ["Finn", 0, "none"], ["Gus", 0, "none"]]
--- check source | Still labels with CASE
\b[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]\b
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Cleo|Dev|Eve|Finn|Gus)'

+++ practice | Pass status from the readings
--- schema
CREATE TABLE readings (
  pass_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  value REAL
);
INSERT INTO readings (pass_id, value) VALUES
  (1, 20), (1, 25), (1, 30),
  (2, 5), (2, 20), (2, 45), (2, NULL),
  (3, 41), (3, 42), (3, 9), (3, 50), (3, 22),
  (4, 10), (4, 40),
  (5, NULL), (5, NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `readings`, with `pass_id` (one pass of a satellite over a ground station) and `value`, which is NULL when a reading was lost. A reading is a **warning** when its value is below 10 or above 40. Exactly 10 and exactly 40 are safe.

Return one row per pass with four columns:

- `pass_id`
- `readings`: how many readings in the pass have a value
- `warnings`: how many of them are warnings
- `status`: `'nominal'` with no warnings, `'watch'` with 1 or 2, `'alert'` with 3 or more

A lost reading is never a warning. Sort by `pass_id`.
--- starter
SELECT pass_id, COUNT(*) AS readings
FROM readings
GROUP BY pass_id
ORDER BY pass_id;
--- solution
SELECT pass_id,
       COUNT(value) AS readings,
       SUM(CASE WHEN value < 10 OR value > 40 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS warnings,
       CASE
         WHEN SUM(CASE WHEN value < 10 OR value > 40 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) >= 3 THEN 'alert'
         WHEN SUM(CASE WHEN value < 10 OR value > 40 THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) >= 1 THEN 'watch'
         ELSE 'nominal'
       END AS status
FROM readings
GROUP BY pass_id
ORDER BY pass_id;
--- hint
Count the warnings with a `CASE` inside `SUM`: 1 for a warning, 0 otherwise. A NULL value fails both comparisons, so it lands in the `ELSE 0`.
--- hint
`readings` counts values, not rows: `COUNT(value)` skips the lost ones.
--- hint
The status is a second `CASE` that tests the same `SUM(...)`: `>= 3` first, then `>= 1`, then `ELSE 'nominal'`.
--- check result | Passes 1, 4 and 5 nominal, pass 2 watch (2 warnings), pass 3 alert (4 warnings)
ordered
[[1, 3, 0, "nominal"], [2, 3, 2, "watch"], [3, 5, 4, "alert"], [4, 2, 0, "nominal"], [5, 0, 0, "nominal"]]
--- check source | Counts with CASE inside SUM
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(\s*[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]
--- check source absent | Does not pick passes by id
pass_id\s*(=|[Ii][Nn])\s*\(?\s*\d

=== sql2-08 | Working with text
--- teach
Last lesson `CASE` turned numbers into labels. This lesson works on text itself. Real text is messy, with capitals, stray spaces and missing values, and a report on messy text quietly comes out wrong. Here you learn to clean text and cut pieces out of it.

### The picture

Picture a sign-up sheet where people wrote their own email addresses. One wrote in capitals. One left a space before it. One left it blank. Before you can count how many use each email provider, you tidy every line the same way.

### Text functions

A piece of text is called a **[[string|string-word]]**. A **function** is a named tool: you give it values in brackets and it gives one value back. You have used `COUNT`, `ROUND` and `substr` already. SQLite has a set of functions for strings. Here they are one at a time.

### lower and upper: change case

`lower(s)` gives the string `s` in small letters, and `upper(s)` in capitals. In SQLite they change [[only the plain letters A to Z|sqlite-ascii]]:

```sql
SELECT email, lower(email) FROM customers WHERE id = 2;
```

| email | lower(email) |
| --- | --- |
| BEN@Mail.com | ben@mail.com |

### trim: remove spaces at the ends

`trim(s)` removes spaces from both ends of a string. `ltrim` removes them from the left end only, and `rtrim` from the right end only:

```sql
SELECT trim('  hi  '), ltrim('  hi  '), rtrim('  hi  ');
```

This gives `'hi'`, `'hi  '` and `'  hi'`.

### length: count the characters

`length(s)` counts the characters in `s`. Spaces count. That makes it a good way to *see* [[invisible spaces|stray-spaces]]:

```sql
SELECT email, length(email), length(trim(email)) FROM customers WHERE id IN (4, 6);
```

| email | length(email) | length(trim(email)) |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  dev@work.io | 12 | 11 |
| finn@Work.io  | 13 | 12 |

Dev's email has a space at the front and Finn's has one at the end. You cannot see them in the table, but `length` can.

### substr: cut out a piece

You met `substr(s, start, n)` when you grouped by month. It gives the piece of `s` that begins at position `start` and is `n` characters long. Positions [[count from 1|count-from-one]]. Leave out `n` and you get everything to the end:

```sql
SELECT substr('2024-03-20', 6, 2), substr('ada@mail.com', 5);
```

This gives `'03'` and `'mail.com'`.

### instr: find where something is

`instr(s, t)` gives the position where `t` first appears in `s`, or 0 if it does not appear at all. `instr('ada@mail.com', '@')` is 4, because `@` is the fourth character.

### Putting them together

Functions can go inside each other. The innermost one runs first. Here is everything after the `@` in each email:

```sql
SELECT email, substr(email, instr(email, '@') + 1) AS domain FROM customers;
```

For `'ada@mail.com'`, `instr` finds the `@` at position 4. Adding 1 gives 5. `substr` from position 5 to the end gives `'mail.com'`. That part after the `@` is called the **domain**.

### Clean before you compare or group

To `GROUP BY`, `'Mail.com'`, `'mail.com'` and `'Work.io '` (with a space) are all different strings. Group the raw domains and you get six groups, where there should be three providers:

| domain | COUNT(*) |
| --- | --- |
| NULL | 1 |
| Mail.com | 1 |
| Work.io  | 1 |
| mail.com | 2 |
| post.org | 1 |
| work.io | 1 |

A report grouped on raw text quietly splits one bucket into several. **Normalize** first, which means making every value follow the same form: wrap it in `lower(trim(...))`.

### NULL goes in, NULL comes out

Look at that first group, NULL. Cleo has no email, and every text function given NULL [[gives back NULL|null-in-null-out]]. A missing email has no domain, so it makes a NULL group of its own. Filter it out with `WHERE email IS NOT NULL`.

**Watch out:** clean the value in the same expression you group by. Writing `lower(...)` in the `SELECT` but grouping on the raw `email` still makes the split groups. Here, giving the cleaned expression a name with `AS domain` and then writing `GROUP BY domain` keeps the two the same.

::: context string-word Why text is a "string"
Programmers call text a string because it is a string of characters, one after another, like beads on a thread. A character is one letter, digit, space or symbol. The word is used in nearly every programming language: Python's `str`, JavaScript's `String`. In SQL you write a string between single quotes, `'like this'`. The quotes are not part of the string; they only show where it starts and ends.
:::

::: context sqlite-ascii Only plain letters change case
In SQLite, `lower` and `upper` only change the 26 plain English letters, A to Z. Accented letters are left alone: `lower('ÉCOLE')` gives `'École'`. Other databases, like PostgreSQL, handle every alphabet. For email addresses this rarely matters. For people's names, like "Óscar" or "Zoë", it does, and it is worth knowing before you trust a cleaned column.
:::

::: context stray-spaces Where stray spaces come from
Most stray spaces arrive by copy and paste: someone copies an address from an email and picks up a space at the end. Phones add them too, after an autocomplete. People cannot see them, but a computer compares every character, so `'finn@work.io'` and `'finn@work.io '` are different. That is why careful apps trim and lower-case an email when the user signs up, before storing it. Cleaning at the door is cheaper than cleaning every report afterwards.
:::

::: context count-from-one Counting from 1
SQL counts positions in a string from 1: the first character is at position 1. Python and JavaScript count from 0, so the same first character is at index 0 there. Moving between languages, this is an easy slip that shifts every piece by one. In SQLite, `substr('ada', 1, 1)` is `'a'`. In Python, `'ada'[0]` is `'a'`.
:::

::: context null-in-null-out NULL passes through
NULL means "no value known". Ask for the lower-case version of an unknown value, and the honest answer is still unknown. So `lower(NULL)`, `trim(NULL)`, `length(NULL)` and `substr(NULL, 1, 3)` all give NULL. The same rule holds for arithmetic: `NULL + 1` is NULL. It spreads quietly through a long expression, so one missing value can blank out a whole column. The lesson Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear shows how to catch and replace it.
:::
--- task
Count customers per email **domain**, the part after the `@`. Return two columns:

- `domain`: the domain in lower case with stray spaces removed, using `lower(trim(...))`
- `customers`: how many customers have that domain

Skip customers with no email. Sort by `customers`, highest first, then by `domain`.
--- starter
SELECT substr(email, instr(email, '@') + 1) AS domain, COUNT(*) AS customers
FROM customers
GROUP BY domain
ORDER BY customers DESC, domain;
--- solution
SELECT lower(trim(substr(email, instr(email, '@') + 1))) AS domain,
       COUNT(*) AS customers
FROM customers
WHERE email IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY domain
ORDER BY customers DESC, domain;
--- hint
Run the starter. `Mail.com` and `mail.com` are counted apart, `Work.io ` has a space at the end, and Cleo's NULL email makes a group of its own.
--- hint
Wrap the whole `substr(...)` expression in `lower(trim(...))`, and keep `AS domain` after it.
--- hint
Add `WHERE email IS NOT NULL` between `FROM customers` and `GROUP BY domain`.
--- check result | mail.com 3, work.io 2, post.org 1
ordered
[["mail.com", 3], ["work.io", 2], ["post.org", 1]]

+++ practice | The name before the @
--- task
For every customer who has an email, return two columns:

- `name`
- `user`: the part of the email **before** the `@`, in lower case

Remove stray spaces from the email first, so Dev's leading space does not end up in `user`. Skip customers with no email, and sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT name, email AS user
FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name,
       lower(substr(trim(email), 1, instr(trim(email), '@') - 1)) AS user
FROM customers
WHERE email IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
`instr(trim(email), '@')` is where the `@` is. The part before it starts at position 1 and is one character shorter than that.
--- hint
`substr(trim(email), 1, instr(trim(email), '@') - 1)` cuts it out. Wrap it in `lower(...)`, and keep out the NULL email with `WHERE email IS NOT NULL`.
--- check result | ada, ben, dev, eve, finn and gus; no leading space on dev
ordered
[["Ada", "ada"], ["Ben", "ben"], ["Dev", "dev"], ["Eve", "eve"], ["Finn", "finn"], ["Gus", "gus"]]
--- check source | Finds the @ with instr
[Ii][Nn][Ss][Tt][Rr]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type any user name
'(ada|ben|dev|eve|finn|gus)'

+++ practice | Emails that need cleaning
--- task
Find the customers whose stored email is not already clean, meaning it is not equal to `lower(trim(email))`. Return four columns:

- `name` and `email`
- `clean`: the email lower-cased and trimmed
- `extra`: how many characters trimming removed, worked out with `length`

Customers with no email are not listed. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT name, email
FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name, email,
       lower(trim(email)) AS clean,
       length(email) - length(trim(email)) AS extra
FROM customers
WHERE email <> lower(trim(email))
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
Compare each email with its cleaned version: `WHERE email <> lower(trim(email))`. For Cleo that comparison is NULL, not true, so she is left out on her own.
--- hint
`extra` is the length before trimming minus the length after: `length(email) - length(trim(email))`.
--- check result | Ben (capitals, 0 extra), Dev (1) and Finn (1)
ordered
[["Ben", "BEN@Mail.com", "ben@mail.com", 0], ["Dev", " dev@work.io", "dev@work.io", 1], ["Finn", "finn@Work.io ", "finn@work.io", 1]]
--- check source | Measures with length
[Ll][Ee][Nn][Gg][Tt][Hh]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ben|Dev|Finn)'

+++ practice | Orders per email domain
--- task
Count orders by the email domain of the customer who placed them, over every order whatever its status. Return two columns:

- `domain`: the part after the `@`, lower-cased and trimmed with `lower(trim(...))`
- `orders`: how many orders came from customers with that domain

Leave out orders from customers with no email. Sort by `orders`, highest first, then by `domain`.
--- starter
SELECT c.email, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
GROUP BY c.email;
--- solution
SELECT lower(trim(substr(c.email, instr(c.email, '@') + 1))) AS domain,
       COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
WHERE c.email IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY domain
ORDER BY orders DESC, domain;
--- hint
Work the domain out of `c.email` the way the lesson did, `substr(c.email, instr(c.email, '@') + 1)`, and clean it with `lower(trim(...))`.
--- hint
Group by the cleaned domain, not the raw email, and drop Cleo's NULL email with `WHERE c.email IS NOT NULL`.
--- check result | mail.com 6 orders, work.io 1
ordered
[["mail.com", 6], ["work.io", 1]]
--- check source | Cleans with lower and trim
[Ll][Oo][Ww][Ee][Rr]\s*\(\s*[Tt][Rr][Ii][Mm]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a domain
'(mail\.com|work\.io)'

+++ practice | Part families from messy codes
--- schema
CREATE TABLE codes (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  code TEXT
);
INSERT INTO codes (id, code) VALUES
  (1, ' ab-12 '), (2, 'AB-7'), (3, 'ab-12'), (4, 'cd-3'), (5, 'CD'),
  (6, NULL), (7, ''), (8, '   '), (9, 'ef-');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `codes`, with `id` and `code`, part codes typed in by hand. A code's **family** is the part before the dash, in capitals, after trimming spaces from the code. A code with no dash at all, like `'CD'`, is its own family.

Return one row per family with two columns:

- `family`
- `codes`: how many codes belong to it

Skip codes that are NULL or blank (empty, or only spaces). Sort by `family`.
--- starter
SELECT upper(substr(code, 1, instr(code, '-') - 1)) AS family, COUNT(*) AS codes
FROM codes
GROUP BY family
ORDER BY family;
--- solution
SELECT CASE
         WHEN instr(trim(code), '-') = 0 THEN upper(trim(code))
         ELSE upper(substr(trim(code), 1, instr(trim(code), '-') - 1))
       END AS family,
       COUNT(*) AS codes
FROM codes
WHERE trim(code) <> ''
GROUP BY family
ORDER BY family;
--- hint
Trim first, everywhere: `' ab-12 '` has a space in front, so its dash is one place later than you think.
--- hint
When there is no dash, `instr` gives 0 and the `substr` comes out empty. Use a `CASE` to pick the whole trimmed code in that case.
--- hint
`WHERE trim(code) <> ''` drops the empty and all-space codes, and the NULL too, because `NULL <> ''` is not true.
--- check result | AB 3, CD 2 (CD has no dash), EF 1; nothing blank
ordered
[["AB", 3], ["CD", 2], ["EF", 1]]
--- check source | Handles the no-dash case with CASE
\b[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]\b
--- check source | Trims the code
[Tt][Rr][Ii][Mm]\s*\(

+++ practice | Codes that are one letter short
--- task
This query should give every product a `code`: the first 3 characters of its name, in capitals, like `'NOT'` for Notebook. It returns `name` and `code`, sorted by name. The codes come out with only 2 letters.

Find the off-by-one mistake and fix it.
--- starter
SELECT name, upper(substr(name, 0, 3)) AS code
FROM products
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name, upper(substr(name, 1, 3)) AS code
FROM products
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
Where does SQL start counting positions in a string: at 0, like Python, or at 1?
--- hint
The first character is at position 1. `substr(name, 1, 3)` gives the first three.
--- check result | BOO, DES, FOU, HEA, MUG, NOT, PLA, USB
ordered
[["Bookends", "BOO"], ["Desk lamp", "DES"], ["Fountain pen", "FOU"], ["Headphones", "HEA"], ["Mug", "MUG"], ["Notebook", "NOT"], ["Planner", "PLA"], ["USB cable", "USB"]]
--- check source | Starts at position 1
[Ss][Uu][Bb][Ss][Tt][Rr]\s*\(\s*name\s*,\s*1\s*,\s*3\s*\)
--- check source absent | Does not type the codes
'(BOO|DES|FOU|HEA|MUG|NOT|PLA|USB)'

+++ practice | Errors per subsystem
--- schema
CREATE TABLE logs (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  line TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO logs (id, line) VALUES
  (1, 'ERROR [nav] star tracker lost lock'),
  (2, 'error [NAV] retry failed'),
  (3, 'WARN [power] bus voltage low'),
  (4, 'ERROR [power] battery heater off'),
  (5, 'INFO [nav] lock regained'),
  (6, 'ERROR [comms] no carrier'),
  (7, 'ERROR [ Comms ] packet loss'),
  (8, 'ERROR no subsystem given');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `logs`, with `id` and `line`, one line of a flight computer's log. A line starts with its level (`ERROR`, `WARN` or `INFO`, typed in any case), and the subsystem that wrote it is between square brackets.

Count the **error** lines per subsystem. Return two columns:

- `subsystem`: the text between `[` and `]`, trimmed and in lower case, so `'[ Comms ]'` and `'[comms]'` count together
- `errors`: how many error lines it wrote

A line is an error line when its first 5 characters are `ERROR` in any case. Skip lines with no `[`. Sort by `errors`, highest first, then by `subsystem`.
--- starter
SELECT substr(line, instr(line, '[')) AS subsystem, COUNT(*) AS errors
FROM logs
GROUP BY subsystem;
--- solution
SELECT lower(trim(substr(line, instr(line, '[') + 1, instr(line, ']') - instr(line, '[') - 1))) AS subsystem,
       COUNT(*) AS errors
FROM logs
WHERE upper(substr(line, 1, 5)) = 'ERROR'
  AND instr(line, '[') > 0
GROUP BY subsystem
ORDER BY errors DESC, subsystem;
--- hint
The subsystem starts one character after the `[`. Its length is the distance from `[` to `]`, minus one: `instr(line, ']') - instr(line, '[') - 1`.
--- hint
Clean the piece with `lower(trim(...))`. Pick the error lines with `upper(substr(line, 1, 5)) = 'ERROR'`.
--- hint
`instr(line, '[') > 0` keeps only lines that have a `[` at all.
--- check result | comms 2, nav 2, power 1
ordered
[["comms", 2], ["nav", 2], ["power", 1]]
--- check source | Finds the brackets with instr
[Ii][Nn][Ss][Tt][Rr]\s*\(\s*line\s*,\s*'\]'
--- check source absent | Does not type a subsystem
'(nav|comms|power)'

=== sql2-08b | Building text: joining, replacing and LIKE
--- teach
Last lesson you cleaned text and cut pieces out of it. This lesson goes the other way: gluing strings together and swapping one piece for another. It ends with how SQLite compares text, where `=` and `LIKE` do not agree about capitals.

### The picture

Picture filling in name badges: "Hello, my name is ____". The words on the badge are the same every time. Only the name changes. SQL can build text the same way, row by row, from fixed words and column values.

### || joins strings

Two bars, `||`, join two strings into one. Joining strings end to end is called **[[concatenation|concat-word]]**:

```sql
SELECT name || ' from ' || city AS badge FROM customers;
```

| badge |
| --- |
| Ada from London |
| Ben from Paris |
| NULL |
| Dev from London |

Notice the spaces inside `' from '`. `||` adds nothing of its own, so without them you would get `AdafromLondon`.

The third row is Cleo, who has no city. A NULL anywhere in `||` [[makes the whole result NULL|concat-null]], the same as it did with the text functions.

### replace swaps pieces

`replace(s, a, b)` gives `s` with every `a` replaced by `b`:

```sql
SELECT replace('2024-03-20', '-', '/');
```

This gives `'2024/03/20'`: both dashes changed. If `a` does not appear, the string comes back unchanged.

### Functions and || together

You can mix them. The cleaned email of each customer, in angle brackets after the name, the way email programs show it:

```sql
SELECT name || ' <' || lower(trim(email)) || '>' AS sender
FROM customers WHERE email IS NOT NULL;
```

Ben's row becomes `Ben <ben@mail.com>`.

### = and LIKE disagree about capitals

`=` compares strings exactly, character by character. Capitals count:

```sql
SELECT name FROM customers WHERE email = 'BEN@MAIL.COM';
```

This finds nobody, because Ben's email is stored as `'BEN@Mail.com'`.

`LIKE` compares with a pattern. You met it in the basics, where `%` means "any run of characters". In SQLite, `LIKE` also [[ignores case for plain letters|like-case]]:

```sql
SELECT name FROM customers WHERE email LIKE 'BEN@MAIL.COM';
```

This finds Ben. With a `%`, `WHERE email LIKE '%@work.io%'` finds Dev and Finn, even with Finn's capital W and trailing space.

So the two can give different answers on the same data. If you mean "equal, ignoring case", say so: `lower(email) = 'ben@mail.com'` works in every database.

**Watch out:** one NULL in a chain of `||` blanks the whole result, with no error. Before you build text from a column, ask whether that column can be NULL. Filter those rows out with `IS NOT NULL`, or give the NULL a stand-in value, which the lesson Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear shows with `COALESCE`.

In the task you put `||` and `replace` together to write email addresses in a form [[that hides them from spam robots|hide-from-bots]].

::: context concat-word What "concatenation" means
The word comes from Latin: *con*, "together", and *catena*, "chain". Concatenating is chaining strings together, end to end. Many databases also have a function for it, `concat(a, b, ...)`, and SQLite added one in version 3.44 (2023). The two differ on NULL: `concat` skips a NULL as if it were empty text, while `||` makes the whole result NULL. The bars `||` are the SQL standard's way, so you will see them everywhere.
:::

::: context concat-null How one NULL blanks the badge
`||` works through the chain from left to right. Once a NULL joins in, every later step stays NULL.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="20" width="70" height="28" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="45" y="38" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">'Cleo'</text>
  <text x="95" y="38" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">||</text>
  <rect x="110" y="20" width="80" height="28" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <text x="150" y="38" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">' from '</text>
  <text x="205" y="38" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">||</text>
  <rect x="220" y="20" width="70" height="28" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="255" y="38" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">NULL</text>
  <line x1="150" y1="52" x2="150" y2="80" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <polygon points="150,86 145,78 155,78" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="100" y="88" width="100" height="26" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="150" y="105" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">NULL</text>
  <text x="215" y="105" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">the whole badge is lost</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context like-case Why LIKE ignores case
SQLite's `LIKE` ignores the difference between capital and small letters, but only for the 26 plain English letters. So `'x' LIKE 'X'` is true, while `'é' LIKE 'É'` is false. Other databases differ: in PostgreSQL, `LIKE` is case-sensitive, and it has a separate `ILIKE` for ignoring case. Because the behavior depends on the database, `lower(...)` on both sides is the safe habit. One more pattern symbol: `_` in a `LIKE` pattern means "exactly one character", so `'abc' LIKE 'a_c'` is true.
:::

::: context hide-from-bots A real use for replace
Web pages sometimes write email addresses as `ada at mail.com` instead of `ada@mail.com`. Programs that crawl the web collecting addresses for spam look for the `@`, so swapping it for `' at '` hides the address from the simplest of them, while a person can still read it. It is a small defense, not a strong one, but it is a neat example of `replace`, and it is exactly what the task builds.
:::
--- task
Build a contact line for every customer who has an email. Return two columns:

- `name`
- `contact`: the name, then `': '`, then the email cleaned with `lower(trim(...))` and with every `'@'` replaced by `' at '`

For Ada that is `Ada: ada at mail.com`. Skip customers with no email, and sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT name, email AS contact
FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name,
       name || ': ' || replace(lower(trim(email)), '@', ' at ') AS contact
FROM customers
WHERE email IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
Start with the email part on its own: `replace(lower(trim(email)), '@', ' at ')`. Try it in the `SELECT` and check Ben's row.
--- hint
Put the name and `': '` in front with `||`: `name || ': ' || replace(...)`, and name the column `AS contact`.
--- hint
Cleo has no email, so add `WHERE email IS NOT NULL` before `ORDER BY name`.
--- check result | Six contact lines, cleaned, with at instead of @
ordered
[["Ada", "Ada: ada at mail.com"], ["Ben", "Ben: ben at mail.com"], ["Dev", "Dev: dev at work.io"], ["Eve", "Eve: eve at mail.com"], ["Finn", "Finn: finn at work.io"], ["Gus", "Gus: gus at post.org"]]
--- check source | Joins text with ||
\|\|
--- check source | Uses replace
[Rr][Ee][Pp][Ll][Aa][Cc][Ee]\s*\(

+++ practice | Shelf labels
--- task
Return one column, `label`, for every product: its name, a space, and its category in round brackets, like `Desk lamp (home)`. Sort by the product's name.
--- starter
SELECT name, category
FROM products
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name || ' (' || category || ')' AS label
FROM products
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
Glue the pieces together with `||`: the name, then `' ('`, then the category, then `')'`.
--- hint
`||` adds nothing of its own, so the space goes inside the quotes: `' ('`.
--- check result | Eight labels, from Bookends (home) to USB cable (electronics)
ordered
[["Bookends (home)"], ["Desk lamp (home)"], ["Fountain pen (stationery)"], ["Headphones (electronics)"], ["Mug (home)"], ["Notebook (stationery)"], ["Planner (stationery)"], ["USB cable (electronics)"]]
--- check source | Joins text with ||
\|\|
--- check source absent | Does not type a label
'[A-Z][a-z]+ \(

+++ practice | Dates written the long way
--- task
For every order, return two columns:

- `id`
- `stamp`: the date of `placed` with slashes instead of dashes, then `' at '`, then the hours and minutes, like `2024/03/02 at 10:15`

Use `replace` for the slashes and `substr` for the pieces. Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, placed AS stamp
FROM orders
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id,
       replace(substr(placed, 1, 10), '-', '/') || ' at ' || substr(placed, 12, 5) AS stamp
FROM orders
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
The date is the first 10 characters, `substr(placed, 1, 10)`. The time `HH:MM` starts at position 12 and is 5 characters long.
--- hint
Swap the dashes with `replace(..., '-', '/')`, then glue on `' at '` and the time with `||`.
--- check result | Eight stamps, from 2024/03/02 at 10:15 to 2024/06/01 at 08:30
ordered
[[1, "2024/03/02 at 10:15"], [2, "2024/03/20 at 18:40"], [3, "2024/03/21 at 09:05"], [4, "2024/04/07 at 14:30"], [5, "2024/04/07 at 20:10"], [6, "2024/05/15 at 11:00"], [7, "2024/05/28 at 16:45"], [8, "2024/06/01 at 08:30"]]
--- check source | Uses replace
[Rr][Ee][Pp][Ll][Aa][Cc][Ee]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a date
'2024

+++ practice | One line per customer
--- task
For **every** customer, return one column, `summary`, like `Ada: 3 orders`. Count every order, whatever its status. Write `order` when the count is exactly 1 (`Cleo: 1 order`) and `orders` otherwise, including 0 (`Finn: 0 orders`). Sort by the customer's name.
--- starter
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name || ': ' || COUNT(o.id) ||
       CASE WHEN COUNT(o.id) = 1 THEN ' order' ELSE ' orders' END AS summary
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Count each customer's orders with a `LEFT JOIN` and `COUNT(o.id)`, so Finn and Gus get 0.
--- hint
`||` can glue a number onto text. For the last word, glue on a `CASE`: `' order'` when the count is 1, `' orders'` otherwise.
--- check result | Ada: 3 orders, Ben: 2 orders, Cleo: 1 order … Gus: 0 orders
ordered
[["Ada: 3 orders"], ["Ben: 2 orders"], ["Cleo: 1 order"], ["Dev: 1 order"], ["Eve: 1 order"], ["Finn: 0 orders"], ["Gus: 0 orders"]]
--- check source | Joins text with ||
\|\|
--- check source | Chooses the word with CASE
\b[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]\b

+++ practice | Badges for customers with no city
--- task
For every customer, return one column, `badge`: the name, then a space and the city in round brackets, like `Ada (London)`. A customer with no city gets just their name, like `Cleo`, never NULL. Sort by the customer's name.
--- starter
SELECT name || ' (' || city || ')' AS badge
FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name || COALESCE(' (' || city || ')', '') AS badge
FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Cleo's and Finn's badges are NULL, because one NULL in a chain of `||` blanks the whole result.
--- hint
The bracket part, `' (' || city || ')'`, is NULL exactly when `city` is. `COALESCE(that, '')` turns it into empty text, and then the name stands alone.
--- check result | Seven badges; Cleo and Finn are just their names
ordered
[["Ada (London)"], ["Ben (Paris)"], ["Cleo"], ["Dev (London)"], ["Eve (Berlin)"], ["Finn"], ["Gus (Paris)"]]
--- check source | Handles the NULL city
[Cc][Oo][Aa][Ll][Ee][Ss][Cc][Ee]\s*\(|\b[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]\b|[Ii][Ff][Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Cleo|Dev|Eve|Finn|Gus)'

+++ practice | Labels that came out as zero
--- task
This query should return one column, `label`, holding each product's name, then `' - '`, then its category, like `Mug - home`, sorted by label. Every label comes out as `0`.

The person who wrote it came from another programming language. Fix the way the text is joined.
--- starter
SELECT name + ' - ' + category AS label
FROM products
ORDER BY label;
--- solution
SELECT name || ' - ' || category AS label
FROM products
ORDER BY label;
--- hint
In SQL, `+` is only for numbers. Given text, SQLite turns each piece into a number, and text that does not start with digits becomes 0.
--- hint
Strings are joined with two bars, `||`.
--- check result | Bookends - home, Desk lamp - home … USB cable - electronics

+++ practice | Titles that make the same web address
--- schema
CREATE TABLE articles (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  title TEXT
);
INSERT INTO articles (id, title) VALUES
  (1, 'Launch Day'),
  (2, ' launch day '),
  (3, 'Launch  Day'),
  (4, 'Orbit Insertion'),
  (5, 'orbit insertion'),
  (6, 'Docking'),
  (7, 'Draft: Docking'),
  (8, 'draft: docking'),
  (9, NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `articles`, with `id` and `title`. Each article's web address ends in a **slug**: the title trimmed, in lower case, with every space replaced by a dash. `' launch day '` becomes `launch-day`.

Two articles with the same slug would fight over one address. Find every slug used by more than one article, with two columns:

- `slug`
- `articles`: how many articles share it

Drafts do not get an address yet: skip titles that start with `draft:`, in any case. Skip NULL titles too. Sort by `slug`.
--- starter
SELECT title, COUNT(*) AS articles
FROM articles
GROUP BY title;
--- solution
SELECT lower(replace(trim(title), ' ', '-')) AS slug, COUNT(*) AS articles
FROM articles
WHERE title NOT LIKE 'draft:%'
GROUP BY slug
HAVING COUNT(*) > 1
ORDER BY slug;
--- hint
Build the slug with three functions inside each other: `trim`, then `replace(..., ' ', '-')`, then `lower`.
--- hint
Group by the slug and keep the groups with `HAVING COUNT(*) > 1`. `'Launch  Day'`, with two spaces, becomes `launch--day`, a different slug.
--- hint
In SQLite, `LIKE` ignores case, so `title NOT LIKE 'draft:%'` drops both drafts. It also drops the NULL title, because `NULL NOT LIKE ...` is not true.
--- check result | launch-day 2 and orbit-insertion 2; the drafts and launch--day are not listed
ordered
[["launch-day", 2], ["orbit-insertion", 2]]
--- check source | Uses replace
[Rr][Ee][Pp][Ll][Aa][Cc][Ee]\s*\(
--- check source | Keeps the shared slugs with HAVING
[Hh][Aa][Vv][Ii][Nn][Gg]

=== sql2-09 | Dates and times
--- teach
Last lesson, in Working with text, you cleaned up messy email addresses with `lower`, `trim` and `substr`. Now you meet the other kind of value almost every table has: dates and times. By the end of this lesson you will build a monthly sales report, with one row per month.

Think of a pile of forms, each dated in the corner. If everyone writes the year first, then the month, then the day, always with two digits (2024-03-20), you can sort the pile alphabetically and it comes out in time order too.

**Step 1: dates are stored as text.** SQLite has no separate date type. A date is stored as text in **[[ISO format|iso-8601]]**: the year, the month and the day, biggest part first, joined by dashes. A time can follow after a space, as hours:minutes:seconds on a 24-hour clock, so `18:40:00` is twenty to seven in the evening. Look at the `placed` column of `orders`:

```sql
SELECT id, placed FROM orders;
```

| id | placed |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | 2024-03-02 10:15:00 |
| 2 | 2024-03-20 18:40:00 |
| 3 | 2024-03-21 09:05:00 |
| … | … |

Because the biggest part comes first and every part has a [[fixed width|fixed-width]], sorting this text A to Z also sorts it oldest to newest. `ORDER BY placed` works with no extra help.

**Step 2: `strftime` pulls out pieces.** `strftime(format, time)` (say it "[[string-format-time|strftime-name]]") takes a time and writes it out in the shape you ask for. The format is text with **placeholders**: a `%` sign followed by a letter, each standing for one piece of the date.

| Placeholder | Means | For `'2024-03-20 18:40:00'` |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `%Y` | the year, 4 digits | 2024 |
| `%m` | the month, 2 digits | 03 |
| `%d` | the day of the month | 20 |
| `%H` | the hour, 00 to 23 | 18 |
| `%W` | the week of the year | 12 |
| `%w` | the weekday, 0 is Sunday | 3 (a Wednesday) |

Anything in the format that is not a placeholder is copied as it is, like the dash here. (Everything after `--` on a line is a **comment**: a note for people, which the database ignores. Here it shows the answer.)

```sql
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', '2024-03-20 18:40:00');   -- '2024-03'
```

**Step 3: group by the month.** For a monthly report, give `GROUP BY` that expression. Every timestamp in March turns into the same text, `'2024-03'`, so all of March lands in one bucket. It is the same trick as grouping by `substr(placed, 1, 7)` in GROUP BY in depth. SQLite lets you group by [[the name you gave with AS|group-by-alias]]:

```sql
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', placed) AS month, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY month;
```

| month | orders |
| --- | --- |
| 2024-03 | 3 |
| 2024-04 | 2 |
| 2024-05 | 2 |
| 2024-06 | 1 |

**Step 4: the join trap comes back.** Money lives in `order_items`, one row per item. Once you join `orders` to `order_items`, there is one row per *item*, so `COUNT(*)` counts items, not orders. `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)` counts the *different* order ids instead. In March, order 1 has two items, so:

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*) AS item_rows, COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) = '2024-03';
```

| item_rows | orders |
| --- | --- |
| 4 | 3 |

This is how engineers build any [[report by time|time-buckets]]: turn each timestamp into its bucket, group, and count the right thing.

**Watch out:** grouping by `placed` itself makes one group per exact second, so almost every order sits alone in its own group. Group by `strftime('%Y-%m', placed)` to get months.

::: context iso-8601 A worldwide way to write dates
ISO format is short for ISO 8601, an international standard for writing dates and times, first published in 1988. It fixes the order as year, month, day, from biggest unit to smallest, so 2024-03-02 can only mean 2 March 2024. That matters because 03/02/2024 means 2 March in Britain and 3 February in the United States. Flight software, mission logs and the web all use ISO 8601 for this reason: when a timestamp crosses from one team, country or program to another, nobody has to guess.
:::

::: context fixed-width Why the zeros matter
Text is sorted one character at a time, from the left, the way a dictionary is. That only matches time order if every date has the same shape. Write September as `9` instead of `09` and it goes wrong: `'2024-10-01'` sorts before `'2024-9-30'`, because at the sixth character `1` comes before `9`. You can check it: `SELECT '2024-10-01' < '2024-9-30';` returns 1, meaning true. The leading zeros in `2024-09-30` keep every part the same width, so the comparison is always fair.
:::

::: context strftime-name An old name that stuck
`strftime` is short for "string format time". The name, and the `%` codes, come from the C programming language's standard library, where a function called `strftime` has formatted dates since the 1980s. Many languages copied it, so the same codes work far beyond SQLite: Python's `datetime` objects have a `strftime` method that understands `%Y`, `%m` and `%d` in the same way. Learn the codes once and you can read date formats in a lot of code.
:::

::: context group-by-alias Naming a group once
In SQLite you can write `GROUP BY month` after `AS month` in the `SELECT`, instead of repeating the whole `strftime('%Y-%m', placed)`. PostgreSQL and MySQL allow this too. Some databases do not: Microsoft SQL Server wants the full expression repeated in `GROUP BY`. If a query that works in SQLite fails somewhere else with "invalid column name", this is a likely reason. Repeating the expression always works everywhere.
:::

::: context time-buckets Buckets of time
A spacecraft's computer might log a temperature reading every second. Nobody reads millions of rows one by one, so engineers group them into buckets (per minute, per hour, per orbit) and look at the count, average or maximum in each bucket. The pattern is the one in this lesson: turn each timestamp into its bucket, then group. `strftime('%Y-%m-%d %H', t)` makes hourly buckets.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="180" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">8 order timestamps, grouped by month</text>
  <circle cx="45" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="60" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="75" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="135" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="150" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="220" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="235" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="312" cy="45" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="20" y="62" width="75" height="44" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="105" y="62" width="75" height="44" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="190" y="62" width="75" height="44" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="275" y="62" width="75" height="44" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="57" y="81" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2024-03</text>
  <text x="142" y="81" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2024-04</text>
  <text x="227" y="81" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2024-05</text>
  <text x="312" y="81" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2024-06</text>
  <text x="57" y="99" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">3</text>
  <text x="142" y="99" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <text x="227" y="99" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">2</text>
  <text x="312" y="99" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">1</text>
  <text x="180" y="132" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">one bucket per strftime('%Y-%m', placed)</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Build a monthly sales report over the orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'` (`o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')` keeps a row whose status is either one). Join `orders` to `order_items` and return three columns:

- `month`: the month the order was placed, as `'YYYY-MM'`;
- `orders`: the number of different orders in that month;
- `revenue`: the sum of `qty * unit_price`, rounded to 2 decimals with `ROUND(…, 2)`.

Sort by month.
--- starter
SELECT placed AS month, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY placed;
--- solution
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders,
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS revenue
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
Start with the month: `strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month`, and `GROUP BY month` instead of `GROUP BY placed`.
--- hint
Revenue needs `JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id`. After that join there is one row per item, so count orders with `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)`, not `COUNT(*)`.
--- hint
The shape is: `SELECT month, COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders, ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS revenue FROM orders o JOIN order_items oi … WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped') GROUP BY month ORDER BY month`, with the `strftime` expression in place of the first `month`.
--- check result | Four months, with order counts and revenue
ordered
[["2024-03", 2, 102.6], ["2024-04", 2, 95.8], ["2024-05", 1, 107.0], ["2024-06", 1, 25.0]]

+++ practice | Orders by day of the week
--- task
Count every order, whatever its status, by the day of the week it was placed. Return two columns:

- `weekday`: `strftime('%w', placed)`, the day as a digit from `'0'` (Sunday) to `'6'` (Saturday)
- `orders`: how many orders were placed on that day of the week

Days with no orders do not appear. Sort by `weekday`.
--- starter
SELECT placed, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY placed;
--- solution
SELECT strftime('%w', placed) AS weekday, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders
GROUP BY weekday
ORDER BY weekday;
--- hint
`%w` is the placeholder for the day of the week. Every Sunday, in any month, turns into the same text, `'0'`.
--- hint
Select `strftime('%w', placed) AS weekday`, group by it, and count.
--- check result | Sunday 2, Tuesday 1, Wednesday 2, Thursday 1, Saturday 2
ordered
[["0", 2], ["2", 1], ["3", 2], ["4", 1], ["6", 2]]
--- check source | Uses strftime with %w
[Ss][Tt][Rr][Ff][Tt][Ii][Mm][Ee]\s*\(\s*'%w'
--- check source absent | Does not type the digits
'[0-6]'

+++ practice | Orders before noon
--- task
Find every order placed before 12:00 on a 24-hour clock, on any day. Return three columns:

- `id`
- `hour`: the hour it was placed, as two digits like `'09'`, with `strftime('%H', ...)`
- `status`

Sort by `hour`, then by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, placed, status
FROM orders
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id, strftime('%H', placed) AS hour, status
FROM orders
WHERE strftime('%H', placed) < '12'
ORDER BY hour, id;
--- hint
`strftime('%H', placed)` gives the hour as text, always two digits, from `'00'` to `'23'`.
--- hint
Because the hour always has two digits, comparing text works: `WHERE strftime('%H', placed) < '12'` keeps the morning.
--- check result | Orders 8 (08), 3 (09), 1 (10) and 6 (11)
ordered
[[8, "08", "paid"], [3, "09", "canceled"], [1, "10", "shipped"], [6, "11", "shipped"]]
--- check source | Uses strftime with %H
[Ss][Tt][Rr][Ff][Tt][Ii][Mm][Ee]\s*\(\s*'%H'
--- check source absent | Does not list the ids
[Ii][Dd]\s*[Ii][Nn]\s*\(

+++ practice | Money in, month by month
--- task
Join `payments` to `orders` and group the payments by the month their **order** was placed. Return three columns:

- `month`: the month as `'YYYY-MM'`, with `strftime`
- `payments`: how many payments there were
- `net`: the sum of `amount`, refunds included, rounded to 2 decimals

Sort by `month`.
--- starter
SELECT order_id, amount
FROM payments;
--- solution
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
       COUNT(*) AS payments,
       ROUND(SUM(p.amount), 2) AS net
FROM payments p
JOIN orders o ON o.id = p.order_id
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
The date lives on the order, so join: `FROM payments p JOIN orders o ON o.id = p.order_id`.
--- hint
Each row is one payment, so `COUNT(*)` counts payments. Group by `strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed)`.
--- check result | March 2 payments and 102.6, April 3 and 95.8, May 3 and 107.0 (the refund cancels out), June 1 and 25.0
ordered
[["2024-03", 2, 102.6], ["2024-04", 3, 95.8], ["2024-05", 3, 107.0], ["2024-06", 1, 25.0]]
--- check source | Uses strftime
[Ss][Tt][Rr][Ff][Tt][Ii][Mm][Ee]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a month
'2024-0\d'

+++ practice | Two Decembers
--- schema
CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  at TEXT
);
INSERT INTO events (id, at) VALUES
  (1, '2023-12-05 10:00:00'),
  (2, '2023-12-31 23:59:59'),
  (3, '2024-01-01 00:00:00'),
  (4, '2024-02-29 12:00:00'),
  (5, '2024-12-24 08:00:00'),
  (6, '2024-12-31 12:00:00'),
  (7, NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `events`, with `id` and `at`, a timestamp. One event has lost its timestamp, so its `at` is NULL.

Count the events per month. Return two columns:

- `month`: the year and month, like `'2023-12'`
- `events`: how many events happened that month

December 2023 and December 2024 are different months. Leave out the event with no timestamp. Sort by `month`.
--- starter
SELECT strftime('%m', at) AS month, COUNT(*) AS events
FROM events
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- solution
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', at) AS month, COUNT(*) AS events
FROM events
WHERE at IS NOT NULL
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
Run the starter: both Decembers land in one group, `'12'`, because the month number alone forgets the year.
--- hint
Put the year in the format: `'%Y-%m'`. Then drop the NULL group with `WHERE at IS NOT NULL`.
--- check result | 2023-12 2, 2024-01 1, 2024-02 1, 2024-12 2; no NULL month
ordered
[["2023-12", 2], ["2024-01", 1], ["2024-02", 1], ["2024-12", 2]]
--- check source | Keeps the year in the month
'%Y-%m'
--- check source | Drops the missing timestamp
[Ii][Ss]\s+[Nn][Oo][Tt]\s+[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll]

+++ practice | Months that look like minutes
--- task
This report should show, for each month, how many **different** orders were placed and how many units (the sum of `qty`) they held, over every order whatever its status. It returns `month`, `orders` and `units`, sorted by month. There should be four months, 2024-03 to 2024-06, but it shows months like `2024-40` and `2024-15`.

Find the wrong placeholder and fix it.
--- starter
SELECT strftime('%Y-%M', o.placed) AS month,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders,
       SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- solution
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders,
       SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- hint
`2024-40` came from the order placed at 18:40. Which piece of the time is 40?
--- hint
Placeholders care about capitals: `%M` is the minute, `%m` is the month.
--- check result | 2024-03 3 orders and 6 units, 2024-04 2 and 6, 2024-05 2 and 4, 2024-06 1 and 4
ordered
[["2024-03", 3, 6], ["2024-04", 2, 6], ["2024-05", 2, 4], ["2024-06", 1, 4]]
--- check source | Uses %m for the month
'%Y-%m'
--- check source absent | No %M left

+++ practice | One day of telemetry, hour by hour
--- schema
CREATE TABLE telemetry (
  craft TEXT NOT NULL,
  at TEXT NOT NULL,
  temp REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO telemetry (craft, at, temp) VALUES
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01 10:05:00', 20.0),
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01T10:35:00', 21.0),
  ('Vega', '2024-05-01 11:15:00', 22.5),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-01 10:50:00', 15.0),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-01 10:59:59', 15.4),
  ('Rho',  '2024-05-01T11:00:00', 16.0),
  ('Rho',  '2024-04-30 23:30:00', 14.0),
  ('Vega', '2024-05-02 10:05:00', 19.0);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `telemetry`, with `craft`, `at` and `temp`. The readings came from two ground stations. One writes timestamps with a space, `'2024-05-01 10:05:00'`; the other writes a `T` instead of the space, `'2024-05-01T10:35:00'`. Both are ISO format, and `strftime` reads both.

Using only the readings from 1 May 2024, return one row per craft **and** hour, with five columns:

- `craft`
- `hour`: `strftime('%Y-%m-%d %H', at)`, like `'2024-05-01 10'`
- `readings`: how many readings fall in that hour
- `avg_temp`: their average `temp`, rounded to 1 decimal
- `max_temp`: their highest `temp`

Sort by `craft`, then `hour`.
--- starter
SELECT craft, substr(at, 1, 13) AS hour, COUNT(*) AS readings
FROM telemetry
GROUP BY craft, hour
ORDER BY craft, hour;
--- solution
SELECT craft,
       strftime('%Y-%m-%d %H', at) AS hour,
       COUNT(*) AS readings,
       ROUND(AVG(temp), 1) AS avg_temp,
       MAX(temp) AS max_temp
FROM telemetry
WHERE strftime('%Y-%m-%d', at) = '2024-05-01'
GROUP BY craft, hour
ORDER BY craft, hour;
--- hint
Run the starter: `substr` copies the `T`, so `'2024-05-01T10'` and `'2024-05-01 10'` become two groups. `strftime` writes every hour the same way.
--- hint
Keep one day with `WHERE strftime('%Y-%m-%d', at) = '2024-05-01'`, which reads both kinds of timestamp.
--- hint
Group by `craft, hour`, then add `ROUND(AVG(temp), 1)` and `MAX(temp)`.
--- check result | Rho 10:00 (2 readings, 15.2) and 11:00, Vega 10:00 (2 readings, 20.5) and 11:00
ordered
[["Rho", "2024-05-01 10", 2, 15.2, 15.4], ["Rho", "2024-05-01 11", 1, 16.0, 16.0], ["Vega", "2024-05-01 10", 2, 20.5, 21.0], ["Vega", "2024-05-01 11", 1, 22.5, 22.5]]
--- check source | Buckets with strftime
[Ss][Tt][Rr][Ff][Tt][Ii][Mm][Ee]\s*\(\s*'%Y-%m-%d %H'
--- check source | Groups by the craft too
[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy][^;]*craft

=== sql2-09b | Date arithmetic and date ranges
--- teach
Last lesson you pulled the month out of a timestamp with `strftime` and grouped orders by it. This lesson does arithmetic with dates: moving a date forward or back, counting the days between two dates, and picking out every row inside a range of dates.

Think of a wall calendar. You can put your finger on the 20th and count ten days forward, or jump back to the 1st of the month. SQLite's date functions do the same moves for you, and they know how long every month is.

**Step 1: `date()` keeps the date part.** Given a timestamp, `date(...)` returns only the year-month-day. A `SELECT` with no `FROM` works out its values once and returns one row, which is a handy way to try a function:

```sql
SELECT date('2024-03-20 18:40:00');   -- '2024-03-20'
```

**Step 2: modifiers move the date.** Any extra text you pass after the time is a **modifier**: an instruction that moves the date before it is returned.

```sql
SELECT date('2024-03-20', '+10 days');        -- '2024-03-30'
SELECT date('2024-03-20', '-1 day');          -- '2024-03-19'
SELECT date('2024-03-20', 'start of month');  -- '2024-03-01'
```

**Step 3: months are uneven.** `'+1 month'` adds one to the month number and keeps the day. When that day does not exist, SQLite [[rolls over into the next month|month-rollover]]:

```sql
SELECT date('2024-01-31', '+1 month');   -- '2024-03-02' (no 31 February!)
```

31 February would be two days after 29 February (2024 is a leap year), so you land on 2 March.

**Step 4: the days between two dates.** `julianday(time)` turns a date into one plain number: a count of days since a [[starting point long ago|julian-day]]. Subtract two of them and you get the days in between:

```sql
SELECT julianday('2024-03-20') - julianday('2024-03-02');   -- 18.0
```

The answer has a decimal point because times of day become fractions of a day: 18:00 is three quarters of the way through a day, so it adds 0.75.

**Step 5: today.** `date('now')` is [[today's date|now-is-utc]]. Its answer changes every day, so it is great in an app and useless in a check with a fixed answer.

**Step 6: a range of dates.** To pick every order placed in April, compare the text directly. `>=` means "greater than or equal to" and `<` means "less than":

```sql
SELECT id, placed FROM orders
WHERE placed >= '2024-04-01' AND placed < '2024-05-01';
```

| id | placed |
| --- | --- |
| 4 | 2024-04-07 14:30:00 |
| 5 | 2024-04-07 20:10:00 |

This works because ISO text sorts in time order. Read it as "from the first moment of April, up to but not including the first moment of May". It is also faster on big tables than wrapping the column in a function such as `strftime('%Y-%m', placed) = '2024-04'`, because the database can use an [[index|index-preview]] for it.

**Watch out:** `placed <= '2024-04-30'` looks like "up to the end of April", but it misses every order placed *during* 30 April. As text, `'2024-04-30 18:00:00'` is longer than `'2024-04-30'` and starts the same, so it sorts after it. End a range with `<` and the first moment you do *not* want, `'2024-05-01'`, as a [[half-open range|half-open]].

::: context month-rollover Why dates roll over
SQLite handles `'+1 month'` in two moves: add one to the month number, then fix any day that does not exist by counting on into the next month. From 31 January 2024 that gives "31 February", which is two days past 29 February, so 2 March. In 2023, which was not a leap year, the same step from 31 January lands on 3 March: `SELECT date('2023-01-31', '+1 month');` returns `'2023-03-03'`. For "the last day of this month" use `date(x, 'start of month', '+1 month', '-1 day')`, which never overflows.
:::

::: context julian-day Days counted from long ago
A Julian day number counts days from noon on 1 January 4713 BC, a starting point astronomers chose centuries ago so that every recorded event gets a positive number. Astronomers still use it to time observations, because subtracting two numbers is easier than juggling months and leap years. `SELECT julianday('2024-03-20');` returns 2460389.5. The .5 is there because Julian days begin at noon, so midnight falls halfway through one.
:::

::: context now-is-utc Whose today?
`date('now')` uses UTC, the world's reference clock (the time in London outside summer time). If you are in California late in the evening, it may already be tomorrow in UTC. Mission control centers and servers usually store every timestamp in UTC for this reason, so that events from different places line up, and only convert to local time when they show a time to a person. SQLite can do that too, with the `'localtime'` modifier.
:::

::: context index-preview A shortcut through a big table
An **index** is like the index at the back of a book: a separate, sorted list that tells the database where to find rows with a given value, so it can jump straight to them instead of reading every row. A sorted list of `placed` values lets it jump to the first April order and stop at the first May one. If you wrap the column in a function, the sorted list no longer matches what you are asking about, so it reads every row. You will build indexes and watch this happen in the advanced course.
:::

::: context half-open Include the start, exclude the end
A range that includes its start but not its end is called half-open. Written this way, April and May meet without a gap or an overlap: April ends exactly where May begins, so every moment belongs to one month only. The same habit shows up in programming, where a loop over 0 up to (not including) 10 runs ten times.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="20" y1="60" x2="340" y2="60" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="60" y1="60" x2="300" y2="60" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="6"/>
  <circle cx="60" cy="60" r="7" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <circle cx="300" cy="60" r="7" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="3"/>
  <text x="60" y="36" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2024-04-01</text>
  <text x="300" y="36" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">2024-05-01</text>
  <text x="60" y="88" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">included (&gt;=)</text>
  <text x="300" y="88" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">left out (&lt;)</text>
  <text x="180" y="116" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">every moment of April, 30 April 18:00 too</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Return every order placed in April 2024. Pick them with a range on `placed`: `>=` the first day of April and `<` the first day of May. For each order return:

- `id`;
- `day`: the date part of `placed`, using `date(...)`;
- `reply_by`: the date 14 days after `day`, using the `'+14 days'` modifier.

Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, placed
FROM orders
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id,
       date(placed) AS day,
       date(placed, '+14 days') AS reply_by
FROM orders
WHERE placed >= '2024-04-01' AND placed < '2024-05-01'
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
First the filter: `WHERE placed >= '2024-04-01' AND placed < '2024-05-01'` keeps exactly April.
--- hint
`date(placed) AS day` gives the date part. A modifier goes after the time inside the same brackets: `date(placed, '+14 days')`.
--- hint
The whole query is `SELECT id, date(placed) AS day, date(placed, '+14 days') AS reply_by FROM orders WHERE … ORDER BY id`, with the April range as the `WHERE`.
--- check result | Orders 4 and 5, placed 7 April, reply by 21 April
ordered
[[4, "2024-04-07", "2024-04-21"], [5, "2024-04-07", "2024-04-21"]]
--- check source | Ends the range with < the first of May
<\s*'2024-05-01'
?? End the April range with `placed < '2024-05-01'`, so every moment of 30 April is included.

+++ practice | When the free trial ends
--- task
Every new customer gets a 30-day free trial that starts on the day they join. For every customer, return three columns:

- `name` and `joined`
- `trial_end`: the date 30 days after `joined`, using a `'+30 days'` modifier

Sort by `joined`.
--- starter
SELECT name, joined
FROM customers
ORDER BY joined;
--- solution
SELECT name, joined, date(joined, '+30 days') AS trial_end
FROM customers
ORDER BY joined;
--- hint
A modifier goes inside `date(...)`, after the date it moves.
--- hint
`date(joined, '+30 days') AS trial_end`. SQLite knows February 2024 has 29 days.
--- check result | Ada's trial ends 2024-02-14, Ben's 2024-03-04 (February has 29 days), Gus's 2024-06-01
ordered
[["Ada", "2024-01-15", "2024-02-14"], ["Ben", "2024-02-03", "2024-03-04"], ["Cleo", "2024-02-20", "2024-03-21"], ["Dev", "2024-03-11", "2024-04-10"], ["Eve", "2024-04-01", "2024-05-01"], ["Finn", "2024-04-18", "2024-05-18"], ["Gus", "2024-05-02", "2024-06-01"]]
--- check source | Uses a +30 days modifier
'\+30 days?'
--- check source absent | Does not type an end date

+++ practice | Joined in the last 60 days
--- task
It is 15 April 2024. Return the `name` and `joined` date of every customer who joined in the 60 days before that: on or after the date 60 days before `'2024-04-15'`, and before `'2024-04-15'` itself.

Work out the start of the range with `date('2024-04-15', '-60 days')`; do not type it. Sort by `joined`.
--- starter
SELECT name, joined
FROM customers
ORDER BY joined;
--- solution
SELECT name, joined
FROM customers
WHERE joined >= date('2024-04-15', '-60 days')
  AND joined < '2024-04-15'
ORDER BY joined;
--- hint
Try `SELECT date('2024-04-15', '-60 days');` on its own first. That is where the range starts.
--- hint
Use it on the left edge of a half-open range: `joined >= date('2024-04-15', '-60 days') AND joined < '2024-04-15'`.
--- check result | Cleo, Dev and Eve
ordered
[["Cleo", "2024-02-20"], ["Dev", "2024-03-11"], ["Eve", "2024-04-01"]]

+++ practice | How long until the first order
--- task
For every customer who has placed at least one order (any status), return two columns:

- `name`
- `days_to_first`: the number of days from `joined` to the **date** of their first order

Use the date part of the first order's timestamp, so the time of day does not add a fraction. Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, MIN(o.placed) AS first_order
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name,
       julianday(date(MIN(o.placed))) - julianday(c.joined) AS days_to_first
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
The first order is `MIN(o.placed)`, and `date(...)` keeps only its date part.
--- hint
Subtract two `julianday` values: `julianday(date(MIN(o.placed))) - julianday(c.joined)`.
--- check result | Ada, Ben and Cleo 47 days each, Dev 65, Eve 57
ordered
[["Ada", 47.0], ["Ben", 47.0], ["Cleo", 47.0], ["Dev", 65.0], ["Eve", 57.0]]
--- check source | Counts days with julianday
[Jj][Uu][Ll][Ii][Aa][Nn][Dd][Aa][Yy]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the answers
\b(47|65|57)\b

+++ practice | Everything on the seventh
--- schema
CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  at TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO events (id, at) VALUES
  (1, '2024-04-06 23:59:59'),
  (2, '2024-04-07 00:00:00'),
  (3, '2024-04-07 12:00:00'),
  (4, '2024-04-07 23:59:59'),
  (5, '2024-04-07'),
  (6, '2024-04-08 00:00:00'),
  (7, '2024-04-08');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `events`, with `id` and `at`. Most `at` values are full timestamps, but some were written as a date alone, like `'2024-04-07'`.

Return the `id` and `at` of every event on 7 April 2024, at any time of that day, including the one written as a date alone. Use a half-open range: from `'2024-04-07'`, up to but not including the next day, which you work out with `date('2024-04-07', '+1 day')`. Midnight at the start of the 8th belongs to the 8th. Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, at
FROM events
WHERE at BETWEEN '2024-04-07' AND '2024-04-08'
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id, at
FROM events
WHERE at >= '2024-04-07'
  AND at < date('2024-04-07', '+1 day')
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Run the starter: `BETWEEN` includes both ends, so `'2024-04-08'` itself sneaks in.
--- hint
Start with `at >= '2024-04-07'`, which also catches `'2024-04-07'` written alone, and end with `at < date('2024-04-07', '+1 day')`.
--- check result | Events 2, 3, 4 and 5; not 6 or 7, which are on the 8th
ordered
[[2, "2024-04-07 00:00:00"], [3, "2024-04-07 12:00:00"], [4, "2024-04-07 23:59:59"], [5, "2024-04-07"]]
--- check source | Ends the range with < the next day
<\s*[Dd][Aa][Tt][Ee]\s*\(\s*'2024-04-07'\s*,\s*'\+1 days?'\s*\)
