[Bb][Ee][Tt][Ww][Ee][Ee][Nn]

+++ practice | The June deliveries that went missing
--- schema
CREATE TABLE deliveries (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  item TEXT NOT NULL,
  at TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO deliveries (id, item, at) VALUES
  (1, 'oxygen',  '2024-05-31 22:00:00'),
  (2, 'water',   '2024-06-01 00:00:00'),
  (3, 'food',    '2024-06-15 09:30:00'),
  (4, 'filters', '2024-06-30 00:00:00'),
  (5, 'fuel',    '2024-06-30 14:00:00'),
  (6, 'spares',  '2024-07-01 00:00:00');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `deliveries`, with `id`, `item` and `at`, a timestamp.

This query should return the `id` and `item` of every delivery in June 2024, sorted by `id`. It misses both deliveries made on 30 June. Fix the range so it covers all of June and nothing else.
--- starter
SELECT id, item
FROM deliveries
WHERE at BETWEEN '2024-06-01' AND '2024-06-30'
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id, item
FROM deliveries
WHERE at >= '2024-06-01' AND at < '2024-07-01'
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
As text, `'2024-06-30 14:00:00'` starts like `'2024-06-30'` but is longer, so it sorts after it and falls outside the `BETWEEN`.
--- hint
End the range with `<` and the first moment you do not want: `at >= '2024-06-01' AND at < '2024-07-01'`.
--- check result | water, food, filters and fuel (ids 2 to 5)
ordered
[[2, "water"], [3, "food"], [4, "filters"], [5, "fuel"]]
--- check source | Ends the range before 1 July
<\s*'2024-07-01'
--- check source absent | Does not list the ids
[Ii][Dd]\s*[Ii][Nn]\s*\(

+++ practice | Overdue invoices
--- schema
CREATE TABLE invoices (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  customer TEXT NOT NULL,
  issued TEXT NOT NULL,
  due_days INTEGER NOT NULL,
  paid_on TEXT
);
INSERT INTO invoices (id, customer, issued, due_days, paid_on) VALUES
  (1, 'Orbital Ltd', '2024-05-01', 30, '2024-05-28'),
  (2, 'Nova Corp',   '2024-05-10', 30, '2024-06-15'),
  (3, 'Astra',       '2024-06-01', 14, NULL),
  (4, 'Helio',       '2024-06-20', 30, NULL),
  (5, 'Kosmo',       '2024-01-31', 30, '2024-03-01'),
  (6, 'Vostok',      '2024-06-01', 7,  '2024-06-09');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `invoices`, with `id`, `customer`, `issued` (a date), `due_days` (how many days the customer has to pay) and `paid_on` (the date it was paid, or NULL if it is still unpaid).

An invoice is due `due_days` days after `issued`. It is **late** if it was paid after its due date, or if it is still unpaid on `'2024-07-01'`, today. Paying on the due date itself is on time. Return every late invoice, with four columns:

- `id` and `customer`
- `due`: its due date
- `days_late`: the days from the due date to the day it was paid, or to today if it is unpaid

Sort by `days_late`, highest first, then by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, customer, issued, due_days, paid_on
FROM invoices
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id, customer,
       date(issued, '+' || due_days || ' days') AS due,
       julianday(COALESCE(paid_on, '2024-07-01')) - julianday(date(issued, '+' || due_days || ' days')) AS days_late
FROM invoices
WHERE julianday(COALESCE(paid_on, '2024-07-01')) > julianday(date(issued, '+' || due_days || ' days'))
ORDER BY days_late DESC, id;
--- hint
A modifier is only text, so you can build it with `||`: `'+' || due_days || ' days'` gives `'+30 days'` for a 30-day invoice.
--- hint
The day to measure to is the payment date, or today when there is none: `COALESCE(paid_on, '2024-07-01')`.
--- hint
Subtract two `julianday` values for `days_late`, and keep the rows where the end day is later than the due date.
--- check result | Astra 16 days late (unpaid), Nova Corp 6, Vostok 1; Kosmo paid on the due day
ordered
[[3, "Astra", "2024-06-15", 16.0], [2, "Nova Corp", "2024-06-09", 6.0], [6, "Vostok", "2024-06-08", 1.0]]
--- check source | Builds the modifier with ||
\|\|\s*due_days\s*\|\||due_days\s*\|\|
--- check source | Counts days with julianday
[Jj][Uu][Ll][Ii][Aa][Nn][Dd][Aa][Yy]\s*\(

=== sql2-10 | Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear
--- teach
Last lesson you picked out a range of dates. This lesson is a debugging case: a query that returns too few rows, and the cause is NULL. You will see how `WHERE` really treats NULL, how to fix the filter, and two functions, `COALESCE` and `NULLIF`, that turn missing values into readable labels.

A quick reminder from LEFT JOIN and NULL: **NULL** means "no value here". The box on the form was left empty. It is not zero, and it is not an empty piece of text. In `orders`, the `coupon` column is NULL for orders where no coupon was used.

**The bug report.** Marketing asked for every order that did **not** use the `SPRING10` coupon, with a readable label for the coupon. This query came back with only two orders. (`!=` means "not equal to", and `<>` [[means the same thing|not-equal-signs]].)

```sql
SELECT id, coupon AS coupon_label
FROM orders
WHERE coupon != 'SPRING10'
ORDER BY id;
```

| id | coupon_label |
| --- | --- |
| 4 | WELCOME5 |
| 7 | (blank) |

There are eight orders and two used `SPRING10`, so six were expected. Here is a [[way to debug|debug-by-counting]] any query that returns too few rows.

**Step 1: reproduce and count.** Count what should be there:

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders;   -- 8
```

8 − 2 = 6 were expected and 2 came back, so four orders went missing.

**Step 2: look at what went missing.**

```sql
SELECT id, coupon FROM orders;
```

| id | coupon |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | NULL |
| 2 | SPRING10 |
| 3 | NULL |
| 4 | WELCOME5 |
| 5 | SPRING10 |
| 6 | NULL |
| 7 | (empty text) |
| 8 | NULL |

The missing orders are 1, 3, 6 and 8, and every one has `coupon` = NULL.

**Step 3: check your assumption.** The query assumes `coupon != 'SPRING10'` is true when there is no coupon. Picture a clerk asked "is the coupon on this form something other than SPRING10?" when the coupon box is empty. The honest answer is "I don't know, nothing was written". SQL answers the same way.

This is **[[three-valued logic|three-valued]]**: in SQL a comparison can be true, false, or **unknown**. Any comparison with NULL is unknown: `NULL = 'x'`, `NULL != 'x'`, even `NULL = NULL`. SQLite shows unknown as NULL:

```sql
SELECT NULL = NULL, NULL != 'x';   -- NULL, NULL
```

And `WHERE` keeps a row only when its condition is **true**. Unknown is thrown away exactly like false. So `coupon != 'SPRING10'` silently drops every order with no coupon. Adding `NOT` does not rescue it: `NOT (coupon = 'SPRING10')` is still unknown for those rows, because "not unknown" is still unknown.

**Step 4: fix the filter.** Here are three fixes, the most explicit first:

```sql
WHERE coupon IS NULL OR coupon <> 'SPRING10'
WHERE coupon IS NOT 'SPRING10'        -- SQLite: IS / IS NOT treat NULL as a value
WHERE COALESCE(coupon, '') <> 'SPRING10'
```

- The first says it out loud: keep the row if there is no coupon, or if the coupon is something else. `IS NULL` is always true or false, never unknown.
- The second uses a SQLite shortcut. `IS` and `IS NOT` can compare with any value, and they treat NULL as an ordinary value, so an empty coupon "is not" SPRING10. Other databases spell this [[IS DISTINCT FROM|is-distinct-from]].
- The third uses `COALESCE`, the next step.

**Step 5: `COALESCE` fills a gap.** `COALESCE(a, b, …)` returns the first of its values that is not NULL. You met it as `COALESCE(x, 0)`. It also makes labels:

```sql
SELECT id, COALESCE(coupon, 'none') AS coupon_label FROM orders;
```

Orders 1, 3, 6 and 8 now say `none`. Order 7 still shows a blank.

**Step 6: the empty string.** Order 7's coupon is `''`, the **[[empty string|empty-string]]**: text with no characters in it. That is not NULL. Something was written, it is only empty. So `COALESCE` leaves it alone. `NULLIF(x, y)` gives NULL when `x` equals `y`, and `x` otherwise, so `NULLIF(coupon, '')` turns `''` into NULL. Put `trim` inside so a coupon made only of spaces counts as blank too:

```sql
COALESCE(NULLIF(trim(coupon), ''), 'none')
```

Read it from the inside out: trim the spaces; if what is left is `''`, make it NULL; if it is NULL, use `'none'`.

| coupon | trim, then NULLIF | label |
| --- | --- | --- |
| WELCOME5 | WELCOME5 | WELCOME5 |
| NULL | NULL | none |
| (empty text) | NULL | none |

**Watch out:** `!=` or `<>` on a column that can be NULL quietly drops the NULL rows. Whenever a column may be NULL, ask "what should happen to rows with no value?" and write the answer into the `WHERE`.

::: context not-equal-signs Two ways to write "not equal"
`<>` is the "not equal" sign in the SQL standard: read it as "less than or greater than", which is another way of saying "different". `!=` comes from programming languages like C, Python and JavaScript. SQLite, PostgreSQL, MySQL and most other databases accept both, and they behave the same way, including with NULL. Pick one and use it consistently in a project.
:::

::: context debug-by-counting Count before you guess
When a result looks wrong, the fastest path is rarely staring at the query. Work out how many rows there *should* be, count how many you got, then look at exactly the rows that went missing and ask what they have in common. Here every missing row had the same thing: an empty coupon. Engineers checking flight telemetry do the same: if a downlink should hold 600 frames and the database has 590, the first job is to find the ten missing frames, not to rewrite the code.
:::

::: context three-valued True, false and unknown
Ordinary logic has two values. SQL adds a third, unknown, so that a missing value never pretends to be known. `AND` and `OR` still give a sure answer when they can: false `AND` anything is false, and true `OR` anything is true. `SELECT NULL OR 1;` returns 1 and `SELECT NULL AND 0;` returns 0. Otherwise unknown spreads. Orange cells are unknown.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 190" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="90" y="20" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">AND</text>
  <text x="270" y="20" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">OR</text>
  <g font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">
    <text x="75" y="50">T</text><text x="105" y="50">F</text><text x="135" y="50">U</text>
    <text x="45" y="80">T</text><text x="45" y="110">F</text><text x="45" y="140">U</text>
    <text x="255" y="50">T</text><text x="285" y="50">F</text><text x="315" y="50">U</text>
    <text x="225" y="80">T</text><text x="225" y="110">F</text><text x="225" y="140">U</text>
  </g>
  <g stroke="#1f2a44" fill="#ffffff">
    <rect x="60" y="60" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="90" y="60" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="120" y="60" width="30" height="30" fill="#f2b880"/>
    <rect x="60" y="90" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="90" y="90" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="120" y="90" width="30" height="30"/>
    <rect x="60" y="120" width="30" height="30" fill="#f2b880"/><rect x="90" y="120" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="120" y="120" width="30" height="30" fill="#f2b880"/>
    <rect x="240" y="60" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="270" y="60" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="300" y="60" width="30" height="30"/>
    <rect x="240" y="90" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="270" y="90" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="300" y="90" width="30" height="30" fill="#f2b880"/>
    <rect x="240" y="120" width="30" height="30"/><rect x="270" y="120" width="30" height="30" fill="#f2b880"/><rect x="300" y="120" width="30" height="30" fill="#f2b880"/>
  </g>
  <g font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">
    <text x="75" y="80">T</text><text x="105" y="80">F</text><text x="135" y="80">U</text>
    <text x="75" y="110">F</text><text x="105" y="110">F</text><text x="135" y="110">F</text>
    <text x="75" y="140">U</text><text x="105" y="140">F</text><text x="135" y="140">U</text>
    <text x="255" y="80">T</text><text x="285" y="80">T</text><text x="315" y="80">T</text>
    <text x="255" y="110">T</text><text x="285" y="110">F</text><text x="315" y="110">U</text>
    <text x="255" y="140">T</text><text x="285" y="140">U</text><text x="315" y="140">U</text>
  </g>
  <text x="180" y="178" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">T true, F false, U unknown (NULL)</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context is-distinct-from The standard name for IS NOT
The SQL standard writes the NULL-safe "not equal" as `a IS DISTINCT FROM b`: true when the two differ, counting NULL as a value of its own. PostgreSQL uses that spelling, and SQLite has accepted it too since version 3.39 (2022), so `WHERE coupon IS DISTINCT FROM 'SPRING10'` returns the same six orders. The short `IS NOT 'SPRING10'` form works in SQLite but not in PostgreSQL, so the long name is the one to reach for when a query has to run in more than one database.
:::

::: context empty-string Nothing written versus no answer
NULL and the empty string are easy to mix up. Think of a form with a "middle name" box. NULL means the box was never filled in: we do not know the middle name. `''` means someone deliberately wrote "no middle name". Most databases keep the two apart, as SQLite does. Oracle Database is a famous exception: it stores an empty string as NULL, which surprises people who move queries between databases. Blank text often sneaks in from web forms and spreadsheets, which is why cleaning with `NULLIF(trim(x), '')` is a common habit.
:::
--- task
Fix the starter query so it returns every order that did not use `SPRING10`: six orders. Return two columns:

- `id`;
- `coupon_label`: the coupon code, or `'none'` when the coupon is NULL or blank (empty, or only spaces).

Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT id, coupon AS coupon_label
FROM orders
WHERE coupon != 'SPRING10'
ORDER BY id;
--- solution
SELECT id, COALESCE(NULLIF(trim(coupon), ''), 'none') AS coupon_label
FROM orders
WHERE coupon IS NULL OR coupon <> 'SPRING10'
ORDER BY id;
--- hint
Run `SELECT id, coupon FROM orders` and look at the orders that are missing from the starter's result. What is `NULL != 'SPRING10'`: true, false or unknown?
--- hint
Let the NULL coupons through on purpose: `WHERE coupon IS NULL OR coupon <> 'SPRING10'`.
--- hint
For the label, `COALESCE(coupon, 'none')` handles NULL but not `''`. Wrap the coupon first: `COALESCE(NULLIF(trim(coupon), ''), 'none') AS coupon_label`.
--- check result | Six orders, blank and missing coupons labelled none
ordered
[[1, "none"], [3, "none"], [4, "WELCOME5"], [6, "none"], [7, "none"], [8, "none"]]

- `name`
- `city_label`: the city, or `'unknown'` when it is NULL

Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT name, city AS city_label
FROM customers
WHERE city <> 'London'
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name, COALESCE(city, 'unknown') AS city_label
FROM customers

+++ practice | Three ways to count coupons
--- task
Return a single row with three columns about the `orders` table:

- `all_orders`: how many orders there are
- `coupon_values`: how many orders have a `coupon` that is not NULL
- `real_coupons`: how many orders have a real coupon code: not NULL, and not blank (empty, or only spaces)

Use `COUNT` for all three. The difference between the last two is order 7, whose coupon is `''`.
--- starter
SELECT COUNT(*) AS all_orders
FROM orders;
--- solution
SELECT COUNT(*) AS all_orders,
       COUNT(coupon) AS coupon_values,
       COUNT(NULLIF(trim(coupon), '')) AS real_coupons
FROM orders;
--- hint
`COUNT(*)` counts rows. `COUNT(column)` counts only the rows where that column is not NULL.
--- hint
Turn blank coupons into NULL first, so `COUNT` skips them too: `COUNT(NULLIF(trim(coupon), ''))`.
--- check result | 8 orders, 4 coupon values, 3 real coupons
ordered
[[8, 4, 3]]
--- check source | Uses NULLIF
[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll][Ii][Ff]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type the counts
[Ss][Ee][Ll][Ee][Cc][Tt]\s+\d|,\s*\d\s+[Aa][Ss]

+++ practice | Not referred by Ada
--- task
Return every customer who was **not** referred by Ada (customer 1), including the customers nobody referred. Return two columns:

- `name`
- `referrer`: the name of the customer who referred them, or `'nobody'`

Use `customers` twice, as in the lesson "Self-joins". Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, r.name AS referrer
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
WHERE c.referred_by != 1
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COALESCE(r.name, 'nobody') AS referrer
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
WHERE c.referred_by IS NULL OR c.referred_by <> 1
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Ada, Eve and Gus are missing. Their `referred_by` is NULL, and `NULL != 1` is unknown.
--- hint
Keep them on purpose: `WHERE c.referred_by IS NULL OR c.referred_by <> 1` (or SQLite's `c.referred_by IS NOT 1`). Then label the empty referrer with `COALESCE(r.name, 'nobody')`.
--- check result | Ada, Eve and Gus (nobody), Dev (Ben) and Finn (Dev)
ordered

+++ practice | Coupon codes typed by hand
--- schema
CREATE TABLE redemptions (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  coupon TEXT
);
INSERT INTO redemptions (id, coupon) VALUES
  (1, 'spring10'), (2, ' SPRING10'), (3, 'SPRING10 '), (4, NULL), (5, ''),
  (6, '   '), (7, 'WELCOME5'), (8, 'welcome5'), (9, NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `redemptions`, with `id` and `coupon`, typed in by hand at a till. The same code shows up in different cases and with stray spaces, and a missing code can be NULL, empty, or only spaces.

Count the redemptions per code. Return two columns:

- `code`: the coupon in capitals with the spaces trimmed, or `'NONE'` when it is NULL or blank
- `uses`: how many redemptions have that code

Sort by `uses`, highest first, then by `code`.
--- starter
SELECT COALESCE(coupon, 'NONE') AS code, COUNT(*) AS uses
FROM redemptions
GROUP BY code
ORDER BY uses DESC, code;
--- solution
SELECT COALESCE(NULLIF(upper(trim(coupon)), ''), 'NONE') AS code,
       COUNT(*) AS uses
FROM redemptions
GROUP BY code
ORDER BY uses DESC, code;
--- hint
Clean the code first with `upper(trim(coupon))`. After trimming, a blank coupon becomes `''`.
--- hint
Turn that `''` into NULL with `NULLIF(..., '')`, then turn every NULL into `'NONE'` with `COALESCE`. Read it from the inside out.
--- check result | NONE 4 (two NULLs and two blanks), SPRING10 3, WELCOME5 2

+++ practice | The email that is never missing
--- task
This query should label every customer's email: `'missing'` when it is NULL and `'on file'` otherwise. It returns `name` and `email_status`, sorted by name. It says every email is on file, but Cleo has none.

Fix the `CASE`.
--- starter
SELECT name,
       CASE email WHEN NULL THEN 'missing' ELSE 'on file' END AS email_status
FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT name,
       CASE WHEN email IS NULL THEN 'missing' ELSE 'on file' END AS email_status
FROM customers
ORDER BY name;
--- hint
The short form of `CASE` compares with `=`. What is `NULL = NULL`?
--- hint
Use the searched form, which can say `IS NULL`: `CASE WHEN email IS NULL THEN 'missing' ELSE 'on file' END`.
--- check result | Cleo missing, the other six on file
ordered
[["Ada", "on file"], ["Ben", "on file"], ["Cleo", "missing"], ["Dev", "on file"], ["Eve", "on file"], ["Finn", "on file"], ["Gus", "on file"]]

+++ practice | Readings with a fake number for missing
--- schema
CREATE TABLE readings (
  sensor TEXT NOT NULL,
  temp REAL
);
INSERT INTO readings (sensor, temp) VALUES
  ('A', 20.0), ('A', -999), ('A', 22.0), ('A', NULL),
  ('B', -999), ('B', -999),
  ('C', 15.5), ('C', 16.5), ('C', 17.0),
  ('D', -5.0), ('D', NULL), ('D', -999);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `readings`, with `sensor` and `temp`. An old logger wrote `-999` when it had no reading; the new one writes NULL. Both mean "missing". A real temperature can still be below zero, like -5.0.

Return one row per sensor with five columns:

- `sensor`
- `readings`: how many rows it has
- `missing`: how many of them are NULL or `-999`
- `avg_temp`: the average of the real readings only, rounded to 1 decimal, or NULL if it has none
- `min_temp`: the lowest real reading, or NULL if it has none

Use `NULLIF` to turn the fake number into NULL. Sort by `sensor`.
--- starter
SELECT sensor, COUNT(*) AS readings, ROUND(AVG(temp), 1) AS avg_temp, MIN(temp) AS min_temp
FROM readings
GROUP BY sensor
ORDER BY sensor;
--- solution
SELECT sensor,
       COUNT(*) AS readings,
       COUNT(*) - COUNT(NULLIF(temp, -999)) AS missing,
       ROUND(AVG(NULLIF(temp, -999)), 1) AS avg_temp,
       MIN(NULLIF(temp, -999)) AS min_temp
FROM readings
GROUP BY sensor
ORDER BY sensor;
--- hint
Aggregates already skip NULL. Turn every `-999` into NULL too, and they will skip those as well: `NULLIF(temp, -999)`.
--- hint
Use `NULLIF(temp, -999)` inside `AVG` and `MIN`. For `missing`, count all rows and take away the real ones: `COUNT(*) - COUNT(NULLIF(temp, -999))`.
--- check result | A avg 21.0, B all missing (NULL), C 16.3, D -5.0 (a real reading below zero)
ordered
[["A", 4, 2, 21.0, 20.0], ["B", 2, 2, null, null], ["C", 3, 0, 16.3, 15.5], ["D", 3, 2, -5.0, -5.0]]
--- check source | Uses NULLIF for the fake number
[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll][Ii][Ff]\s*\(\s*temp\s*,\s*-999\s*\)
--- check source absent | Does not pick sensors by name
'[ABCD]'

=== sql2-11 | DISTINCT, UNION and UNION ALL
--- teach
Last lesson you made NULL and blank coupons behave. This lesson is about duplicates: how to remove repeated rows from a result, and how to stack two results into one list. By the end you will build one mailing list from two tables.

Picture two guest lists for a party, one from you and one from a friend. You want a single list with everybody on it, and every name only once.

**Step 1: `DISTINCT` removes repeats.** `SELECT DISTINCT` returns each different row once. `DISTINCT` means "different".

```sql
SELECT city FROM customers;            -- 7 rows
SELECT DISTINCT city FROM customers;   -- 4 rows
```

| city (DISTINCT) |
| --- |
| London |
| Paris |
| NULL |
| Berlin |

London, Paris and NULL each appeared twice. Notice that the two [[NULLs count as one|nulls-in-distinct]].

**Step 2: `DISTINCT` looks at whole rows.** A row is a duplicate only when *every* selected column matches, not only the first:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT city, referred_by FROM customers;   -- 7 rows
```

Ada is London with no referrer, and Dev is London referred by customer 2. Those are different pairs, so both stay. In fact all seven pairs differ, so nothing is removed.

**Step 3: `UNION ALL` stacks two results.** `UNION ALL` puts the rows of a second `SELECT` underneath the rows of the first. The two `SELECT`s must return the [[same number of columns|same-shape]], and the column names come from the first one.

```sql
SELECT email FROM customers
UNION ALL
SELECT email FROM newsletter;
```

This returns 12 rows: 7 from `customers` (Cleo's NULL email included) and then 5 from `newsletter`. `ada@mail.com` is on both lists, so it appears twice.

**Step 4: `UNION` stacks and removes repeats.** Leave out the `ALL` and `UNION` removes duplicate rows from the combined result, including duplicates that were both on the *same* side. The same query with `UNION` returns 11 rows, with `ada@mail.com` once.

Which to use? `UNION ALL` keeps every row, and it is [[cheaper|union-cost]] because it never checks for repeats. It is the right choice when you want to count everything, or when you know the parts cannot overlap. Use `UNION` when repeats should go.

**Step 5: one `ORDER BY`, at the very end.** An `ORDER BY` after the last `SELECT` sorts the whole combined result. It uses the column names from the first `SELECT`:

```sql
SELECT email FROM customers
UNION
SELECT email FROM newsletter
ORDER BY email;
```

**Step 6: repeats must match exactly.** `DISTINCT` and `UNION` compare values character by character. `'BEN@Mail.com'` and `'ben@mail.com'` are different strings, and so are `'Zoe@mail.com '` (with a space at the end) and `'zoe@mail.com'`. Neither will be merged. When you mean "the [[same address|email-case]]", clean each side first with `lower(trim(email))` from Working with text, so the union compares tidy values.

**Watch out:** each `SELECT` in a union is separate. A `lower(trim(...))` or a `WHERE` written on the first `SELECT` does nothing to the second, so clean both sides.

`UNION` also has [[two relatives|set-operations]], `INTERSECT` and `EXCEPT`, which combine whole results in other ways.

::: context nulls-in-distinct NULL meets NULL
Last lesson said `NULL = NULL` is unknown, not true. Yet `DISTINCT` keeps only one NULL city. That is on purpose: for removing duplicates, the SQL standard says two NULLs are "not distinct" from each other, so they count as the same. `GROUP BY` does the same thing, which is why all the NULLs landed in one group in GROUP BY in depth, and `UNION` does too. So the rule has two halves: comparisons with `=` treat NULL as unknown, while `DISTINCT`, `GROUP BY` and `UNION` treat NULLs as one value.
:::

::: context same-shape When the columns do not line up
If the two sides have different numbers of columns, SQLite refuses to run the query and tells you why: "SELECTs to the left and right of UNION ALL do not have the same number of result columns". The column *names* on the second side do not matter, only how many there are and their order. So `SELECT name FROM products UNION ALL SELECT email FROM newsletter` runs happily and mixes product names with email addresses in one column. SQL trusts you to line up columns that mean the same thing.
:::

::: context union-cost Why removing repeats costs more
To find duplicates, the database has to compare every row with the others. In practice it sorts all the rows or builds a lookup table of them, then drops the repeats. `UNION ALL` skips that work and passes the rows straight through. On a few rows the difference is invisible. On millions of log rows from a fleet of satellites it can be the difference between a quick query and a slow one.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="60" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">side A</text>
  <rect x="20" y="28" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/><text x="60" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">ada</text>
  <rect x="20" y="52" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/><text x="60" y="68" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">eve</text>
  <text x="60" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">side B</text>
  <rect x="20" y="110" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#f2b880"/><text x="60" y="126" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">ada</text>
  <rect x="20" y="134" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#f2b880"/><text x="60" y="150" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">zoe</text>
  <text x="180" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">UNION ALL: 4</text>
  <rect x="140" y="28" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/><text x="180" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">ada</text>
  <rect x="140" y="52" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1"/><text x="180" y="68" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">eve</text>
  <rect x="140" y="76" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#f2b880"/><text x="180" y="92" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">ada</text>
  <rect x="140" y="100" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#f2b880"/><text x="180" y="116" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">zoe</text>
  <text x="300" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">UNION: 3</text>
  <rect x="260" y="28" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="300" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">ada</text>
  <rect x="260" y="52" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="300" y="68" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">eve</text>
  <rect x="260" y="76" width="80" height="24" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="300" y="92" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">zoe</text>
  <text x="180" y="150" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">the repeat (red) is kept</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context email-case Is BEN the same as ben?
Strictly, the rules for email say the part before the `@` could be case-sensitive, so `BEN@` and `ben@` might be two mailboxes. In practice almost every email provider treats them as the same person, and the part after the `@` (the domain) is never case-sensitive. That is why mailing lists store addresses in lower case with no stray spaces. Sorting shows why cleaning matters: in SQLite, capital letters sort before lower-case ones and a space sorts before both, so an unclean list puts `' dev@work.io'` first and `'BEN@Mail.com'` before `'ada@mail.com'`.
:::

::: context set-operations Two more ways to combine
`UNION` has two relatives that also stack whole results. `INTERSECT` keeps only the rows that appear on both sides, and `EXCEPT` keeps the rows of the first side that are not on the second. `SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM newsletter EXCEPT SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM customers` lists subscribers who are not customers yet. You will see `EXCEPT` again in the advanced course, checking that a redesigned set of tables still holds exactly the same data as the old one.
:::
--- task
Build one mailing list from `customers` and `newsletter`. Return a single column named `email` holding every **distinct** email address, lower-cased and trimmed (`lower(trim(email))`), with no NULLs, sorted alphabetically.
--- starter
SELECT email FROM customers
UNION ALL
SELECT email FROM newsletter;
--- solution
SELECT lower(trim(email)) AS email FROM customers WHERE email IS NOT NULL
UNION
SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM newsletter
ORDER BY email;
--- hint
`UNION` (without `ALL`) removes repeats, but only exact ones. Clean each side with `lower(trim(email))`, and name the first one `AS email`.
--- hint
Cleo's email is NULL: add `WHERE email IS NOT NULL` to the `customers` side.
--- hint
The shape: `SELECT lower(trim(email)) AS email FROM customers WHERE … UNION SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM newsletter ORDER BY email`, with one `ORDER BY` at the very end.
--- check result | Eight addresses, each once
ordered
[["ada@mail.com"], ["ben@mail.com"], ["dev@work.io"], ["eve@mail.com"], ["finn@work.io"], ["gus@post.org"], ["max@post.org"], ["zoe@mail.com"]]

+++ practice | Who bought from which category
--- task
Return every **different** pair of a customer's `name` and a product `category` they bought from, in any order of any status. A customer who bought two stationery items still gets one stationery row. Sort by `name`, then `category`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, p.category
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
ORDER BY c.name, p.category;
--- solution
SELECT DISTINCT c.name, p.category
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
ORDER BY c.name, p.category;
--- hint
Run the starter: Ada has stationery twice, from two different orders. The rows repeat because every item makes a row.
--- hint
`SELECT DISTINCT` keeps each different (name, category) row once.
--- check result | Ten pairs; Ada and Ben bought from all three categories, Eve from one
ordered
[["Ada", "electronics"], ["Ada", "home"], ["Ada", "stationery"], ["Ben", "electronics"], ["Ben", "home"], ["Ben", "stationery"], ["Cleo", "stationery"], ["Dev", "electronics"], ["Dev", "home"], ["Eve", "home"]]
--- check source | Uses DISTINCT
[Ss][Ee][Ll][Ee][Cc][Tt]\s+[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source absent | Does not type a category
'(electronics|home|stationery)'

+++ practice | A feed of March
--- task
Build a feed of what happened in March 2024 from two tables, with two columns:

- `at`: when it happened
- `event`: what happened

From `customers`, every customer who joined in March: `at` is `joined`, and `event` is their name followed by `' joined'`, like `Dev joined`. From `orders`, every order placed in March, any status: `at` is `placed`, and `event` is `'order '` followed by its id and `' placed'`, like `order 2 placed`.

Stack the two with `UNION ALL` and sort the whole feed by `at`.
--- starter
SELECT joined AS at, name AS event
FROM customers;
--- solution
SELECT joined AS at, name || ' joined' AS event
FROM customers
WHERE joined LIKE '2024-03%'
UNION ALL
SELECT placed, 'order ' || id || ' placed'
FROM orders
WHERE placed LIKE '2024-03%'
ORDER BY at;
--- hint
Each `SELECT` needs its own `WHERE`: a filter on the first one does nothing to the second.
--- hint
Both sides must give two columns, in the same order. The names come from the first side. One `ORDER BY at` goes at the very end.
--- check result | order 1, Dev joined, order 2, order 3, in time order
ordered
[["2024-03-02 10:15:00", "order 1 placed"], ["2024-03-11", "Dev joined"], ["2024-03-20 18:40:00", "order 2 placed"], ["2024-03-21 09:05:00", "order 3 placed"]]
--- check source | Stacks with UNION ALL
[Uu][Nn][Ii][Oo][Nn]\s+[Aa][Ll][Ll]
--- check source absent | Does not type an event
'(Dev joined|order \d)

+++ practice | Addresses per domain, from both lists
--- task
Count the **different** email addresses per domain, across `customers` and `newsletter` together. Clean every address with `lower(trim(email))` first, so the same person on both lists counts once. Return two columns:

- `domain`: the part after the `@`
- `addresses`: how many different cleaned addresses have that domain

Leave out customers with no email. Name the combined list of addresses as a step with `WITH`, then group it. Sort by `addresses`, highest first, then by `domain`.
--- starter
SELECT email FROM customers
UNION ALL
SELECT email FROM newsletter;
--- solution
WITH addrs AS (
  SELECT lower(trim(email)) AS email FROM customers WHERE email IS NOT NULL
  UNION
  SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM newsletter
)
SELECT substr(email, instr(email, '@') + 1) AS domain, COUNT(*) AS addresses
FROM addrs
GROUP BY domain
ORDER BY addresses DESC, domain;
--- hint
First build one clean list with each address once: two cleaned `SELECT`s joined by `UNION`, which removes repeats.
--- hint
Put that list in a step, `WITH addrs AS (...)`. Then select from `addrs`, cut the domain out with `substr(email, instr(email, '@') + 1)`, and group by it.
--- check result | mail.com 4, post.org 2, work.io 2
ordered
[["mail.com", 4], ["post.org", 2], ["work.io", 2]]
--- check source | Stacks with UNION
[Uu][Nn][Ii][Oo][Nn]
--- check source | Names the list with WITH
[Ww][Ii][Tt][Hh]\s+\w+\s+[Aa][Ss]\s*\(

+++ practice | Two identical payments
--- schema
CREATE TABLE card_payments (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  amount REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO card_payments (order_id, amount) VALUES
  (1, 30.0), (1, 30.0), (2, 50.0), (3, 20.0);
CREATE TABLE cash_payments (
  order_id INTEGER NOT NULL,
  amount REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO cash_payments (order_id, amount) VALUES
  (1, 30.0), (3, 5.0), (4, 12.5);
--- task
This problem has its own database: `card_payments` and `cash_payments`, each with `order_id` and `amount`. Order 1 was paid in three instalments of 30.00: two by card and one in cash. Rows that look identical are still separate, real payments.

Return one row per order with three columns:

- `order_id`
- `payments`: how many payments it received, from both tables together
- `paid`: the total amount paid

Name the combined list of payments as a step with `WITH`. Sort by `order_id`.
--- starter
SELECT order_id, amount
FROM card_payments;
--- solution
WITH all_payments AS (
  SELECT order_id, amount FROM card_payments
  UNION ALL
  SELECT order_id, amount FROM cash_payments
)
SELECT order_id, COUNT(*) AS payments, SUM(amount) AS paid
FROM all_payments
GROUP BY order_id
ORDER BY order_id;
--- hint
Stack the two tables into one list inside `WITH all_payments AS (...)`, then group that list by `order_id`.
--- hint
`UNION` would remove rows that match exactly, even real ones, and order 1 would show one payment of 30.0. When every row counts, stack with `UNION ALL`.
--- check result | Order 1 has 3 payments and 90.0; orders 2, 3 and 4 have 50.0, 25.0 and 12.5
ordered
[[1, 3, 90.0], [2, 1, 50.0], [3, 2, 25.0], [4, 1, 12.5]]

+++ practice | Where each contact came from
--- task
Build one contact list from `customers` and `newsletter`, with every address cleaned by `lower(trim(email))` and customers with no email left out. Return one row per **different** address, with two columns:

- `email`: the cleaned address
- `source`: `'both'` if the address is on both lists, otherwise `'customer'` or `'newsletter'`, whichever list it came from

An address that is on the newsletter twice, in different spellings, is still only a newsletter address. Sort by `email`.

Stack the two lists with `UNION ALL` inside a `WITH` step, keeping a column that says which list each row came from. Then group by address.
--- starter
SELECT lower(trim(email)) AS email FROM customers WHERE email IS NOT NULL
UNION
SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM newsletter
ORDER BY email;
--- solution
WITH everyone AS (
  SELECT lower(trim(email)) AS email, 'customer' AS source FROM customers WHERE email IS NOT NULL
  UNION ALL
  SELECT lower(trim(email)), 'newsletter' FROM newsletter
)
SELECT email,
       CASE WHEN COUNT(DISTINCT source) = 2 THEN 'both' ELSE MIN(source) END AS source
FROM everyone
GROUP BY email
ORDER BY email;
--- hint
Tag each row with where it came from: a fixed text column, `'customer' AS source` on one side and `'newsletter'` on the other.
--- hint
Group the tagged rows by `email`. An address on both lists has two different sources: `COUNT(DISTINCT source) = 2`.
--- hint
When there is only one source, `MIN(source)` gives it: the smallest of one value is that value.
--- check result | ada and ben both; dev, eve, finn and gus customer; max and zoe newsletter
ordered
[["ada@mail.com", "both"], ["ben@mail.com", "both"], ["dev@work.io", "customer"], ["eve@mail.com", "customer"], ["finn@work.io", "customer"], ["gus@post.org", "customer"], ["max@post.org", "newsletter"], ["zoe@mail.com", "newsletter"]]
--- check source | Stacks with UNION ALL
[Uu][Nn][Ii][Oo][Nn]\s+[Aa][Ll][Ll]
--- check source | Decides with CASE
\b[Cc][Aa][Ss][Ee]\b

=== sql2-12 | Pagination with LIMIT and OFFSET
--- teach
Last lesson you built one long mailing list. Long lists are where this lesson comes in: apps show them a page at a time. You will fetch any page with `LIMIT` and `OFFSET`, learn why the sort needs a tie-breaker, and meet a faster way to reach deep pages.

Think of search results: the first page shows results 1 to 10, and "next" shows 11 to 20. Splitting a long list into pages like this is called **[[pagination|pagination-apis]]**.

**Step 1: `OFFSET` skips rows.** You know `LIMIT n` keeps the first `n` rows. `OFFSET k` skips `k` rows first. Together, `LIMIT n OFFSET k` means "skip `k` rows, then return the next `n`". Here are all eight products, most expensive first:

| position | name | price |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Headphones | 89.00 |
| 2 | Desk lamp | 45.00 |
| 3 | Fountain pen | 32.00 |
| 4 | Planner | 18.00 |
| 5 | Bookends | 9.00 |
| 6 | Mug | 9.00 |
| 7 | USB cable | 6.50 |
| 8 | Notebook | 4.50 |

With 3 products per page, page 1 is positions 1 to 3, page 2 is 4 to 6, page 3 is 7 and 8. To reach page `p`, skip the pages before it: skip (`p` − 1) × 3 rows. Page 3 skips (3 − 1) × 3 = 6 rows:

```sql
SELECT name, price FROM products
ORDER BY price DESC, name
LIMIT 3 OFFSET 6;   -- page 3: USB cable, Notebook
```

Page 3 has only two rows. The last page is often short, and that is fine.

**Step 2: the tie-breaker.** Bookends and Mug both cost 9.00. `ORDER BY price DESC` on its own does not say which comes first, so the database [[may choose differently from one query to the next|tie-order]]. With pages of 5, Mug could be position 5 when page 1 is fetched and Bookends position 5 when page 2 is fetched. Then Bookends appears on no page, and Mug on both.

The fix is a **tie-breaker**: one more column at the end of the `ORDER BY` that settles every tie. `ORDER BY price DESC, name` means "by price, high to low, and where prices are equal, by name A to Z". Always end the `ORDER BY` with something unique, such as the name here or the `id` in general, so the order is fully decided.

**Step 3: `OFFSET` has a cost.** To skip 10,000 rows, the database still has to find those 10,000 rows in order and throw them away. It is like reaching page 500 of a book by counting pages from the start, instead of opening it at a bookmark.

**Step 4: keyset pagination.** The bookmark version is **[[keyset pagination|keyset-apis]]**: remember the last row you showed, and ask for the rows that come after it. Say the last row on page 2 was `('Mug', 9.0)`. In the order "price high to low, then name A to Z", a row comes after Mug if it is cheaper, or if it costs the same and its name comes [[later in the alphabet|text-order]]:

```sql
SELECT name, price FROM products
WHERE price < 9.0 OR (price = 9.0 AND name > 'Mug')
ORDER BY price DESC, name
LIMIT 3;
```

The brackets keep the "same price and later name" parts together. The result is USB cable and Notebook, the same as page 3 above, with nothing skipped. Keyset pagination also stays correct when rows are added while someone is paging. With `OFFSET`, a new expensive product pushes every row down one place, so the reader sees one product twice.

**Watch out:** `OFFSET` counts rows, not pages. Page 2 of 3 per page is `OFFSET 3`, not `OFFSET 2`. And without a tie-breaker, products with equal prices can repeat or vanish between pages.

::: context pagination-apis Pages in real apps
Almost every app that shows a list pages it, because sending ten thousand rows to a phone at once is slow and wasteful. Web services usually let the app ask for a page size. GitHub's public API, for example, takes a `per_page` setting, 30 by default and at most 100, and a `page` number. Behind a request like that is often a query shaped like this lesson's: `ORDER BY … LIMIT per_page OFFSET (page − 1) × per_page`.
:::

::: context tie-order Why ties have no fixed order
SQL promises exactly the order you ask for, and nothing more. When two rows tie on every `ORDER BY` column, the database may return them either way, depending on how it happened to read the rows: which index it used, how the data sits on disk, or whether several workers read the table in parallel. The same query can give a different order after the table changes or the database is upgraded. A test that passes today can fail next month, which is why the tie-breaker is a habit, not an extra.
:::

::: context keyset-apis Cursors in real services
Keyset pagination is also called the "seek method", and in web services it often appears as a **cursor**: a token that stands for "the last row you saw". Stripe's API, for example, takes a `starting_after` value holding the id of the last object on the previous page. Slack's API hands back a `next_cursor` to pass in the next request. Both let a client walk through huge lists quickly, without the service counting past every earlier row.
:::

::: context text-order How text compares
`>` and `<` work on text by comparing one character at a time from the left, using each character's code number. `'Mug' > 'Bookends'` is true because M comes after B. In SQLite's normal comparison, every capital letter comes before every lower-case letter and a space comes before both, so `'Z' < 'a'` is true. That is why the product names here, which all start with a capital, sort in plain alphabetical order, and why messy text should be cleaned before it is compared.
:::
--- task
Return **page 2** of the product list, 3 products per page. Sort by `price` from highest to lowest, with ties broken by `name` A to Z. Return `name` and `price`.
--- starter
SELECT name, price
FROM products
ORDER BY price DESC
LIMIT 3;
--- solution
SELECT name, price
FROM products
ORDER BY price DESC, name
LIMIT 3 OFFSET 3;
--- hint
Page 2 skips page 1. How many rows is that with 3 per page? That number goes after `OFFSET`.
--- hint
Bookends and Mug cost the same. Add `name` after `price DESC` in the `ORDER BY` so their order is decided.
--- hint
The ending is `ORDER BY price DESC, name LIMIT 3 OFFSET 3`.
--- check result | Planner, Bookends, Mug — in that order
ordered
[["Planner", 18.0], ["Bookends", 9.0], ["Mug", 9.0]]

FROM products

FROM products
