+++ practice | Page two of the biggest orders
--- task
Rank every order by its total, the sum of `qty * unit_price` over its items, highest first, with ties broken by order id. With 3 orders per page, return **page 2**, with two columns:

- `order_id`
- `total`: rounded to 2 decimals
--- starter
SELECT order_id, qty * unit_price AS total
FROM order_items
LIMIT 3;
--- solution
SELECT order_id, ROUND(SUM(qty * unit_price), 2) AS total
FROM order_items
GROUP BY order_id
ORDER BY SUM(qty * unit_price) DESC, order_id
LIMIT 3 OFFSET 3;
--- hint
First build the full ranking: one row per order with `GROUP BY order_id`, sorted by the sum, highest first, then by `order_id`.
--- hint
Then fetch page 2: `LIMIT 3 OFFSET 3`. `LIMIT` and `OFFSET` work on the grouped rows.
--- check result | Order 7 (45.0), order 5 (37.8), order 3 (32.0)
ordered
[[7, 45.0], [5, 37.8], [3, 32.0]]
--- check source | Uses OFFSET
[Oo][Ff][Ff][Ss][Ee][Tt]
--- check source absent | Does not pick orders by id
order_id\s*(=|[Ii][Nn])\s*\(?\s*\d

+++ practice | A tie across the page break
--- schema
CREATE TABLE players (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  score INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO players (id, name, score) VALUES
  (1, 'Zed', 90), (2, 'Mia', 85), (3, 'Ali', 85), (4, 'Kai', 85), (5, 'Bea', 80),
  (6, 'Uma', 80), (7, 'Leo', 75), (8, 'Noa', 70), (9, 'Eli', 70), (10, 'Oz', 60);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `players`, with `id`, `name` and `score`.

The leaderboard is sorted by `score`, highest first. Players with the same score are sorted by `name` A to Z. It shows 3 players per page. Return **page 2**, with the columns `name` and `score`.

Three players share 85, and the page break falls between them, so the tie-breaker decides who is on which page.
--- starter
SELECT name, score
FROM players;
--- solution
SELECT name, score
FROM players
ORDER BY score DESC, name
LIMIT 3 OFFSET 3;
--- hint
Sort by `score DESC` alone, and nothing decides the order of Mia, Ali and Kai. Add `name` after it in the `ORDER BY`.
--- hint
Page 2 skips one page of 3 rows: `LIMIT 3 OFFSET 3`.
--- check result | Mia (85), Bea (80) and Uma (80)
ordered
[["Mia", 85], ["Bea", 80], ["Uma", 80]]
--- check source | Breaks ties by name
[Oo][Rr][Dd][Ee][Rr]\s+[Bb][Yy]\s+score\s+[Dd][Ee][Ss][Cc]\s*,\s*name
--- check source absent | Does not type a name
'(Mia|Bea|Uma)'

+++ practice | Page three starts too late
--- task
This query should return **page 3** of the product list, 2 products per page, sorted by `name` A to Z. It returns Planner and USB cable, which are on page 4. Page 3 is Mug and Notebook.

Find the wrong number and fix it.
--- starter
SELECT name
FROM products
ORDER BY name
LIMIT 2 OFFSET 6;
--- solution
SELECT name
FROM products
ORDER BY name
LIMIT 2 OFFSET 4;
--- hint
`OFFSET` is how many rows to skip. To reach page 3, how many *pages* do you skip?
--- hint
Skip the 2 pages before it: (3 − 1) × 2 = 4 rows, not 3 × 2.
--- check result | Mug and Notebook
ordered
[["Mug"], ["Notebook"]]
--- check source | Skips 4 rows
[Oo][Ff][Ff][Ss][Ee][Tt]\s+4\b
--- check source absent | Does not type a name
'(Mug|Notebook)'

+++ practice | Scrolling back through the log
--- schema
CREATE TABLE events (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  at TEXT NOT NULL,
  msg TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO events (id, at, msg) VALUES
  (1, '2024-06-01 09:00:00', 'boot'),
  (2, '2024-06-01 09:30:00', 'sensors on'),
  (3, '2024-06-01 10:00:00', 'burn start'),
  (4, '2024-06-01 10:00:00', 'valve open'),
  (5, '2024-06-01 09:30:00', 'radio on'),
  (6, '2024-06-01 11:00:00', 'burn end'),
  (7, '2024-06-01 10:00:00', 'gimbal set'),
  (8, '2024-06-01 10:00:00', 'pump on'),
  (9, '2024-06-01 08:00:00', 'power up');
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `events`, with `id`, `at` (a timestamp) and `msg`. Several events share the same second.

A screen shows the log **newest first**: by `at` from latest to earliest, and among events with the same `at`, by `id` from highest to lowest. It shows 3 events at a time. The last event on the screen now is event 7, at `'2024-06-01 10:00:00'`.

Return the next 3 events with keyset pagination, starting right after event 7 in this order. Do not use `OFFSET`. Return `id`, `at` and `msg`.
--- starter
SELECT id, at, msg
FROM events
WHERE at < '2024-06-01 10:00:00'
ORDER BY at DESC
LIMIT 3;
--- solution
SELECT id, at, msg
FROM events
WHERE at < '2024-06-01 10:00:00'
   OR (at = '2024-06-01 10:00:00' AND id < 7)
ORDER BY at DESC, id DESC
LIMIT 3;
--- hint
Run the starter: it jumps straight to 09:30 and skips events 4 and 3, which share event 7's second but come after it in this order.
--- hint
In a newest-first order, "after event 7" means an earlier time, or the same time and a smaller id: `at < '...' OR (at = '...' AND id < 7)`.
--- hint
The `ORDER BY` needs the tie-breaker too: `ORDER BY at DESC, id DESC`.
--- check result | Events 4 and 3 (still 10:00), then event 5 (09:30)
ordered
[[4, "2024-06-01 10:00:00", "valve open"], [3, "2024-06-01 10:00:00", "burn start"], [5, "2024-06-01 09:30:00", "radio on"]]
--- check source | Breaks ties by id, highest first
[Ii][Dd]\s+[Dd][Ee][Ss][Cc]
--- check source absent | Does not use OFFSET
[Oo][Ff][Ff][Ss][Ee][Tt]

=== sql2-13 | Changing data with conditions
--- teach
Last lesson you showed products a page at a time. Every query in this course so far has only *read* the shop's data. In the basics you also *changed* data with `UPDATE` and `DELETE`. This lesson lets you change exactly the right rows, even when the rule for "the right rows" lives in another table. It also shows how to fill a table with rows copied out of a query.

### The picture

Picture a class register and a note from the bus company: "Bus 4 was late today." You do not mark the whole class late. You look up which students ride bus 4, and you mark only those. The rule, "rides bus 4", comes from a second list.

A database works the same way. The **`WHERE` clause** is the part of an `UPDATE` or `DELETE` that picks which rows change. It can use everything you have learned so far, including a question put to another table.

### A quick reminder

```sql
UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped' WHERE id = 8;
```

`UPDATE orders` names the table. `SET status = 'shipped'` says what to write. `WHERE id = 8` says which rows get it. Leave out the `WHERE` and **every** row in the table changes.

### Step 1: a rule that lives in another table

Say the shop wants to cancel every `paid` order from a customer who has no email on file, because it cannot send them a receipt.

Whether a customer has an email is stored in `customers`. The orders are in `orders`. A **[[subquery|subquery-recap]]** (a query inside brackets, inside another query) builds the list of customers with no email:

```sql
SELECT id FROM customers WHERE email IS NULL;
```

| id |
| --- |
| 3 |

Only Cleo, id 3, has no email. `IN (…)` means "is one of the values in this list", and `AND` means "both must be true". So the whole change reads:

```sql
UPDATE orders SET status = 'canceled'
WHERE status = 'paid'
  AND customer_id IN (SELECT id FROM customers WHERE email IS NULL);
```

Read the `WHERE` aloud: "the status is paid, and the customer is on the no-email list". Only order 5 passes both tests, so only order 5 changes.

### Step 2: a new value worked out from the old one

`SET` can calculate. It reads each row's own current value, works out the new one, and writes it back. Here is 10% off every `home` product. `*` means "times", and multiplying by 0.9 keeps 90% of the price:

```sql
UPDATE products
SET price = ROUND(price * 0.9, 2)
WHERE category = 'home';
```

| name | price before | price after |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Desk lamp | 45.00 | 40.50 |
| Mug | 9.00 | 8.10 |
| Bookends | 9.00 | 8.10 |

Each row used its own price. To raise a price by 10% instead, multiply by 1.1: the old price plus a tenth of it.

`ROUND(x, 2)` means "round x to 2 decimal places". Prices here are stored as [[floating-point numbers|floating-point]], which can come out of arithmetic with a long tail of digits. Round what you store, so the price stays in whole cents.

### Step 3: new rows copied from a query

`INSERT` usually takes its rows from `VALUES (…)`. It can take them from a `SELECT` instead: every row the query returns becomes a new row in the table.

```sql
INSERT INTO newsletter (email, signed_up)
SELECT lower(trim(email)), date('now') FROM customers WHERE city = 'Paris';
```

The `SELECT` part on its own returns two rows:

| lower(trim(email)) | date('now') |
| --- | --- |
| ben@mail.com | today's date |
| gus@post.org | today's date |

The columns line up by position: the first result column goes into `email`, the second into `signed_up`. `lower(trim(…))` comes from the lesson Working with text, and `date('now')` from Dates and times. This is a common way of [[copying rows between tables|insert-select]].

### Step 4: the safe routine

A database has no undo button: once a change runs, the old values are gone unless you planned a way back in advance. So every change follows the same three steps:

1. Write a `SELECT` with the exact `WHERE` you plan to use. Check it returns exactly the rows you mean.
2. Change `SELECT …` to `UPDATE … SET …` or `DELETE`, keeping the `WHERE` word for word.
3. Query again to confirm the effect.

For the cancel rule, step 1 is:

```sql
SELECT id, status FROM orders
WHERE status = 'paid'
  AND customer_id IN (SELECT id FROM customers WHERE email IS NULL);
```

| id | status |
| --- | --- |
| 5 | paid |

One row, as intended. Now step 2 is the `UPDATE` from Step 1, and step 3 is `SELECT id, status FROM orders WHERE id = 5;`, which should say `canceled`. Step 1 is a [[dry run|dry-run]]: a rehearsal that changes nothing.

### Step 5: a subquery on the same table

A subquery may read the very table you are changing. It [[sees the table as it was|before-snapshot]] before the statement started, not half-way through:

```sql
UPDATE products SET price = price - 1
WHERE price > (SELECT AVG(price) FROM products);
```

`AVG` works out the average of the eight prices once, from the prices before any change: 26.625. The Fountain pen (32.00), the Desk lamp (45.00) and the Headphones (89.00) are above it, so those three each drop by 1.

**Watch out:** text that looks equal is not always equal. The customer Ben's email is stored as `'BEN@Mail.com'`, but the newsletter has `'ben@mail.com'`. So `WHERE email IN (SELECT email FROM customers)` only matches Ada's address. Put `lower(trim(…))` on **both** sides of the comparison. Also keep NULL emails out of the list with `WHERE email IS NOT NULL`: a NULL inside a list can do real damage, as the lesson Problem solving: finding what is missing shows.

::: context subquery-recap Where you met subqueries
The basics showed a subquery giving one value, like `(SELECT AVG(cost_usd) FROM requests)`. The lesson Subqueries: a value, or a list added the second kind: a subquery that returns a whole column, used after `IN`. That is the kind in Step 1. The database runs the inner query, gets its list (here only the number 3), and then checks each order's `customer_id` against it. You can always run the inner query on its own first, which is exactly what the safe routine asks you to do.
:::

::: context floating-point Why prices get long tails
A **floating-point** number is how a computer stores a number with a decimal point. It stores it in binary (base 2), and most decimals, like 0.1, cannot be written exactly in binary, the same way 1/3 cannot be written exactly in decimal (0.3333…). The tiny errors usually hide, but sometimes they show: in SQLite, `SELECT 0.1 + 0.2 = 0.3;` returns 0, which means false. Rounding to 2 places after each calculation keeps prices tidy. Many real payment systems avoid the problem entirely by storing money as a whole number of cents in an `INTEGER` column: 32.00 becomes 3200.
:::

::: context insert-select How engineers use INSERT … SELECT
Copying rows with a query is everyday work. A common nightly job copies last month's rows from a busy table into an archive table, then deletes them from the busy one, so the table the app reads stays small. Mission teams do the same with telemetry: raw sensor readings pour into one table, and a scheduled `INSERT … SELECT` writes one summary row per minute into another, which the charts then read. The query decides what goes in, so everything you know about `WHERE`, joins and `GROUP BY` works here too.
:::

::: context dry-run The safe routine, as a picture
The `WHERE` is written once and never retyped between the rehearsal and the real change. That is what makes the rehearsal count.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="arr" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <rect x="6" y="20" width="100" height="56" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="56" y="42" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1. SELECT</text>
  <text x="56" y="60" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">look at the rows</text>
  <rect x="130" y="20" width="100" height="56" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="42" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2. UPDATE</text>
  <text x="180" y="60" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">same WHERE</text>
  <rect x="254" y="20" width="100" height="56" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="304" y="42" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3. SELECT</text>
  <text x="304" y="60" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">check the effect</text>
  <line x1="106" y1="48" x2="128" y2="48" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arr)"/>
  <line x1="230" y1="48" x2="252" y2="48" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#arr)"/>
  <text x="180" y="110" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">WHERE status = 'paid' AND customer_id IN (…)</text>
</svg>
```

The advanced course adds two safety nets: the lesson Transactions: all or nothing lets you undo a change that failed its check, and the lesson RETURNING shows the changed rows as part of the change itself.
:::

::: context before-snapshot Why the average does not drift
Imagine the database recomputed the average after each row changed. The Fountain pen drops to 31.00, so the average falls a little, and that might pull a different product over the line. The result would then depend on which row happened to be visited first. SQL avoids that by treating a statement as if every row changed at the same instant: all the conditions are judged against the table as it stood before the statement began. Here that average is the sum of the eight prices, 213.00, divided by 8, which is 26.625.
:::
--- task
Make two changes, one after the other:

1. Raise the price of every product whose `category` is `'stationery'` by 10%, rounded to 2 decimal places.
2. Delete every row of `newsletter` whose email belongs to a customer: they already hear from the shop. Compare the emails lower-cased and trimmed on **both** sides (`lower(trim(email))`), and leave out customers whose `email` is NULL.
--- starter
-- 1. Raise stationery prices by 10%.

-- 2. Remove subscribers who are already customers.
--- solution
UPDATE products
SET price = ROUND(price * 1.1, 2)
WHERE category = 'stationery';

DELETE FROM newsletter
WHERE lower(trim(email)) IN (
  SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM customers WHERE email IS NOT NULL
);
--- hint
Write two statements, each ending in `;`. The first is like Step 2, with `* 1.1` for "plus 10%" and `category = 'stationery'` in the `WHERE`.
--- hint
The first is `UPDATE products SET price = ROUND(price * 1.1, 2) WHERE category = 'stationery';`. The second is a `DELETE FROM newsletter` whose `WHERE` uses `IN (SELECT … FROM customers …)`, as in Step 1.
--- hint
`DELETE FROM newsletter WHERE lower(trim(email)) IN (SELECT lower(trim(email)) FROM customers WHERE email IS NOT NULL);` Both sides are cleaned the same way, so `'BEN@Mail.com'` and `'ben@mail.com'` match.
--- check query | Stationery prices went up 10%
SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE category = 'stationery' ORDER BY id
=> [["Notebook", 4.95], ["Fountain pen", 35.2], ["Planner", 19.8]]
--- check query | Other prices did not change
SELECT SUM(price) FROM products WHERE category <> 'stationery'
=> [[158.5]]
--- check query | Only non-customers are left on the newsletter
SELECT email FROM newsletter ORDER BY signed_up
=> [["zoe@mail.com"], ["Zoe@mail.com "], ["max@post.org"]]

+++ practice | Ship the London orders
--- task
Mark as `'shipped'` every order whose `status` is `'paid'` and whose customer lives in London (`city` is `'London'`). Use one `UPDATE` with a subquery that lists the London customers' ids. Leave every other order as it is.
--- starter
-- Mark London's paid orders as shipped.
--- solution
UPDATE orders
SET status = 'shipped'
WHERE status = 'paid'
  AND customer_id IN (SELECT id FROM customers WHERE city = 'London');
--- hint
Run the dry run first: `SELECT id, status FROM orders WHERE status = 'paid' AND customer_id IN (SELECT id FROM customers WHERE city = 'London');` It should return one order.
--- hint
Keep that `WHERE` word for word, and turn the `SELECT ...` into `UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped'`.
--- check query | Order 8, Ada's, is now shipped
SELECT id, status FROM orders ORDER BY id
=> [[1, "shipped"], [2, "shipped"], [3, "canceled"], [4, "shipped"], [5, "paid"], [6, "shipped"], [7, "refunded"], [8, "shipped"]]
--- check query | Order 5 stays paid: Cleo has no city
SELECT status FROM orders WHERE id = 5
=> [["paid"]]
--- check query | Five orders are shipped now
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders WHERE status = 'shipped'
=> [[5]]

+++ practice | A table of big spenders
--- task
Make a new table and fill it from a query:

1. Create a table `vip` with two columns: `customer_id` (an `INTEGER`) and `total` (a `REAL`).
2. Fill it with one row for every customer whose **shipped** orders add up to 100 or more: their `customer_id`, and `total`, the sum of `qty * unit_price` over their shipped orders, rounded to 2 decimals.

Use `INSERT INTO vip ... SELECT`, not typed values.
--- starter
-- 1. Create the vip table.

-- 2. Fill it from a query.
--- solution
CREATE TABLE vip (
  customer_id INTEGER,
  total REAL
);

INSERT INTO vip (customer_id, total)
SELECT o.customer_id, ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2)
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.status = 'shipped'
GROUP BY o.customer_id
HAVING SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) >= 100;
--- hint
Write the `SELECT` on its own first: shipped orders joined to their items, grouped by `o.customer_id`, keeping the groups with `HAVING SUM(...) >= 100`.
--- hint
Then put `INSERT INTO vip (customer_id, total)` in front of it. The columns line up by position.
--- check query | Ada (102.6) and Dev (107.0) are in the table
SELECT customer_id, total FROM vip ORDER BY customer_id
=> [[1, 102.6], [4, 107.0]]
--- check query | Ben, with 58.00 shipped, is not
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM vip WHERE customer_id = 2
=> [[0]]
--- check query | Exactly two rows
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM vip
=> [[2]]
--- check source | Fills the table from a SELECT
[Ii][Nn][Ss][Ee][Rr][Tt]\s+[Ii][Nn][Tt][Oo]\s+vip[^;]*[Ss][Ee][Ll][Ee][Cc][Tt]

+++ practice | Catch up with the highest price paid
--- task
Some products have been sold for more than their current list price. For every product whose highest `unit_price` in `order_items` is **above** its `price`, set its `price` to that highest `unit_price`. Products that never sold, or never sold above their price, keep their price.

Use one `UPDATE`. Inside a subquery, you can refer to the row being updated as `products.id`.
--- starter
-- Raise a product's price to the highest price it has sold for, when that is higher.
--- solution
UPDATE products
SET price = (SELECT MAX(oi.unit_price) FROM order_items oi WHERE oi.product_id = products.id)
WHERE (SELECT MAX(oi.unit_price) FROM order_items oi WHERE oi.product_id = products.id) > price;
--- hint
The highest price a product sold for is a correlated subquery: `(SELECT MAX(oi.unit_price) FROM order_items oi WHERE oi.product_id = products.id)`.
--- hint
Use it twice: in `SET price = ...`, and in the `WHERE`, compared with `price`. A product that never sold gets NULL from the subquery, and `NULL > price` is not true, so it is left alone.
--- check query | The Notebook, sold once at 5.50, now costs 5.5
SELECT price FROM products WHERE name = 'Notebook'
=> [[5.5]]
--- check query | Every other price is unchanged
SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE name <> 'Notebook' ORDER BY id
=> [["Fountain pen", 32.0], ["Desk lamp", 45.0], ["Mug", 9.0], ["Headphones", 89.0], ["USB cable", 6.5], ["Planner", 18.0], ["Bookends", 9.0]]
--- check query | No price became NULL
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM products WHERE price IS NULL
=> [[0]]

+++ practice | A base from your mentor
--- schema
CREATE TABLE crew (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  base TEXT,
  mentor_id INTEGER
);
INSERT INTO crew (id, name, base, mentor_id) VALUES
  (1, 'Ana', 'Houston', NULL),
  (2, 'Ben', NULL,      1),
  (3, 'Cai', NULL,      5),
  (4, 'Dee', 'Cologne', 1),
  (5, 'Eli', NULL,      NULL),
  (6, 'Fay', NULL,      9);
--- task
This problem has its own database: one table, `crew`, with `id`, `name`, `base` (NULL if not assigned yet) and `mentor_id`, the `id` of the person who trains them (or NULL).

Give every crew member who has **no** base the base of their mentor. Use one `UPDATE`; inside a subquery, refer to the row being updated as `crew.mentor_id`.

Watch the edges:

- Someone who already has a base keeps it, even if their mentor works somewhere else.
- Someone with no mentor, or whose mentor (id 9) is not in the table, stays NULL.
- Someone whose mentor has no base either, like Cai, whose mentor is Eli, stays NULL.
--- starter
-- Fill in missing bases from each person's mentor.
--- solution
UPDATE crew
SET base = (SELECT m.base FROM crew m WHERE m.id = crew.mentor_id)
WHERE base IS NULL;
--- hint
The mentor's base is `(SELECT m.base FROM crew m WHERE m.id = crew.mentor_id)`. When no mentor is found, it gives NULL, which is the right answer.
--- hint
Without a `WHERE`, Dee's Cologne would be overwritten by Ana's Houston. Only change rows `WHERE base IS NULL`.
--- check query | Ben gets Houston; Dee keeps Cologne; Cai, Eli and Fay stay NULL
SELECT id, base FROM crew ORDER BY id
=> [[1, "Houston"], [2, "Houston"], [3, null], [4, "Cologne"], [5, null], [6, null]]
--- check query | Three people still have no base
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM crew WHERE base IS NULL
=> [[3]]
--- check query | Dee still works from Cologne
SELECT base FROM crew WHERE name = 'Dee'
=> [["Cologne"]]

+++ practice | The sale that reached the mugs
--- task
The shop wants 10% off every product in the `'home'` or `'electronics'` category that costs **more than 20**, rounded to 2 decimals. This statement ran, and it also cut the price of the Mug and the Bookends, which cost 9.00.

Fix the `WHERE` so only the right products change. Keep it one `UPDATE`.
--- starter
UPDATE products
SET price = ROUND(price * 0.9, 2)
WHERE category = 'home' OR category = 'electronics' AND price > 20;
--- solution
UPDATE products
SET price = ROUND(price * 0.9, 2)
WHERE (category = 'home' OR category = 'electronics') AND price > 20;
--- hint
`AND` is worked out before `OR`, like multiplication before addition. So the starter means: home, **or** (electronics and more than 20).
--- hint
Put brackets around the two categories: `(category = 'home' OR category = 'electronics') AND price > 20`. `category IN ('home', 'electronics')` works too.
--- check query | Desk lamp 40.5 and Headphones 80.1; everything else unchanged
SELECT name, price FROM products ORDER BY id
=> [["Notebook", 4.5], ["Fountain pen", 32.0], ["Desk lamp", 40.5], ["Mug", 9.0], ["Headphones", 80.1], ["USB cable", 6.5], ["Planner", 18.0], ["Bookends", 9.0]]
--- check query | The Mug and the Bookends still cost 9.00
SELECT price FROM products WHERE name IN ('Mug', 'Bookends')
=> [[9.0], [9.0]]
--- check query | The USB cable (6.50) is untouched
SELECT price FROM products WHERE name = 'USB cable'
=> [[6.5]]

+++ practice | A monthly summary table
--- task
Build a summary table and tidy it, in three statements:

1. Create a table `monthly` with three columns: `month` (`TEXT`), `orders` (`INTEGER`) and `revenue` (`REAL`).
2. Fill it with one row per month from the orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`: `month` as `'YYYY-MM'` with `strftime`, `orders` the number of different orders, and `revenue` the sum of `qty * unit_price`, rounded to 2 decimals.
3. Delete the months with `revenue` under 50.

Leave the shop's own tables unchanged.
--- starter
-- 1. Create the table.

-- 2. Fill it.

-- 3. Remove the small months.
--- solution
CREATE TABLE monthly (
  month TEXT,
  orders INTEGER,
  revenue REAL
);

INSERT INTO monthly (month, orders, revenue)
SELECT strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed),
       COUNT(DISTINCT o.id),
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2)
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed);

DELETE FROM monthly WHERE revenue < 50;
--- hint
Step 2 is an `INSERT INTO monthly (month, orders, revenue)` followed by a grouped `SELECT`, like the monthly report in the lesson "Dates and times".
--- hint
After a join with `order_items`, count orders with `COUNT(DISTINCT o.id)`. Step 3 is a plain `DELETE FROM monthly WHERE revenue < 50;`.
--- check query | March, April and May are left; June (25.00) is gone
SELECT month, orders, revenue FROM monthly ORDER BY month
=> [["2024-03", 2, 102.6], ["2024-04", 2, 95.8], ["2024-05", 1, 107.0]]
--- check query | Three months in the table
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM monthly
=> [[3]]
--- check query | The orders table still has all 8 orders
SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders
=> [[8]]

=== sql2-14 | Debugging: the join that double-counts
--- teach
Last lesson you changed rows on purpose. This lesson is a bug hunt, like the lesson Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear. That time a query returned too few rows. This time every row is there, but some totals are too big. You will see why a join can copy rows, and how to build a report where that cannot happen.

### The picture

A school survey puts each student's pets and each student's cousins on one sheet, with one line for every pet-and-cousin pair. A student with 2 pets and 3 cousins fills 2 × 3 = 6 lines. Count the pets on that sheet and you get 6, not 2: every pet was written down once per cousin.

### The bug report

Finance runs this report: what each customer's orders were worth (`items_total`), and how much each customer paid (`paid_total`).

```sql
SELECT c.name,
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS items_total,
       ROUND(SUM(p.amount), 2) AS paid_total
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN payments p ON p.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id;
```

| name | items_total | paid_total |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Ada | 127.6 | 175.1 |
| Ben | 148.0 | 116.0 |
| Cleo | 37.8 | 75.6 |
| Dev | 107.0 | 214.0 |
| Eve | 90.0 | 0.0 |

Ada's items do come to 127.60. But Ada's three payments are 22.50 + 80.10 + 25.00 = 127.60, and the report says 175.10. Ben's numbers are both too big: really 90.00 of items and 58.00 paid. The query runs without any error. It is still wrong.

### Step 1: shrink it to one wrong number

Pick one wrong number and [[shrink the query|minimal-case]] down to it. Add `WHERE c.name = 'Ada'` before the `GROUP BY`. Five rows are far easier to read than a whole report.

### Step 2: take the SUM away and look at the raw rows

A `SUM` hides the rows it adds up. Remove it and select the pieces instead:

```sql
SELECT o.id AS order_id, oi.product_id, oi.qty * oi.unit_price AS item_total,
       p.id AS payment_id, p.amount
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN payments p ON p.order_id = o.id
WHERE c.name = 'Ada';
```

| order_id | product_id | item_total | payment_id | amount |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1 | 13.5 | 1 | 22.5 |
| 1 | 4 | 9.0 | 1 | 22.5 |
| 2 | 5 | 80.1 | 2 | 80.1 |
| 8 | 6 | 19.5 | 9 | 25.0 |
| 8 | 1 | 5.5 | 9 | 25.0 |

There it is. Payment 1 appears twice, and so does payment 9. Add the `amount` column: 22.5 + 22.5 + 80.1 + 25.0 + 25.0 = 175.1, the wrong total.

### Step 3: name the cause

This is **[[fan-out|fan-out-picture]]**: a join turning one row into several. You met the word in the lesson GROUP BY in depth.

Order 1 has 2 items. Joining `order_items` turns order 1 into 2 rows, one per item. The payments join then gives **each** of those rows the order's payment, so payment 1 is copied onto both.

Now both at once. Ben's order 4 has 2 items **and** 2 payments. Every item is paired with every payment: 2 × 2 = 4 rows. `SUM` then adds each payment once per item and each item once per payment:

- items: 32.00 from order 3, plus 2 × (45.00 + 13.00) from order 4 = 148.00
- paid: 2 × (30.00 + 28.00) = 116.00

Counting rows per order shows the pattern at a glance: add `COUNT(*)` and `GROUP BY o.id` to the raw query. Orders 1, 5, 6, 7 and 8 give 2 rows each and order 4 gives 4, where one row per order would be honest. Order 7 has one item and two payments (45.00 and the refund of −45.00), which is why Eve's items doubled to 90.0.

### Step 4: a shortcut that does not work

`SUM(DISTINCT p.amount)` adds each *different* value only once. It would fix Ada's number today. But two real payments of the same amount, say two instalments of 30.00, would [[merge into one|sum-distinct]], and the total would quietly be 30.00 short.

### Step 5: the fix, one row per order first

The real fix: **add up each table to one row per order first**, then join those pieces. Here is the items step on its own:

```sql
SELECT order_id, SUM(qty * unit_price) AS items_total
FROM order_items
GROUP BY order_id;
```

It returns 8 rows, one per order: 22.5, 80.1, 32.0, 58.0, 37.8, 107.0, 45.0 and 25.0 for orders 1 to 8. The same step on `payments` returns 7 rows: order 3 was canceled and never paid, so it has no row, and order 7 nets to 0.0 (45.00 − 45.00).

In the basics, `WITH` named one step. It can hold [[several named steps|multi-step-with]], separated by commas:

```sql
WITH items AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(qty * unit_price) AS items_total
  FROM order_items GROUP BY order_id
), paid AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(amount) AS paid_total
  FROM payments GROUP BY order_id
)
SELECT o.id, i.items_total, p.paid_total
FROM orders o
JOIN items i     ON i.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN paid p ON p.order_id = o.id
ORDER BY o.id;
```

| id | items_total | paid_total |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 22.5 | 22.5 |
| 2 | 80.1 | 80.1 |
| 3 | 32.0 | NULL |
| 4 | 58.0 | 58.0 |
| 5 | 37.8 | 37.8 |
| 6 | 107.0 | 107.0 |
| 7 | 45.0 | 0.0 |
| 8 | 25.0 | 25.0 |

Each order meets exactly one `items` row and at most one `paid` row, so nothing can be copied. `paid` is a `LEFT JOIN` because order 3 has no payments: an inner join would drop order 3 and its 32.00 of items with it.

The general rule: never add up across two separate [[one-to-many|one-to-many]] joins at once. Shrink each to one row per key first.

**Watch out:** a query that runs without an error is not a query that is right. Before you trust a total, check one number by hand, as you did with Ada's payments.

::: context minimal-case Shrinking a bug
Engineers call this a **minimal reproduction**: the smallest input that still shows the bug. A report over thousands of customers hides the problem in a crowd. One customer with five rows shows it in seconds. The same habit works everywhere: a crashing program is cut down to the ten lines that still crash, and a flight-software team replays the one second of sensor data where a reading jumped. Pick a case where you already know the right answer, so you can tell the moment the query disagrees.
:::

::: context fan-out-picture Every item meets every payment
Order 4 as the broken query sees it. Each line is one result row, so the order becomes four rows instead of one.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 150" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="70" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">order_items</text>
  <text x="290" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">payments</text>
  <rect x="10" y="30" width="120" height="32" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="51" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Desk lamp 45.00</text>
  <rect x="10" y="84" width="120" height="32" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="70" y="105" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">USB cable 13.00</text>
  <rect x="240" y="30" width="100" height="32" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="290" y="51" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">30.00</text>
  <rect x="240" y="84" width="100" height="32" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="290" y="105" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">28.00</text>
  <line x1="130" y1="46" x2="240" y2="46" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="130" y1="46" x2="240" y2="100" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="130" y1="100" x2="240" y2="46" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="130" y1="100" x2="240" y2="100" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="140" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2 items × 2 payments = 4 rows</text>
</svg>
```

Summed over those four rows, the items come to 116.00 instead of 58.00, and the payments to 116.00 instead of 58.00.
:::

::: context sum-distinct Why DISTINCT is the wrong fix
`DISTINCT` inside `SUM` throws away repeated *values*, not repeated *rows*. It cannot tell a payment that was copied by a join from a second, real payment that happens to be the same amount. Try it in SQLite:

```sql
SELECT SUM(amount), SUM(DISTINCT amount)
FROM (SELECT 30.0 AS amount UNION ALL SELECT 30.0);
```

It returns 60.0 and 30.0. Two genuine instalments of 30.00 should total 60.00. The dangerous part is that the shortcut gives the right answer on today's data, so the bug waits until the day two payments match.
:::

::: context multi-step-with More than one named step
Each step in a `WITH` is a **CTE**, short for common table expression: a named query that the rest of the statement can read like a table. Steps are separated by commas, and the word `WITH` appears only once, at the top. A later step may read an earlier one. The best part for debugging: you can run any step on its own, as you did with the items step, and check its rows before you trust it. The advanced course opens with a whole lesson on CTEs.
:::

::: context one-to-many One row here, many rows there
A link is **one-to-many** when one row on one side can match many rows on the other: one order, many items; one order, many payments. Joining along one such link is fine; the grain becomes "one row per item". Joining along two separate ones from the same row multiplies them together. Real systems are full of these pairs: one flight with many sensor readings and many log messages, one app user with many logins and many purchases. The same trap comes back in the advanced course, in the lesson Debugging: the running balance that repeats.
:::
--- task
Fix the report so each customer with orders shows the correct `items_total` and `paid_total` (each over all of that customer's orders, rounded to 2 decimals). Keep the columns `name`, `items_total`, `paid_total`. A customer whose orders have no payments at all should show `0` paid.
--- starter
SELECT c.name,
       ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2) AS items_total,
       ROUND(SUM(p.amount), 2) AS paid_total
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN payments p ON p.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id;
--- solution
WITH items AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(qty * unit_price) AS items_total
  FROM order_items
  GROUP BY order_id
), paid AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(amount) AS paid_total
  FROM payments
  GROUP BY order_id
)
SELECT c.name,
       ROUND(SUM(i.items_total), 2) AS items_total,
       ROUND(COALESCE(SUM(p.paid_total), 0), 2) AS paid_total
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o    ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN items i     ON i.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN paid p ON p.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id;
--- hint
Start from the `WITH items AS (…), paid AS (…)` query in Step 5. It already gives one honest row per order.
--- hint
Add `JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id` (or start `FROM customers c` and join `orders` to it), then `GROUP BY c.id` and select `c.name`, `ROUND(SUM(i.items_total), 2)` and the paid sum.
--- hint
For the paid column, write `ROUND(COALESCE(SUM(p.paid_total), 0), 2) AS paid_total`. `COALESCE(…, 0)` turns a NULL sum into 0 for a customer with no payments at all.
--- check result | Correct totals; Ben paid 58, Eve's refund nets to 0
[["Ada", 127.6, 127.6], ["Ben", 90.0, 58.0], ["Cleo", 37.8, 37.8], ["Dev", 107.0, 107.0], ["Eve", 45.0, 0.0]]

+++ practice | Measure the fan-out
--- task
Before fixing a fan-out, measure it. Join `orders` to `order_items` and then `LEFT JOIN` `payments`, all on the order's id, and return one row per order with four columns:

- `id`: the order id
- `items`: how many **different** products the order holds
- `payments`: how many **different** payments it received (0 if none)
- `joined_rows`: how many rows this join makes for the order

Sort by `id`. Where `joined_rows` is bigger than both `items` and `payments`, every item met every payment.
--- starter
SELECT o.id, COUNT(*) AS joined_rows
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY o.id
ORDER BY o.id;
--- solution
SELECT o.id,
       COUNT(DISTINCT oi.product_id) AS items,
       COUNT(DISTINCT p.id) AS payments,
       COUNT(*) AS joined_rows
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi  ON oi.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN payments p ON p.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY o.id
ORDER BY o.id;
--- hint
Add the payments join: `LEFT JOIN payments p ON p.order_id = o.id`, so order 3, which has no payments, stays.
--- hint
`COUNT(*)` counts the joined rows. Counting ids with `DISTINCT` is honest even after a fan-out: `COUNT(DISTINCT oi.product_id)` and `COUNT(DISTINCT p.id)`.
--- check result | Order 4 has 2 items and 2 payments but 4 rows; order 3 has no payment
ordered
[[1, 2, 1, 2], [2, 1, 1, 1], [3, 1, 0, 1], [4, 2, 2, 4], [5, 2, 1, 2], [6, 2, 1, 2], [7, 1, 2, 2], [8, 2, 1, 2]]
--- check source | Uses LEFT JOIN for the payments
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]\s+payments
--- check source | Counts different payments
[Cc][Oo][Uu][Nn][Tt]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]\s+p\.id

+++ practice | Hours and people per project
--- schema
CREATE TABLE projects (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO projects (id, name) VALUES
  (1, 'Lander'), (2, 'Rover'), (3, 'Relay');
CREATE TABLE tasks (
  project_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES projects(id),
  hours INTEGER NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO tasks (project_id, hours) VALUES
  (1, 10), (1, 5), (1, 5), (2, 8);
CREATE TABLE members (
  project_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES projects(id),
  person TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO members (project_id, person) VALUES
  (1, 'Ana'), (1, 'Ben'), (2, 'Cai'), (2, 'Dee'), (2, 'Eli'), (3, 'Fay');
--- task
This problem has its own database. `projects` has `id` and `name`. `tasks` has `project_id` and `hours`, one row per task. `members` has `project_id` and `person`, one row per person on the project.

Return **every** project, with three columns:

- `name`
- `hours`: the total `hours` of its tasks, or `0` if it has none
- `members`: how many people are on it

Lander has two tasks of 5 hours each; both count. Add each table up to one row per project first, in `WITH` steps, then join the steps to `projects`. Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT pr.name, SUM(t.hours) AS hours, COUNT(m.person) AS members
FROM projects pr
LEFT JOIN tasks t   ON t.project_id = pr.id
LEFT JOIN members m ON m.project_id = pr.id
GROUP BY pr.id, pr.name
ORDER BY pr.name;
--- solution
WITH h AS (
  SELECT project_id, SUM(hours) AS hours
  FROM tasks
  GROUP BY project_id
), m AS (
  SELECT project_id, COUNT(*) AS members
  FROM members
  GROUP BY project_id
)
SELECT pr.name,
       COALESCE(h.hours, 0) AS hours,
       COALESCE(m.members, 0) AS members
FROM projects pr
LEFT JOIN h ON h.project_id = pr.id
LEFT JOIN m ON m.project_id = pr.id
ORDER BY pr.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Lander shows 40 hours and 6 members. Each of its 3 tasks met each of its 2 members.
--- hint
Make two steps, one row per project each: the hours from `tasks`, and the head count from `members`. Then `LEFT JOIN` both to `projects`.
--- hint
Relay has no tasks, so its hours come back NULL. `COALESCE(h.hours, 0)` turns that into 0.
--- check result | Lander 20 hours and 2 members, Relay 0 and 1, Rover 8 and 3
ordered
[["Lander", 20, 2], ["Relay", 0, 1], ["Rover", 8, 3]]
--- check source | Adds up first in WITH steps
[Ww][Ii][Tt][Hh]\s+\w+\s+[Aa][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not use SUM(DISTINCT), which merges the two 5-hour tasks
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]

+++ practice | Every customer, three honest numbers
--- task
Return **every** customer, with four columns:

- `name`
- `orders`: how many orders they placed, of any status
- `units`: the total `qty` over all their orders' items, or `0`
- `paid`: the total `amount` of all their orders' payments, refunds included, rounded to 2 decimals, or `0`

Finn and Gus have no orders and show 0 in all three. Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS orders, SUM(oi.qty) AS units, SUM(p.amount) AS paid
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN payments p     ON p.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
WITH units AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(qty) AS units
  FROM order_items
  GROUP BY order_id
), paid AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(amount) AS paid
  FROM payments
  GROUP BY order_id
)
SELECT c.name,
       COUNT(o.id) AS orders,
       COALESCE(SUM(u.units), 0) AS units,
       ROUND(COALESCE(SUM(p.paid), 0), 2) AS paid
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN units u  ON u.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN paid p   ON p.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
The starter hangs two one-to-many tables off each order, so both units and payments get copied. Add each one up to one row per order first.
--- hint
Two `WITH` steps: units per `order_id` from `order_items`, and paid per `order_id` from `payments`. Each order meets at most one row of each.
--- hint
Keep every customer with `LEFT JOIN`s from `customers`, and wrap the sums in `COALESCE(..., 0)`.
--- check result | Ada 3 orders, 9 units, 127.6 paid; Eve 1, 1, 0.0; Finn and Gus all 0
ordered

'(Ada|Ben|Cleo|Dev|Eve|Finn|Gus)'

+++ practice | Two lines, three instalments
--- schema
CREATE TABLE invoices (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  client TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO invoices (id, client) VALUES
  (1, 'Orbital'), (2, 'Nova'), (3, 'Astra');
CREATE TABLE lines (
  invoice_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES invoices(id),
  amount REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO lines (invoice_id, amount) VALUES
  (1, 100.0), (1, 100.0), (2, 50.0), (3, 70.0), (3, 30.0);
CREATE TABLE instalments (
  invoice_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES invoices(id),
  amount REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO instalments (invoice_id, amount) VALUES
  (1, 100.0), (1, 50.0), (1, 50.0), (3, 100.0);
--- task
This problem has its own database. `invoices` has `id` and `client`. `lines` has `invoice_id` and `amount`, one row per thing billed. `instalments` has `invoice_id` and `amount`, one row per payment.

Invoice 1 bills two identical lines of 100.00, and was paid in three instalments, two of them 50.00 each. Every one of those rows is real.

Return every invoice, with five columns:

- `id` and `client`
- `billed`: the total of its lines
- `paid`: the total of its instalments, or `0` if it has none
- `balance`: `billed` minus `paid`

Sort by `id`.
--- starter
SELECT i.id, i.client, SUM(DISTINCT l.amount) AS billed, SUM(DISTINCT s.amount) AS paid
FROM invoices i
JOIN lines l            ON l.invoice_id = i.id
LEFT JOIN instalments s ON s.invoice_id = i.id
GROUP BY i.id, i.client
ORDER BY i.id;
--- solution
WITH b AS (
  SELECT invoice_id, SUM(amount) AS billed
  FROM lines
  GROUP BY invoice_id
), p AS (
  SELECT invoice_id, SUM(amount) AS paid
  FROM instalments
  GROUP BY invoice_id
)
SELECT i.id, i.client,
       b.billed,
       COALESCE(p.paid, 0) AS paid,
       b.billed - COALESCE(p.paid, 0) AS balance
FROM invoices i
JOIN b      ON b.invoice_id = i.id
LEFT JOIN p ON p.invoice_id = i.id
ORDER BY i.id;
--- hint
Run the starter: invoice 1 shows 100.0 billed and 150.0 paid. `SUM(DISTINCT ...)` merged the equal amounts, which are separate real rows.
--- hint
Without `DISTINCT` the join copies rows instead: 2 lines × 3 instalments. The honest way is to add each table up per invoice first.
--- hint
Two `WITH` steps, billed per invoice and paid per invoice, joined to `invoices`. Invoice 2 has no instalments, so use `LEFT JOIN` and `COALESCE`.
--- check result | Orbital 200 billed and 200 paid, Nova 50 and 0 (balance 50), Astra 100 and 100
ordered
[[1, "Orbital", 200.0, 200.0, 0.0], [2, "Nova", 50.0, 0, 50.0], [3, "Astra", 100.0, 100.0, 0.0]]
--- check source absent | Does not use SUM(DISTINCT)
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source | Adds up first in WITH steps
[Ww][Ii][Tt][Hh]\s+\w+\s+[Aa][Ss]\s*\(

+++ practice | One payment total for everyone
--- task
This report should show every order with its `items_total`, its `paid_total` (0 if it has no payments) and its `balance`, which is `items_total` minus `paid_total`. All three are rounded to 2 decimals, and the rows are sorted by `id`.

It already adds each table up before joining, but the `paid` step is broken: only one order gets a payment, and it is far too big. Find the missing line and add it.
--- starter
WITH items AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(qty * unit_price) AS items_total
  FROM order_items
  GROUP BY order_id
), paid AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(amount) AS paid_total
  FROM payments
)
SELECT o.id,
       ROUND(i.items_total, 2) AS items_total,
       ROUND(COALESCE(p.paid_total, 0), 2) AS paid_total,
       ROUND(i.items_total - COALESCE(p.paid_total, 0), 2) AS balance
FROM orders o
JOIN items i     ON i.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN paid p ON p.order_id = o.id
ORDER BY o.id;
--- solution
WITH items AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(qty * unit_price) AS items_total
  FROM order_items
  GROUP BY order_id
), paid AS (
  SELECT order_id, SUM(amount) AS paid_total
  FROM payments
  GROUP BY order_id
)
SELECT o.id,
       ROUND(i.items_total, 2) AS items_total,
       ROUND(COALESCE(p.paid_total, 0), 2) AS paid_total,
       ROUND(i.items_total - COALESCE(p.paid_total, 0), 2) AS balance
FROM orders o
JOIN items i     ON i.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN paid p ON p.order_id = o.id
ORDER BY o.id;
--- hint
Run the `paid` step on its own. How many rows does it return?
--- hint
Without a `GROUP BY`, `SUM` adds up the whole table into one row, and `order_id` is a bare column from some row. Add `GROUP BY order_id`.
--- check result | Orders 3 and 7 owe 32.0 and 45.0; every other balance is 0.0
ordered
[[1, 22.5, 22.5, 0.0], [2, 80.1, 80.1, 0.0], [3, 32.0, 0.0, 32.0], [4, 58.0, 58.0, 0.0], [5, 37.8, 37.8, 0.0], [6, 107.0, 107.0, 0.0], [7, 45.0, 0.0, 45.0], [8, 25.0, 25.0, 0.0]]
--- check source | Groups the payments by order
[Ff][Rr][Oo][Mm]\s+payments\s+[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy]\s+order_id
--- check source | Still adds up the items per order
[Ff][Rr][Oo][Mm]\s+order_items\s+[Gg][Rr][Oo][Uu][Pp]\s+[Bb][Yy]\s+order_id

+++ practice | Flights with readings and logs
--- schema
CREATE TABLE flights (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO flights (id, name) VALUES
  (1, 'F-101'), (2, 'F-102'), (3, 'F-103'), (4, 'F-104');
CREATE TABLE readings (
  flight_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES flights(id),
  temp REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO readings (flight_id, temp) VALUES
  (1, 20.0), (1, 22.0), (1, 24.0), (2, 30.0), (4, 10.0), (4, 14.0);
CREATE TABLE logs (
  flight_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES flights(id),
  level TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO logs (flight_id, level) VALUES
  (1, 'warn'), (1, 'warn'), (1, 'info'), (2, 'info'), (3, 'warn');
--- task
This problem has its own database. `flights` has `id` and `name`. `readings` has `flight_id` and `temp`, one row per temperature reading. `logs` has `flight_id` and `level` (`'warn'` or `'info'`), one row per log message.

Return **every** flight, with four columns:

- `name`
- `readings`: how many readings it has, or `0`
- `avg_temp`: the average of its readings, rounded to 1 decimal, or NULL if it has none
- `warnings`: how many of its log messages have `level` `'warn'`, or `0`

F-103 has a warning but no readings, and F-104 has readings but no logs. Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT f.name, COUNT(r.temp) AS readings, ROUND(AVG(r.temp), 1) AS avg_temp, COUNT(l.level) AS warnings
FROM flights f
LEFT JOIN readings r ON r.flight_id = f.id
LEFT JOIN logs l     ON l.flight_id = f.id AND l.level = 'warn'
GROUP BY f.id, f.name
ORDER BY f.name;
--- solution
WITH r AS (
  SELECT flight_id, COUNT(*) AS readings, AVG(temp) AS avg_temp
  FROM readings
  GROUP BY flight_id
), w AS (
  SELECT flight_id, COUNT(*) AS warnings
  FROM logs
  WHERE level = 'warn'
  GROUP BY flight_id
)
SELECT f.name,
       COALESCE(r.readings, 0) AS readings,
       ROUND(r.avg_temp, 1) AS avg_temp,
       COALESCE(w.warnings, 0) AS warnings
FROM flights f
LEFT JOIN r ON r.flight_id = f.id
LEFT JOIN w ON w.flight_id = f.id
ORDER BY f.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: F-101 shows 6 readings and 6 warnings. Each of its 3 readings met each of its 2 warnings.
--- hint
Add each table up to one row per flight first: a `WITH` step for the readings (count and average), and one for the warnings.
--- hint
`LEFT JOIN` both steps to `flights`, and wrap the two counts in `COALESCE(..., 0)`. Leave `avg_temp` NULL when there are no readings.
--- check result | F-101 3 readings, 22.0, 2 warnings; F-103 0, NULL, 1; F-104 2, 12.0, 0
ordered
[["F-101", 3, 22.0, 2], ["F-102", 1, 30.0, 0], ["F-103", 0, null, 1], ["F-104", 2, 12.0, 0]]
--- check source | Adds up first in WITH steps
[Ww][Ii][Tt][Hh]\s+\w+\s+[Aa][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a flight's name
'F-10\d'

=== sql2-15 | Problem solving: the second-highest salary
--- schema
CREATE TABLE employees (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  dept TEXT NOT NULL,
  salary INTEGER
);
INSERT INTO employees (id, name, dept, salary) VALUES
  (1,  'Ana', 'eng',    120000),
  (2,  'Bo',  'eng',    120000),
  (3,  'Cy',  'eng',     95000),
  (4,  'Di',  'eng',     80000),
  (5,  'Ed',  'sales',   70000),
  (6,  'Flo', 'sales',   70000),
  (7,  'Gia', 'ops',     60000),
  (8,  'Hal', 'sales',   55000),
  (9,  'Ivy', 'design',  65000),
  (10, 'Jo',  'eng',     NULL),
  (11, 'Kit', 'design',  65000);
--- teach
Last lesson you hunted down a bug. This lesson and the next are puzzles: questions where the first answer that comes to mind is wrong. You will practice a way of working through them, one step at a time.

This lesson has its own database: one table, `employees`, with the columns `id`, `name`, `dept` (the department) and `salary`. The [[contractors|null-salary]] have a NULL salary. Here is every salary, sorted by department:

| dept | salaries |
| --- | --- |
| design | 65000, 65000 |
| eng | 120000, 120000, 95000, 80000, NULL |
| ops | 60000 |
| sales | 70000, 70000, 55000 |

### The problem

For each department, find the second-highest salary.

Picture a podium where two runners tie for the fastest time. The next-fastest time is the one below theirs, not the second runner's. In the same way, "second-highest" here means the second-highest **[[distinct|distinct-levels]]** amount, where distinct means "different". If two people share the top salary, the answer is the next amount below it.

A department with fewer than two different salaries has no second-highest. It should still appear, with NULL, rather than vanish from the result.

### Step 1: restate it with examples

Work the answer out by hand for each department before writing any SQL:

- eng: the amounts are 120000 and 120000, then 95000. The second-highest is **95000**, not 120000.
- sales: 70000 twice, then 55000. The answer is **55000**.
- ops: one person, one amount. No answer: **NULL**.
- design: everyone earns 65000. Only one different amount, so no answer: **NULL**.

Now you know what a correct query must return.

### Step 2: try the obvious answer and find where it breaks

The lesson Pagination with LIMIT and OFFSET skipped rows. Skip one row, keep the next:

```sql
SELECT salary FROM employees
WHERE dept = 'eng'
ORDER BY salary DESC
LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1;
```

It returns 120000. `OFFSET 1` skipped Ana's row, and Bo's row, with the same salary, came next. `OFFSET` counts rows, not different amounts. It also answers only one department at a time.

### Step 3: say it another way

"The second-highest salary" is the same as "the highest salary that is below the highest salary". That is two `MAX`es, one inside the other. The inner one runs first and gives one value:

```sql
SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees;
```

That is 120000. Now ask for the highest salary below it:

```sql
SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees
WHERE salary < (SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees);
```

That gives 95000, for the whole company. `<` means "less than", so both 120000 rows are left out.

### Step 4: one department

Put the department in **both** queries, inner and outer:

```sql
SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees
WHERE dept = 'eng'
  AND salary < (SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees WHERE dept = 'eng');
```

That gives 95000, the answer from Step 1.

### Step 5: every department

To get one row per department, even for the ones with no answer, start from the list of departments:

```sql
SELECT DISTINCT dept FROM employees;
```

That gives four rows: eng, sales, ops, design. A subquery can stand in `FROM`, where it works like a table. Give it a short name, an alias, so you can point at its columns. Here it is called `d`, so its column is `d.dept`. This is called a **[[derived table|derived-table]]**.

Then, for each department, ask a [[correlated subquery|correlated-recap]] that uses `d.dept` in place of `'eng'`. Here is the same shape with a simpler question, the *highest* salary in each department:

```sql
SELECT d.dept,
       (SELECT MAX(e.salary) FROM employees e WHERE e.dept = d.dept) AS top_salary
FROM (SELECT DISTINCT dept FROM employees) d
ORDER BY d.dept;
```

| dept | top_salary |
| --- | --- |
| design | 65000 |
| eng | 120000 |
| ops | 60000 |
| sales | 70000 |

For the real answer, swap the inner `MAX` for the "highest below the highest" from Step 4, with `d.dept` in place of `'eng'` in both places.

What about ops? No salary in ops is below 60000, so the "below the max" part finds no rows. `MAX` over no rows returns NULL, which is exactly the "no answer" you want. And ops still gets its row, because the query starts from the list of departments.

### Step 6: check the edge cases

Test your result against the awkward cases in the data, the **[[edge cases|edge-cases]]**:

- a tie at the top: eng and sales;
- a department with one person: ops;
- a department where everyone earns the same: design;
- NULL salaries: `MAX` ignores them, so Jo does not disturb eng.

**Watch out:** the inner `MAX` needs `dept = d.dept` too. Without it, the inner query finds the whole company's top salary, 120000, and every department answers "my highest salary below 120000". Design would then get 65000 instead of NULL.

::: context null-salary Why NULL and not 0
A contractor is paid by an outside company, so there is no salary to record. NULL says "no value here". A 0 would be a lie: it would say "this person earns nothing", and it would drag down every average. Aggregates like `MAX`, `MIN`, `AVG` and `SUM` skip NULLs, which is why Jo does not change the answer for eng. `COUNT(salary)` skips them too, while `COUNT(*)` counts every row, NULL or not.
:::

::: context distinct-levels Second row or second amount
The same five eng rows, seen two ways.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="90" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">rows, highest first</text>
  <text x="270" y="18" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">different amounts</text>
  <rect x="30" y="28" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="45" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1st: 120000</text>
  <rect x="30" y="56" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="90" y="73" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2nd: 120000</text>
  <rect x="30" y="84" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="101" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3rd: 95000</text>
  <rect x="30" y="112" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="129" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4th: 80000</text>
  <text x="90" y="152" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">(Jo's NULL sorts last)</text>
  <rect x="210" y="28" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="270" y="45" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1st: 120000</text>
  <rect x="210" y="56" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="270" y="73" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2nd: 95000</text>
  <rect x="210" y="84" width="120" height="24" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="270" y="101" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3rd: 80000</text>
</svg>
```

`OFFSET 1` picks the orange box on the left. The question asks for the blue box on the right.
:::

::: context derived-table A table made by a query
A **derived table** is a subquery in `FROM`. It exists only while the query runs. SQLite lets you leave off the alias, but then you cannot write `d.dept`, and some other databases (MySQL, and PostgreSQL before version 16) refuse a derived table with no alias at all. So always name it. Derived tables do the same job as `WITH` steps: `WITH d AS (SELECT DISTINCT dept FROM employees)` followed by `FROM d` gives the same rows.
:::

::: context correlated-recap A subquery that runs once per row
You met these in the lesson Correlated subqueries and EXISTS. A **correlated** subquery mentions a column of the outer query, here `d.dept`, so it cannot run on its own: it runs once for each outer row, with that row's value filled in. For the eng row it asks about eng, for the ops row about ops. That is how one query answers four departments without you writing four queries.
:::

::: context edge-cases Testing at the edges
An **edge case** is an input at the boundary of what the code expects: nothing, exactly one, a tie, a missing value. Most bugs live there, because the obvious examples are the ones everyone thinks of. Flight-software testers work the same way: zero fuel, one engine out, two sensors that disagree. A good habit is to put an edge case of each kind into your test data on purpose, as this table does. The advanced course solves this very problem again with a window function, `DENSE_RANK()`, which numbers tied amounts with the same rank.
:::
--- task
Return one row per department, with two columns: `dept`, and `second_salary`, the second-highest **different** salary in that department (or NULL if there is none). Sort by `dept`.
--- starter
SELECT dept, salary AS second_salary
FROM employees
ORDER BY salary DESC
LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1;
--- solution
SELECT d.dept,
       (SELECT MAX(e.salary)
        FROM employees e
        WHERE e.dept = d.dept
          AND e.salary < (SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees WHERE dept = d.dept)
       ) AS second_salary
FROM (SELECT DISTINCT dept FROM employees) d
ORDER BY d.dept;
--- hint
Start from the `top_salary` query in Step 5. It already gives one row per department, sorted by `dept`.
--- hint
Replace its inner `MAX` with Step 4's "highest salary below the highest salary", using `d.dept` wherever Step 4 said `'eng'`.
--- hint
The inner part becomes `(SELECT MAX(e.salary) FROM employees e WHERE e.dept = d.dept AND e.salary < (SELECT MAX(salary) FROM employees WHERE dept = d.dept)) AS second_salary`.
--- check result | design NULL, eng 95000, ops NULL, sales 55000
ordered
[["design", null], ["eng", 95000], ["ops", null], ["sales", 55000]]

+++ practice | The second-lowest salary
--- task
Using the lesson's `employees` table, return one row with one column, `second_lowest`: the second-lowest **different** salary in the whole company. NULL salaries do not count.
--- starter
SELECT salary AS second_lowest
FROM employees
ORDER BY salary
LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1;
--- solution
SELECT MIN(salary) AS second_lowest
FROM employees
WHERE salary > (SELECT MIN(salary) FROM employees);
--- hint
Run the starter: in an A-to-Z sort the NULL salary comes first, so skipping one row only skips Jo. Say the question another way instead.
--- hint
"The second-lowest salary" is "the lowest salary that is above the lowest salary": two `MIN`s, one inside the other.
--- check result | 60000
ordered

\(\s*[Ss][Ee][Ll][Ee][Cc][Tt]

+++ practice | How many share the top
--- task
Using `employees`, return one row per department, with three columns:

- `dept`
- `top_salary`: the highest salary in that department
- `top_earners`: how many people in that department earn exactly that

Start from the list of departments, as in the lesson, and use correlated subqueries for the two numbers. Sort by `dept`.
--- starter
SELECT dept, MAX(salary) AS top_salary
FROM employees
GROUP BY dept
ORDER BY dept;
--- solution
SELECT d.dept,
       (SELECT MAX(e.salary) FROM employees e WHERE e.dept = d.dept) AS top_salary,
       (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM employees e
        WHERE e.dept = d.dept
          AND e.salary = (SELECT MAX(x.salary) FROM employees x WHERE x.dept = d.dept)) AS top_earners
FROM (SELECT DISTINCT dept FROM employees) d
ORDER BY d.dept;
--- hint
The top salary per department is the lesson's `top_salary` query. For the count, ask a second correlated subquery: how many rows in this department have that salary?
--- hint
`(SELECT COUNT(*) FROM employees e WHERE e.dept = d.dept AND e.salary = (SELECT MAX(x.salary) FROM employees x WHERE x.dept = d.dept))`.
--- check result | design 65000 shared by 2, eng 120000 by 2, ops 60000 by 1, sales 70000 by 2
ordered
[["design", 65000, 2], ["eng", 120000, 2], ["ops", 60000, 1], ["sales", 70000, 2]]
--- check source | Uses a correlated subquery
[Ww][Hh][Ee][Rr][Ee]\s+\w+\.dept\s*=\s*\w+\.dept
--- check source absent | Does not type a salary
\d{5}

+++ practice | Who earns the second-highest
--- task
Using `employees`, return the people who earn the second-highest **different** salary in their own department, with the columns `name`, `dept` and `salary`. A department with no second-highest salary has nobody to return. Sort by `dept`, then `name`.
--- starter
SELECT name, dept, salary
FROM employees
ORDER BY dept, salary DESC;
--- solution
SELECT e.name, e.dept, e.salary
FROM employees e
WHERE e.salary = (
  SELECT MAX(x.salary)
  FROM employees x
  WHERE x.dept = e.dept
    AND x.salary < (SELECT MAX(y.salary) FROM employees y WHERE y.dept = e.dept)
)
ORDER BY e.dept, e.name;
--- hint
For each person, work out their department's second-highest salary with the lesson's "highest below the highest", linked to `e.dept`, and keep them if they earn it.
--- hint
`WHERE e.salary = (SELECT MAX(x.salary) FROM employees x WHERE x.dept = e.dept AND x.salary < (SELECT MAX(y.salary) FROM employees y WHERE y.dept = e.dept))`. For design the subquery is NULL, and `= NULL` is never true.
--- check result | Cy (eng, 95000) and Hal (sales, 55000)
ordered
[["Cy", "eng", 95000], ["Hal", "sales", 55000]]
--- check source | Uses a correlated subquery
\w+\.dept\s*=\s*\w+\.dept
--- check source absent | Does not type a name or a salary
'(Cy|Hal)'|\d{5}

+++ practice | Runner-up scores for every team
--- schema
CREATE TABLE teams (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO teams (id, name) VALUES
  (1, 'Apollo'), (2, 'Borealis'), (3, 'Comet'), (4, 'Draco'), (5, 'Eclipse'), (6, 'Fenix');
CREATE TABLE members (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  team_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES teams(id),
  score INTEGER
);
INSERT INTO members (id, team_id, score) VALUES
  (1, 1, 90), (2, 1, 90), (3, 1, 85), (4, 1, 70),
  (5, 2, 60),
  (6, 3, NULL), (7, 3, NULL),
  (8, 4, 75), (9, 4, 75), (10, 4, 75),
  (11, 6, 50), (12, 6, 40), (13, 6, 40), (14, 6, NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database. `teams` has `id` and `name`. `members` has `id`, `team_id` and `score`, which is NULL for a member who has not played yet.

Return **every** team, with two columns:

- `team`: the team's name
- `second_score`: the second-highest **different** score in the team, or NULL if there is none

Check the edges: two members tie at the top of Apollo; Borealis has one member; Comet's members have not played; everyone in Draco scored the same; Eclipse has no members at all. Every team must still appear. Sort by `team`.
--- starter
SELECT t.name AS team, MAX(m.score) AS second_score
FROM teams t
JOIN members m ON m.team_id = t.id
GROUP BY t.id, t.name
ORDER BY t.name;
--- solution
SELECT t.name AS team,
       (SELECT MAX(m.score)
        FROM members m
        WHERE m.team_id = t.id
          AND m.score < (SELECT MAX(x.score) FROM members x WHERE x.team_id = t.id)
       ) AS second_score
FROM teams t
ORDER BY t.name;
--- hint
Start from `teams`, not `members`, so a team with no members still gets a row.
--- hint
For each team, ask the lesson's question with a correlated subquery: the highest score below the team's highest score. Link both levels to `t.id`.
--- hint
Every awkward team comes out NULL on its own: when nothing is below the top, `MAX` over no rows is NULL.
--- check result | Apollo 85, Fenix 40, and NULL for Borealis, Comet, Draco and Eclipse

+++ practice | A clear winner
--- task
In some departments of `employees` the top salary is far ahead of the rest. Return the departments where the highest salary is at least 10000 more than the second-highest **different** salary, with four columns:

- `dept`
- `top`: the highest salary
- `second`: the second-highest different salary
- `gap`: `top` minus `second`

A department with no second-highest salary has no gap and is left out. Sort by `gap`, highest first.
--- starter
SELECT dept, MAX(salary) AS top
FROM employees
GROUP BY dept;
--- solution
WITH levels AS (
  SELECT d.dept,
         (SELECT MAX(e.salary) FROM employees e WHERE e.dept = d.dept) AS top,
         (SELECT MAX(e.salary) FROM employees e
          WHERE e.dept = d.dept
            AND e.salary < (SELECT MAX(x.salary) FROM employees x WHERE x.dept = d.dept)) AS second
  FROM (SELECT DISTINCT dept FROM employees) d
)
SELECT dept, top, second, top - second AS gap
FROM levels
WHERE top - second >= 10000
ORDER BY gap DESC;
--- hint
First build one row per department with its `top` and `second`, the way the lesson did, and name that as a step with `WITH`.
--- hint
Then select from the step, work out `top - second AS gap`, and keep `WHERE top - second >= 10000`.
--- hint
Design and ops have a NULL `second`, so their gap is NULL, and `NULL >= 10000` is not true. They drop out on their own.
--- check result | eng (gap 25000) then sales (gap 15000)
ordered
[["eng", 120000, 95000, 25000], ["sales", 70000, 55000, 15000]]
--- check source | Names a step with WITH
[Ww][Ii][Tt][Hh]\s+\w+\s+[Aa][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a salary other than the threshold
\b(120000|95000|70000|55000|25000|15000)\b

=== sql2-16 | Problem solving: finding what is missing
--- teach
Last lesson you found a value by saying the question another way. This lesson's answer is harder to see: the rows that are *not* there. You will learn three ways to ask for a missing match, and the one that silently goes wrong.

### The picture

A teacher wants to know who has not handed in their homework. Staring at the pile of homework will never show it: the missing names are not in the pile. The teacher has to start from the class list and check each name against the pile.

### The problem

The shop wants to [[win back|win-back]] customers who have never bought anything. "Bought" means an order whose status is `paid` or `shipped`. A customer whose only order was refunded or canceled has not really bought.

Rows in one table with *no* match in another are found with an **[[anti-join|anti-join-picture]]**. An ordinary join cannot do it, because a join only produces rows that match.

Start with a simpler question: customers with no orders at all. There are three standard ways to ask it. All three give Finn and Gus.

### Way 1: NOT EXISTS

```sql
SELECT c.name FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id);
```

You met `EXISTS` in the lesson Correlated subqueries and EXISTS: it is true when the subquery returns at least one row. `NOT EXISTS` is true when it returns none. The `1` after `SELECT` is [[a placeholder|select-one]]: only *whether* a row exists matters, not what is in it. Read it as: "keep the customer when there is no order whose `customer_id` is theirs".

### Way 2: LEFT JOIN, then keep the empty side

```sql
SELECT c.name FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.id IS NULL;
```

The `LEFT JOIN` keeps every customer and fills the order columns with NULL when nothing matches. Its last rows look like this:

| name | o.id | o.status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Eve | 7 | refunded |
| Finn | NULL | NULL |
| Gus | NULL | NULL |

`WHERE o.id IS NULL` keeps only the customers whose order side came back empty.

### Way 3: NOT IN

```sql
SELECT c.name FROM customers c
WHERE c.id NOT IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders);
```

`NOT IN (…)` means "is none of the values in this list". It works here, until the list contains a NULL.

### The trap in NOT IN

`x NOT IN (1, 2, NULL)` means `x <> 1 AND x <> 2 AND x <> NULL` (`<>` is "not equal"). The last comparison is unknown, as the lesson Debugging: the rows NULL made disappear showed, because of **[[three-valued logic|three-valued-recap]]**. So the whole condition can never be true.

Try "customers who never referred anyone". The `referred_by` column holds NULL, 1, 1, 2, NULL, 4, NULL:

```sql
SELECT name FROM customers
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT referred_by FROM customers);
```

No rows at all, and [[no error to warn you|silent-wrong]]. The same question with `NOT EXISTS`:

```sql
SELECT c.name FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM customers k WHERE k.referred_by = c.id);
```

| name |
| --- |
| Cleo |
| Eve |
| Finn |
| Gus |

That is right: only Ada (1), Ben (2) and Dev (4) ever referred anyone. Prefer `NOT EXISTS`. It has no NULL trap.

### Back to the real question

A tempting first try is `JOIN orders … WHERE status NOT IN ('paid', 'shipped')`. That finds customers who have *some* order that is not a purchase, which is a different question. It returns Ben and Eve. Ben is wrong, because Ben's order 4 was shipped. And Finn and Gus are missing: they have no orders to join.

The right shape is Way 1, with the purchase rule inside the subquery, next to the link: keep the customer when no order exists that is theirs **and** is paid or shipped.

**Watch out:** never use `NOT IN` with a subquery whose column can hold NULL. It returns nothing, with no error. Use `NOT EXISTS`.

::: context win-back Anti-joins in real work
"Who has not…?" is one of the most common questions a business asks: users who signed up but never bought, invoices with no payment, products that never sold. Engineers ask it too. A ground station might list every satellite that has sent no status message in the last hour, which is an anti-join between the list of satellites and the table of recent messages. Each of these is a list of rows that are *not* in some other table.
:::

::: context anti-join-picture The rows with no line
Each customer, with a line to each of their orders. The anti-join keeps the customers with no line at all.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="12" y="14" width="44" height="26" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="34" y="32" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Ada</text>

  <text x="181" y="32" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Dev</text>
  <rect x="208" y="14" width="44" height="26" rx="4" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="230" y="32" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Eve</text>

  <line x1="34" y1="40" x2="18" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>

  <line x1="34" y1="40" x2="50" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>

  <text x="34" y="94" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2</text>
```
