```svg
  <line x1="83" y1="40" x2="75" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="83" y1="40" x2="91" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="75" y="94" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3</text>
  <text x="91" y="94" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4</text>
  <line x1="132" y1="40" x2="132" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="132" y="94" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">5</text>
  <line x1="181" y1="40" x2="181" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="181" y="94" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">6</text>
  <line x1="230" y1="40" x2="230" y2="80" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="230" y="94" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">7</text>
  <text x="303" y="64" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">no orders</text>
  <text x="180" y="120" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">numbers are order ids</text>
</svg>
```

The opposite, "customers that *do* have a match", is called a **semi-join**, and `EXISTS` is the usual way to write it.
:::

::: context select-one Why SELECT 1
Inside `EXISTS`, the columns you select are never looked at. People write `SELECT 1` to say so plainly; `SELECT *` would work the same. The database can also stop searching at the first matching row, because one row is already enough to answer "does any exist?". That makes `NOT EXISTS` a cheap question even against a large table, especially when the linking column has an index, which the advanced course covers.
:::

::: context three-valued-recap True, false and unknown
In SQL a condition can be true, false or unknown, and any comparison with NULL is unknown. `AND` needs every part to be true. If one part is false, the whole thing is false. If none is false but one is unknown, the whole thing is unknown. So `3 NOT IN (1, 2, NULL)` is unknown, and `WHERE` throws the row away. You can see it in SQLite: `SELECT 3 NOT IN (1, 2, NULL);` returns NULL, not 1. `IN` on its own is kinder: `1 IN (1, 2, NULL)` is still true, because one match is enough.
:::

::: context silent-wrong Wrong answers without errors
An error is annoying but honest: you know something broke. An empty result looks like a real answer, "nobody", and it can go into a report or a mailing list unnoticed. That is why experienced engineers are more afraid of silent bugs than loud ones. If you ever must use `NOT IN` with a subquery, filter the NULLs out inside it: `WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT referred_by FROM customers WHERE referred_by IS NOT NULL)` gives Cleo, Eve, Finn and Gus, the right answer.
:::
--- task
Return the names of the customers who have **no** order with status `paid` or `shipped`. Include customers with no orders at all. Sort them alphabetically.
--- starter
SELECT DISTINCT c.name
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status NOT IN ('paid', 'shipped')
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM orders o
  WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
)
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
The starter finds Ben, who has bought (order 4), and misses Finn and Gus, who have no orders to join. Start again from Way 1.
--- hint
Ask, per customer, whether a purchase exists: `WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND …)`.
--- hint
Fill the gap with `o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')`, and end with `ORDER BY c.name`.
--- check result | Eve (refunded only), Finn and Gus
ordered
[["Eve"], ["Finn"], ["Gus"]]

+++ practice | Products that never shipped
--- task
Return the `name` of every product that has **never** been in an order whose `status` is `'shipped'`. Products that never sold at all count too. Sort by name.

Use `NOT EXISTS`, with the shipped rule inside the subquery.
--- starter
SELECT p.name
FROM products p
ORDER BY p.name;
--- solution
SELECT p.name
FROM products p
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
  WHERE oi.product_id = p.id AND o.status = 'shipped'
)
ORDER BY p.name;
--- hint
Inside the `NOT EXISTS`, look for one row: an item of this product in a shipped order. That needs `order_items` joined to `orders`.
--- hint
Link to the outer product and check the status in the same `WHERE`: `oi.product_id = p.id AND o.status = 'shipped'`.
--- check result | Bookends, Fountain pen and Planner
ordered
[["Bookends"], ["Fountain pen"], ["Planner"]]
--- check source | Uses NOT EXISTS
[Nn][Oo][Tt]\s+[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a product's name
'(Bookends|Fountain pen|Planner)'

+++ practice | Customers missing from the newsletter
--- task
Return the `name` of every customer whose email is **not** on the newsletter list. Compare the emails cleaned with `lower(trim(...))` on both sides. A customer with no email is not on the list either. Sort by name.

Use a `LEFT JOIN` from `customers` to `newsletter`, then keep the rows where the newsletter side is empty.
--- starter
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
JOIN newsletter n ON n.email = c.email
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN newsletter n ON lower(trim(n.email)) = lower(trim(c.email))
WHERE n.email IS NULL
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
The starter finds the customers who **are** on the list, and only with exactly matching text. Turn it round: `LEFT JOIN`, then keep the rows with no match.
--- hint
Clean both sides in the `ON`, and test a newsletter column that is never NULL in a real match: `WHERE n.email IS NULL`.
--- check result | Cleo, Dev, Eve, Finn and Gus; Ben matches despite his capitals
ordered
[["Cleo"], ["Dev"], ["Eve"], ["Finn"], ["Gus"]]
--- check source | Uses LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source | Keeps the rows with no match
[Ii][Ss]\s+[Nn][Uu][Ll][Ll]

+++ practice | Quiet since May
--- task
The shop wants the customers who have placed **no** order, of any status, on or after `'2024-05-01'`. Customers with no orders at all count too. Return two columns:

- `name`
- `last_order`: the `placed` value of their most recent order, or NULL if they have never ordered

Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, MAX(o.placed) AS last_order
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.placed < '2024-05-01'
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name,
       (SELECT MAX(o.placed) FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) AS last_order
FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM orders o
  WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND o.placed >= '2024-05-01'
)
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Ada is there, because she also has an older order, and Finn and Gus are missing. "No order since May" is an anti-join.
--- hint
Keep a customer `WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND o.placed >= '2024-05-01')`.
--- hint
For `last_order`, a correlated `MAX(o.placed)` in the `SELECT` list gives NULL for a customer with no orders.
--- check result | Ben and Cleo (last ordered 7 April), Finn and Gus (NULL)
ordered
[["Ben", "2024-04-07 14:30:00"], ["Cleo", "2024-04-07 20:10:00"], ["Finn", null], ["Gus", null]]
--- check source | Uses NOT EXISTS
[Nn][Oo][Tt]\s+[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ben|Cleo|Finn|Gus|Ada)'

+++ practice | Pilots without a seat
--- schema
CREATE TABLE pilots (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO pilots (id, name) VALUES
  (1, 'Ana'), (2, 'Bo'), (3, 'Cy'), (4, 'Dee');
CREATE TABLE seats (
  mission TEXT NOT NULL,
  pilot_id INTEGER
);
INSERT INTO seats (mission, pilot_id) VALUES
  ('M1', 1), ('M1', NULL), ('M2', 3), ('M3', NULL);
--- task
This problem has its own database. `pilots` has `id` and `name`. `seats` has `mission` and `pilot_id`, one row per seat on a mission. A seat nobody has been given yet has a NULL `pilot_id`.

Return the `name` of every pilot who has **no** seat on any mission. Sort by name.
--- starter
SELECT name
FROM pilots
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT pilot_id FROM seats)
ORDER BY name;
--- solution
SELECT p.name
FROM pilots p
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM seats s WHERE s.pilot_id = p.id)
ORDER BY p.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: it returns nobody, with no error. The list inside `NOT IN` holds a NULL.
--- hint
`2 NOT IN (1, NULL, 3, NULL)` includes `2 <> NULL`, which is unknown, so it is never true. Ask with `NOT EXISTS` instead, which has no NULL trap.
--- check result | Bo and Dee

+++ practice | Nothing is ever missing
--- task
This query should return the products that have **never** been ordered, sorted by name. The answer is Bookends and Planner. It returns no rows at all.

The bug is in the subquery's `WHERE`. Fix it.
--- starter
SELECT p.name
FROM products p
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM order_items oi
  WHERE oi.product_id = product_id
)
ORDER BY p.name;
--- solution
SELECT p.name
FROM products p
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM order_items oi
  WHERE oi.product_id = p.id
)
ORDER BY p.name;
--- hint
Inside the subquery, a plain column name belongs to the nearest table. Which table does the bare `product_id` belong to?
--- hint
It is `oi.product_id` again, so the test compares each row with itself and is always true. Link to the outer product instead: `oi.product_id = p.id`.
--- check result | Bookends and Planner

[["Bookends"], ["Planner"]]

+++ practice | Satellites gone quiet
--- schema
CREATE TABLE satellites (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO satellites (id, name) VALUES
  (1, 'Aurora'), (2, 'Borealis'), (3, 'Cygnus'), (4, 'Draco'), (5, 'Eos');
CREATE TABLE messages (
  sat_id INTEGER,
  at TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO messages (sat_id, at) VALUES
  (1, '2024-06-01 11:30:00'),
  (1, '2024-06-01 09:00:00'),
  (2, '2024-06-01 10:59:59'),
  (3, '2024-06-01 11:00:00'),
  (NULL, '2024-06-01 11:45:00'),
  (4, '2024-05-31 23:00:00');
--- task
This problem has its own database. `satellites` has `id` and `name`. `messages` has `sat_id` and `at`, one row per status message received. A message that could not be decoded has a NULL `sat_id`.

It is now `'2024-06-01 12:00:00'`. A satellite has **gone quiet** if it sent no message in the last hour: none with `at` on or after `'2024-06-01 11:00:00'`. A satellite that never sent anything has gone quiet too. Return two columns:

- `name`
- `last_message`: the `at` of its most recent message, or NULL if it never sent one

Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT s.name, MAX(m.at) AS last_message
FROM satellites s
JOIN messages m ON m.sat_id = s.id
WHERE m.at < '2024-06-01 11:00:00'
GROUP BY s.id, s.name
ORDER BY s.name;
--- solution
SELECT s.name,
       (SELECT MAX(m.at) FROM messages m WHERE m.sat_id = s.id) AS last_message
FROM satellites s
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM messages m
  WHERE m.sat_id = s.id AND m.at >= '2024-06-01 11:00:00'
)
ORDER BY s.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: it lists every satellite with an old message, including Aurora, which spoke at 11:30. You want satellites with no recent message: an anti-join.
--- hint
Keep a satellite `WHERE NOT EXISTS` a message of its own at or after `'2024-06-01 11:00:00'`. The undecoded message matches no satellite, so it changes nothing.
--- hint
For `last_message`, a correlated `MAX(m.at)` in the `SELECT` list gives NULL for Eos, which never spoke.
--- check result | Borealis (10:59:59), Draco (the day before) and Eos (never); Cygnus at exactly 11:00 is not quiet
ordered

--- check source | Uses NOT EXISTS
[Nn][Oo][Tt]\s+[Ee][Xx][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ss]\s*\(
--- check source absent | Does not type a satellite's name
'(Aurora|Borealis|Cygnus|Draco|Eos)'

=== sql2-17 | Design: choosing the joins for a report
--- teach
Last lesson you found the rows that were missing. This is the last lesson of the course, and it puts the joins together. You will choose each join on purpose, from what the report must show, before you write a line of SQL.

### The picture

Picture a list for a class trip. "Every student, with their permission slip if they have one" means you start from the class register, and a student with no slip still appears, with a blank. "Only the students who have a slip" is a different list. The words of the request decide the list, and in SQL they decide the join.

### Step 1: turn the words into joins

Read the [[requirements|requirements]] (what the report must show) and match them to a join:

| The requirement says… | Use |
| --- | --- |
| "only X that have a Y" | `JOIN` (inner) |
| "every X, with its Y if there is one" | `LEFT JOIN` from X |
| "every X and every Y, matched where possible" | `FULL OUTER JOIN` |
| "every combination of X and Y" | `CROSS JOIN`: calendars, grids |
| "X that has no Y" | `NOT EXISTS` (an anti-join) |

You have used the inner `JOIN`, `LEFT JOIN` and `NOT EXISTS`. The other two are new.

### FULL OUTER JOIN: keep both sides

A `FULL OUTER JOIN` keeps every row from **both** tables. Where a row has no partner, the other side is NULL. It works in [[SQLite 3.39 and later|full-outer-version]].

```sql
SELECT c.name, n.email
FROM customers c
FULL OUTER JOIN newsletter n ON n.email = c.email;
```

It returns 11 rows: Ada matched to `ada@mail.com`, six customers with NULL in `email` (no newsletter row with their exact address), and four newsletter addresses with NULL in `name` (no customer).

### CROSS JOIN: every pair

A `CROSS JOIN` has no `ON`. It pairs every row of one table with every row of the other:

```sql
SELECT c.name, p.category
FROM customers c
CROSS JOIN (SELECT DISTINCT category FROM products) p
WHERE c.id <= 2;
```

| name | category |
| --- | --- |
| Ada | stationery |
| Ada | home |
| Ada | electronics |
| Ben | stationery |
| Ben | home |
| Ben | electronics |

2 customers × 3 categories = 6 rows. It is how you build a grid with [[every day or every cell|cross-join-calendar]], even the empty ones.

### Step 2: three questions for each LEFT JOIN

1. **Which rows must survive?** That table goes in `FROM`, as the [[driving table|driving-table]]. Everything else hangs off it with `LEFT JOIN`.
2. **Where does each filter go?** A condition on a LEFT-joined table goes in its `ON`. In `WHERE` it turns the join back into an inner join, as the lesson LEFT JOIN: where the filter goes showed.
3. **Could this join multiply rows?** If two one-to-many tables hang off the same row, add one of them up first, as in the lesson Debugging: the join that double-counts. Count rows as you add each join.

### Step 3: decide what "nothing" looks like

For a customer with no matching rows, each aggregate gives something different:

```sql
SELECT c.name,
       COUNT(oi.qty) AS item_lines,
       SUM(oi.qty) AS units,
       COALESCE(SUM(oi.qty), 0) AS units_or_zero,
       MAX(o.placed) AS latest
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE c.name IN ('Dev', 'Finn')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name;
```

| name | item_lines | units | units_or_zero | latest |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dev | 2 | 3 | 3 | 2024-05-15 11:00:00 |
| Finn | 0 | NULL | 0 | NULL |

`COUNT(column)` gives 0 for no matches. `MAX(…)` gives NULL. `SUM(…)` gives NULL unless you wrap it in `COALESCE(…, 0)`. Decide [[which of these you want|empty-aggregates]] for every column.

### Step 4: build it one join at a time

Add one join, run the query, check the row count, then add the next. For a report on every customer:

- `FROM customers c`: 7 rows.
- add `LEFT JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by` (the referrer): still 7, because each customer has at most one referrer.
- add a `LEFT JOIN` to the customer's live orders: 9 rows, because Ada has 3 live orders.
- add `GROUP BY c.id`: back to 7, one per customer.

Each count is what you expected, so each join is doing its job. A report that is wrong usually [[went wrong at one specific join|one-join-at-a-time]], and you see it the moment it happens.

**Watch out:** "every customer" needs `LEFT JOIN` for **every** join off `customers`, not only the first. One inner `JOIN`, or one filter on a joined table in `WHERE`, and the customers with nothing to match quietly disappear.

::: context requirements Reading the request carefully
**Requirements** are the plain-language description of what a report or program must do. Small words carry big decisions: "every", "only", "at least one", "most recent". When a word is unclear, ask the person who wrote the request before you guess. Should a customer with no orders appear with 0, or not at all? Both are reasonable, and they need different joins. Engineers building mission software write requirements down and number them, so each one can be checked by a test.
:::

::: context full-outer-version FULL OUTER JOIN in SQLite
SQLite added `RIGHT JOIN` and `FULL OUTER JOIN` in version 3.39.0, released in 2022. On older versions you build the same result from pieces you already know: a `LEFT JOIN` from the first table, then `UNION ALL` the rows of the second table that have no match, found with `NOT EXISTS`:

```sql
SELECT c.name, n.email FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN newsletter n ON n.email = c.email
UNION ALL
SELECT NULL, n.email FROM newsletter n
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM customers c WHERE c.email = n.email);
```

It returns the same 11 rows.
:::

::: context cross-join-calendar Grids that show the zeros
A sales chart per day has a problem: a day with no sales has no rows, so it is missing from the chart instead of showing 0. The fix is a `CROSS JOIN` that builds every day paired with every product first. Then you `LEFT JOIN` the real sales onto that grid, and the empty cells come out as 0 with `COALESCE`. A small grid, two days by the three home products:

```sql
SELECT d.day, p.name
FROM (SELECT '2024-06-01' AS day UNION ALL SELECT '2024-06-02') d
CROSS JOIN products p
WHERE p.category = 'home';
```

That gives 2 × 3 = 6 rows.
:::

::: context driving-table The table everything hangs off
The table in `FROM` is sometimes called the **driving table**: it decides which rows the report is about. Every `LEFT JOIN` hangs off it, and the row count never drops below its own.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="42" width="110" height="36" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="65" y="65" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">customers c</text>
  <rect x="220" y="10" width="130" height="36" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="285" y="33" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">customers r</text>
  <rect x="220" y="74" width="130" height="36" rx="6" fill="white" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="285" y="97" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">orders o (live)</text>
  <line x1="120" y1="54" x2="220" y2="28" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="120" y1="66" x2="220" y2="92" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="170" y="30" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">LEFT JOIN</text>
  <text x="170" y="100" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">LEFT JOIN</text>
</svg>
```

Both joins start at `customers c`, so no customer can be lost.
:::

::: context empty-aggregates COUNT(*) counts the empty row
After a `LEFT JOIN`, a customer with no matches still has one row, with NULLs on the joined side. `COUNT(*)` counts rows, so it gives 1 for that customer, not 0. `COUNT(o.id)` counts only the non-NULL values of `o.id`, so it gives 0. In this shop, `SELECT c.name, COUNT(*) FROM customers c LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped') GROUP BY c.id;` wrongly shows 1 live order for Eve, Finn and Gus. Always count a column from the joined table.
:::

::: context one-join-at-a-time Why build in small steps
When a query with five joins gives a wrong total, the bug could be in any of them. When you add joins one at a time and check the row count after each, you know the exact join where the count first went wrong. Engineers build systems the same way: small steps, each tested before the next, so a surprise can only come from the last change. The advanced course makes this even easier with `WITH` steps, where every step can be run and checked on its own.
:::
--- task
Build the **account review**: one row for **every** customer, sorted by name, with four columns:

- `name`;
- `referrer`: the name of the customer who referred them, or NULL;
- `live_orders`: their number of `paid` or `shipped` orders, 0 if none;
- `last_order`: the date of their most recent `paid` or `shipped` order, written `'YYYY-MM-DD'` with no time, or NULL if none.

`date(…)` from the lesson Dates and times keeps only the date part of a timestamp.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, r.name AS referrer, COUNT(o.id) AS live_orders, MAX(o.placed) AS last_order
FROM customers c
JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
JOIN orders o    ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name, r.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name,
       r.name AS referrer,
       COUNT(o.id) AS live_orders,
       date(MAX(o.placed)) AS last_order
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
LEFT JOIN orders o
  ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name, r.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
"Every customer" means both joins must be `LEFT JOIN`s from `customers`. The starter uses two inner joins and loses four customers.
--- hint
The status filter belongs in the orders join's `ON`: `LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')`. Remove the `WHERE`.
--- hint
Wrap the latest timestamp in `date(…)`: `date(MAX(o.placed)) AS last_order`. It stays NULL when there are no live orders.
--- check result | All seven customers, with NULLs and zeros where nothing matched
ordered
[["Ada", null, 3, "2024-06-01"], ["Ben", "Ada", 1, "2024-04-07"], ["Cleo", "Ada", 1, "2024-04-07"], ["Dev", "Ben", 1, "2024-05-15"], ["Eve", null, 0, null], ["Finn", "Dev", 0, null], ["Gus", null, 0, null]]

+++ practice | Live units for every product
--- task
Return **every** product, with two columns:

- `name`
- `live_units`: the total `qty` of it in orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, or `0` if none

Products that never sold, and products that only sold in canceled or refunded orders, still appear. Sort by `name`.

Name the live order items as a step with `WITH` (items joined to their orders, keeping only live ones), then `LEFT JOIN` that step to `products`.
--- starter
SELECT p.name, SUM(oi.qty) AS live_units
FROM products p
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.product_id = p.id
GROUP BY p.id, p.name
ORDER BY p.name;
--- solution
WITH live AS (
  SELECT oi.product_id, oi.qty
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
  WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
)
SELECT p.name, COALESCE(SUM(l.qty), 0) AS live_units
FROM products p
LEFT JOIN live l ON l.product_id = p.id
GROUP BY p.id, p.name
ORDER BY p.name;
--- hint
"Every product" means `products` is the driving table, and everything else hangs off it with a `LEFT JOIN`.
--- hint
The live items need two tables, so build them first: `WITH live AS (SELECT oi.product_id, oi.qty FROM order_items oi JOIN orders o ON ... WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped'))`.
--- hint
`SUM` over no rows is NULL. Wrap it: `COALESCE(SUM(l.qty), 0)`.
--- check result | Notebook 6, USB cable 5, Mug 3, Headphones 2, Desk lamp and Fountain pen 1, Bookends and Planner 0
ordered
[["Bookends", 0], ["Desk lamp", 1], ["Fountain pen", 1], ["Headphones", 2], ["Mug", 3], ["Notebook", 6], ["Planner", 0], ["USB cable", 5]]
--- check source | Uses LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source | Turns an empty sum into 0
[Cc][Oo][Aa][Ll][Ee][Ss][Cc][Ee]\s*\(

+++ practice | The full grid of customers and categories
--- task
For the customers in London and Paris, build a grid with **every** pair of customer and product category, even pairs with no purchases. Return three columns:

- `name`
- `category`
- `units`: the total `qty` that customer bought in that category, in any order of any status, or `0`

Sort by `name`, then `category`. Use a `CROSS JOIN` for the grid.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, p.category, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE c.city IN ('London', 'Paris')
GROUP BY c.id, c.name, p.category
ORDER BY c.name, p.category;
--- solution
WITH bought AS (
  SELECT o.customer_id, p.category, SUM(oi.qty) AS units
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
  GROUP BY o.customer_id, p.category
)
SELECT c.name, k.category, COALESCE(b.units, 0) AS units
FROM customers c
CROSS JOIN (SELECT DISTINCT category FROM products) k
LEFT JOIN bought b ON b.customer_id = c.id AND b.category = k.category
WHERE c.city IN ('London', 'Paris')
ORDER BY c.name, k.category;
--- hint
Run the starter: Gus is missing, and so is Dev's stationery. A grid needs every pair first, then the purchases hung onto it.
--- hint
Build the pairs with `customers c CROSS JOIN (SELECT DISTINCT category FROM products) k`. Add up the purchases per customer and category in a `WITH` step.
--- hint
`LEFT JOIN` the step on both columns: `ON b.customer_id = c.id AND b.category = k.category`, and show a missing total as `COALESCE(b.units, 0)`.
--- check result | Twelve rows for Ada, Ben, Dev and Gus; Dev's stationery and all of Gus's are 0
ordered
[["Ada", "electronics", 4], ["Ada", "home", 1], ["Ada", "stationery", 4], ["Ben", "electronics", 2], ["Ben", "home", 1], ["Ben", "stationery", 1], ["Dev", "electronics", 1], ["Dev", "home", 2], ["Dev", "stationery", 0], ["Gus", "electronics", 0], ["Gus", "home", 0], ["Gus", "stationery", 0]]
--- check source | Uses CROSS JOIN
[Cc][Rr][Oo][Ss][Ss]\s+[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not type a category
'(electronics|home|stationery)'

+++ practice | Customers and subscribers, side by side
--- task
Line up the two lists of addresses. Clean every email with `lower(trim(...))`, leave out customers with no email, and count each newsletter address once. Return one row for every address that is on **either** list, with three columns:

- `email`: the cleaned address
- `customer`: the name of the customer with that address, or NULL if none
- `subscribed`: `1` if it is on the newsletter, `0` if not

Sort by `email`. Put each cleaned list in a `WITH` step, then join them with a `FULL OUTER JOIN`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, n.email
FROM customers c
FULL OUTER JOIN newsletter n ON n.email = c.email;
--- solution
WITH c AS (
  SELECT name, lower(trim(email)) AS email
  FROM customers
  WHERE email IS NOT NULL
), n AS (
  SELECT DISTINCT lower(trim(email)) AS email
  FROM newsletter
)
SELECT COALESCE(c.email, n.email) AS email,
       c.name AS customer,
       CASE WHEN n.email IS NULL THEN 0 ELSE 1 END AS subscribed
FROM c
FULL OUTER JOIN n ON n.email = c.email
ORDER BY email;
--- hint
Clean both lists first, each in its own step. `SELECT DISTINCT` on the newsletter side merges Zoe's two spellings into one address.
--- hint
After a `FULL OUTER JOIN`, either side can be NULL. The address is whichever side has one: `COALESCE(c.email, n.email)`.
--- hint
`subscribed` is a `CASE` on the newsletter side: `CASE WHEN n.email IS NULL THEN 0 ELSE 1 END`.
--- check result | Eight addresses: ada and ben on both lists, four customers not subscribed, max and zoe with no customer
ordered
[["ada@mail.com", "Ada", 1], ["ben@mail.com", "Ben", 1], ["dev@work.io", "Dev", 0], ["eve@mail.com", "Eve", 0], ["finn@work.io", "Finn", 0], ["gus@post.org", "Gus", 0], ["max@post.org", null, 1], ["zoe@mail.com", null, 1]]
--- check source | Uses FULL OUTER JOIN

--- check source absent | Does not type an address
'[a-z]+@

+++ practice | Orders and referrals, without the fan-out
--- task
Return **every** customer, with three columns:

- `name`
- `orders`: how many orders they placed, of any status
- `referrals`: how many customers they referred

Both hang off `customers`, and Ada has 3 orders and 2 referrals. Joining both at once would give her 6 of each. Customers with neither show `0` and `0`. Sort by `name`.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COUNT(o.id) AS orders, COUNT(k.id) AS referrals
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o    ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN customers k ON k.referred_by = c.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
WITH o AS (
  SELECT customer_id, COUNT(*) AS orders
  FROM orders
  GROUP BY customer_id
), r AS (
  SELECT referred_by, COUNT(*) AS referrals
  FROM customers
  WHERE referred_by IS NOT NULL
  GROUP BY referred_by
)
SELECT c.name,
       COALESCE(o.orders, 0) AS orders,
       COALESCE(r.referrals, 0) AS referrals
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN o ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN r ON r.referred_by = c.id
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Run the starter: Ada shows 6 and 6. Two one-to-many joins off the same row multiply each other.
--- hint
Count each one on its own first, one row per customer: orders per `customer_id`, and referrals per `referred_by`. Then `LEFT JOIN` both counts to `customers`.
--- check result | Ada 3 and 2, Ben 2 and 1, Dev 1 and 1, Cleo and Eve 1 and 0, Finn and Gus 0 and 0
ordered
[["Ada", 3, 2], ["Ben", 2, 1], ["Cleo", 1, 0], ["Dev", 1, 1], ["Eve", 1, 0], ["Finn", 0, 0], ["Gus", 0, 0]]
--- check source | Uses LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Cleo|Dev|Eve|Finn|Gus)'

+++ practice | The customers one join dropped
--- task
This report should show **every** customer with the total `units` they ordered (the sum of `qty`, any status), or `0` if none, sorted by name. Finn and Gus, who have no orders, are missing.

The first join is already a `LEFT JOIN`. Find the join that drops them again and fix it.
--- starter
SELECT c.name, COALESCE(SUM(oi.qty), 0) AS units
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o  ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- solution
SELECT c.name, COALESCE(SUM(oi.qty), 0) AS units
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id, c.name
ORDER BY c.name;
--- hint
Finn's row reaches the second join with `o.id` = NULL. An inner join needs a match, and NULL matches nothing.
--- hint
"Every customer" needs `LEFT JOIN` for every join off `customers`, all the way down the chain: `LEFT JOIN order_items oi`.
--- check result | Ada 9, Ben 4, Cleo 3, Dev 3, Eve 1, Finn 0, Gus 0
ordered
[["Ada", 9], ["Ben", 4], ["Cleo", 3], ["Dev", 3], ["Eve", 1], ["Finn", 0], ["Gus", 0]]
--- check source | Both joins are LEFT JOINs
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn][\s\S]*[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Cleo|Dev|Eve|Finn|Gus)'

+++ practice | A sales chart with the empty days
--- task
Build the data for a daily sales chart from `'2024-04-05'` to `'2024-04-08'`, one row for **every** day, even the days with no sales. Count only orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`. Return three columns:

- `day`: the date, like `'2024-04-05'`
- `orders`: how many orders were placed that day
- `revenue`: the sum of `qty * unit_price` over those orders, rounded to 2 decimals, or `0`

Make the four days with `SELECT ... UNION ALL SELECT ...` in a `WITH` step. Sort by `day`.
--- starter
SELECT date(o.placed) AS day, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders o
WHERE o.placed >= '2024-04-05' AND o.placed < '2024-04-09'
GROUP BY day
ORDER BY day;
--- solution
WITH days AS (
  SELECT '2024-04-05' AS day
  UNION ALL SELECT '2024-04-06'
  UNION ALL SELECT '2024-04-07'
  UNION ALL SELECT '2024-04-08'
), live AS (
  SELECT o.id, date(o.placed) AS day, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) AS total
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
  GROUP BY o.id
)
SELECT d.day,
       COUNT(l.id) AS orders,
       ROUND(COALESCE(SUM(l.total), 0), 2) AS revenue
FROM days d
LEFT JOIN live l ON l.day = d.day
GROUP BY d.day
ORDER BY d.day;
--- hint
A day with no orders has no rows in `orders`, so it can never come out of a query that starts there. Start from a list of the days instead.
--- hint
Make the days in a step: `WITH days AS (SELECT '2024-04-05' AS day UNION ALL SELECT '2024-04-06' ...)`. Add up each live order to one row with its `day` and `total` in a second step.
--- hint
`LEFT JOIN` the orders step onto the days, count `l.id`, and wrap the sum in `COALESCE(..., 0)`.
--- check result | 5 and 6 April 0, 7 April 2 orders and 95.8, 8 April 0
ordered
[["2024-04-05", 0, 0.0], ["2024-04-06", 0, 0.0], ["2024-04-07", 2, 95.8], ["2024-04-08", 0, 0.0]]
--- check source | Starts from a list of days
[Uu][Nn][Ii][Oo][Nn]\s+[Aa][Ll][Ll]
--- check source | Uses LEFT JOIN
[Ll][Ee][Ff][Tt]\s+([Oo][Uu][Tt][Ee][Rr]\s+)?[Jj][Oo][Ii][Nn]

=== sql2-gate | SQL, intermediate: mastery gate
--- teach
This gate covers the whole course: joins of every kind, grouping, subqueries and `EXISTS`, `CASE`, text and dates, NULLs, unions, pagination, changing data, and the fan-outs that make totals wrong. It has ten problems you have not seen, most of them on the shop's database, and questions that check you understand why the queries work. To get ready, redo the practice problems of the lessons that felt hard, especially the debugging lessons, and build each query one join at a time, checking the row count as you go.
--- gate
pass 7
questions 9
minutes 112

+++ problem | The referral leaderboard
--- task
For every customer who referred at least one other customer, return three columns:

- `referrer`: the referrer's name
- `referred`: how many customers they referred
- `referred_spend`: the total spend of the customers they referred, rounded to 2 decimals, or `0` if those customers bought nothing

A customer's spend is the sum of `qty * unit_price` over their orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`. Sort by `referred_spend`, highest first, then by `referrer`.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
WITH spend AS (
  SELECT o.customer_id, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) AS total
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
  GROUP BY o.customer_id
)
SELECT r.name AS referrer,
       COUNT(*) AS referred,
       ROUND(COALESCE(SUM(s.total), 0), 2) AS referred_spend
FROM customers c
JOIN customers r  ON r.id = c.referred_by
LEFT JOIN spend s ON s.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY r.id, r.name
ORDER BY referred_spend DESC, r.name;
--- check result | Ben 1 and 107.0, Ada 2 and 95.8, Dev 1 and 0.0 (Finn bought nothing)
ordered
[["Ben", 1, 107.0], ["Ada", 2, 95.8], ["Dev", 1, 0.0]]
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Dev|Finn|Cleo)'
--- check source absent | Does not type the totals
107|95\.8

+++ problem | The monthly board
--- task
Return one row per month in which orders were placed, with four columns:

- `month`: the month of `placed`, as `'YYYY-MM'`
- `orders`: how many orders were placed that month, of any status
- `live_revenue`: the sum of `qty * unit_price` over that month's orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, rounded to 2 decimals
- `lost_orders`: how many of that month's orders are `'canceled'` or `'refunded'`

Sort by `month`.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
WITH t AS (
  SELECT o.id, o.status, strftime('%Y-%m', o.placed) AS month,
         SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) AS total
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  GROUP BY o.id
)
SELECT month,
       COUNT(*) AS orders,
       ROUND(SUM(CASE WHEN status IN ('paid', 'shipped') THEN total ELSE 0 END), 2) AS live_revenue,
       SUM(CASE WHEN status IN ('canceled', 'refunded') THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS lost_orders
FROM t
GROUP BY month
ORDER BY month;
--- check result | March 3 orders, 102.6, 1 lost; April 2, 95.8, 0; May 2, 107.0, 1; June 1, 25.0, 0
ordered
[["2024-03", 3, 102.6, 1], ["2024-04", 2, 95.8, 0], ["2024-05", 2, 107.0, 1], ["2024-06", 1, 25.0, 0]]
--- check source absent | Does not type a month
'2024-0\d'
--- check source absent | Does not type the revenue
102\.6|95\.8

+++ problem | The order before the last
--- task
For every customer who has placed at least 2 orders (any status), return four columns:

- `name`
- `latest`: the date (no time) of their most recent order
- `previous`: the date of the order they placed just before that one
- `days_between`: the number of days from `previous` to `latest`

Sort by `name`.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
WITH d AS (
  SELECT c.name,
         (SELECT MAX(o.placed) FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) AS latest,
         (SELECT MAX(o.placed) FROM orders o
          WHERE o.customer_id = c.id
            AND o.placed < (SELECT MAX(x.placed) FROM orders x WHERE x.customer_id = c.id)) AS previous
  FROM customers c
)
SELECT name,
       date(latest) AS latest,
       date(previous) AS previous,
       julianday(date(latest)) - julianday(date(previous)) AS days_between
FROM d
WHERE previous IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY name;
--- check result | Ada: 2024-03-20 then 2024-06-01, 73 days; Ben: 2024-03-21 then 2024-04-07, 17 days
ordered
[["Ada", "2024-06-01", "2024-03-20", 73.0], ["Ben", "2024-04-07", "2024-03-21", 17.0]]
--- check source absent | Does not type a date
'2024-0\d-\d\d'
--- check source absent | Does not type the day counts
\b(73|17)\b

+++ problem | Subscribers who never bought
--- task
Return every address on the `newsletter` that does **not** belong to any customer, with two columns:

- `email`: the address cleaned with `lower(trim(...))`
- `first_signed_up`: the earliest `signed_up` date among the newsletter rows with that cleaned address

Compare addresses cleaned on both sides. An address that is on the newsletter twice, in two spellings, appears once. Sort by `email`.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
SELECT lower(trim(n.email)) AS email, MIN(n.signed_up) AS first_signed_up
FROM newsletter n
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM customers c
  WHERE lower(trim(c.email)) = lower(trim(n.email))
)
GROUP BY lower(trim(n.email))
ORDER BY email;
--- check result | max@post.org (2024-06-11) and zoe@mail.com (2024-02-14, her first spelling)
ordered
[["max@post.org", "2024-06-11"], ["zoe@mail.com", "2024-02-14"]]
--- check source absent | Does not type an address
'(max|zoe|Zoe)@
--- check source | Cleans the addresses
[Ll][Oo][Ww][Ee][Rr]\s*\(\s*[Tt][Rr][Ii][Mm]

+++ problem | Archive the dead orders
--- task
The shop is moving its canceled and refunded orders out of the live tables. Do it in this order:

1. Create a table `archived_orders` with three columns: `id` (`INTEGER`), `customer` (`TEXT`) and `total` (`REAL`).
2. Fill it with one row for every order whose `status` is `'canceled'` or `'refunded'`: its `id`, its customer's name, and its total, the sum of `qty * unit_price` over its items, rounded to 2 decimals.
3. Delete those orders' rows from `order_items` and from `payments`.
4. Delete those orders from `orders`.

Every other order, item and payment must stay exactly as it was.
--- starter
-- 1. Create the archive table.

-- 2. Fill it.

-- 3. Delete the items and payments of those orders.

-- 4. Delete the orders.
--- solution
CREATE TABLE archived_orders (
  id INTEGER,
  customer TEXT,
  total REAL
);

INSERT INTO archived_orders (id, customer, total)
SELECT o.id, c.name, ROUND(SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price), 2)
FROM orders o
JOIN customers c    ON c.id = o.customer_id
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.status IN ('canceled', 'refunded')
GROUP BY o.id, c.name;

DELETE FROM order_items
WHERE order_id IN (SELECT id FROM orders WHERE status IN ('canceled', 'refunded'));

DELETE FROM payments
WHERE order_id IN (SELECT id FROM orders WHERE status IN ('canceled', 'refunded'));

DELETE FROM orders WHERE status IN ('canceled', 'refunded');
--- check query | The archive holds order 3 (Ben, 32.0) and order 7 (Eve, 45.0)
SELECT id, customer, total FROM archived_orders ORDER BY id
=> [[3, "Ben", 32.0], [7, "Eve", 45.0]]
--- check query | Orders 1, 2, 4, 5, 6 and 8 are left
SELECT id FROM orders ORDER BY id
=> [[1], [2], [4], [5], [6], [8]]
--- check query | 11 items are left, none of them orphaned
SELECT COUNT(*), SUM(order_id IN (SELECT id FROM orders)) FROM order_items
=> [[11, 11]]
--- check query | 7 payments are left, and order 7's two are gone
SELECT COUNT(*), SUM(order_id = 7) FROM payments
=> [[7, 0]]

+++ problem | Pages two and three of the best sellers
--- task
The shop ranks **every** product by its live revenue: the sum of `qty * unit_price` over its items in orders whose `status` is `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, or `0` for a product with none. The ranking is highest revenue first, with ties broken by `name` A to Z, and it shows 3 products per page.

In one query, return pages 2 and 3 together: skip the first page, then return up to 6 rows. Return two columns:

- `name`
- `revenue`: rounded to 2 decimals
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
WITH rev AS (
  SELECT oi.product_id, SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) AS revenue
  FROM order_items oi
  JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id
  WHERE o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
  GROUP BY oi.product_id
)
SELECT p.name, ROUND(COALESCE(r.revenue, 0), 2) AS revenue
FROM products p
LEFT JOIN rev r ON r.product_id = p.id
ORDER BY COALESCE(r.revenue, 0) DESC, p.name
LIMIT 6 OFFSET 3;
--- check result | Fountain pen 28.8, Notebook 28.0, Mug 27.0, then Bookends and Planner at 0.0
ordered
[["Fountain pen", 28.8], ["Notebook", 28.0], ["Mug", 27.0], ["Bookends", 0.0], ["Planner", 0.0]]
--- check source absent | Does not type a product's name
'(Fountain pen|Notebook|Mug|Bookends|Planner)'
--- check source | Breaks ties by name
[Dd][Ee][Ss][Cc]\s*,\s*(\w+\.)?name

+++ problem | Missions over budget
--- schema
CREATE TABLE missions (
  id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  budget REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO missions (id, name, budget) VALUES
  (1, 'Aries', 100.0), (2, 'Boreas', 50.0), (3, 'Castor', 80.0), (4, 'Deneb', 30.0);
CREATE TABLE costs (
  mission_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES missions(id),
  amount REAL NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO costs (mission_id, amount) VALUES
  (1, 40.0), (1, 40.0), (1, 30.0), (2, 20.0), (4, 35.0);
CREATE TABLE crew (
  mission_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES missions(id),
  person TEXT NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO crew (mission_id, person) VALUES
  (1, 'Ana'), (1, 'Ben'), (2, 'Cai'), (4, 'Dee'), (4, 'Eli'), (4, 'Fay');
--- task
This problem has its own database. `missions` has `id`, `name` and `budget`. `costs` has `mission_id` and `amount`, one row per bill; two bills of the same amount are two real bills. `crew` has `mission_id` and `person`, one row per person.

Return **every** mission, with four columns:

- `name`
- `spent`: the total of its costs, or `0`
- `crew_size`: how many people are on it, or `0`
- `status`: `'over'` when `spent` is more than its `budget`; otherwise `'no crew'` when `crew_size` is 0; otherwise `'ok'`

Sort by `name`.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
WITH s AS (
  SELECT mission_id, SUM(amount) AS spent
  FROM costs
  GROUP BY mission_id
), k AS (
  SELECT mission_id, COUNT(*) AS crew_size
  FROM crew
  GROUP BY mission_id
)
SELECT m.name,
       COALESCE(s.spent, 0) AS spent,
       COALESCE(k.crew_size, 0) AS crew_size,
       CASE
         WHEN COALESCE(s.spent, 0) > m.budget THEN 'over'
         WHEN COALESCE(k.crew_size, 0) = 0 THEN 'no crew'
         ELSE 'ok'
       END AS status
FROM missions m
LEFT JOIN s ON s.mission_id = m.id
LEFT JOIN k ON k.mission_id = m.id
ORDER BY m.name;
--- check result | Aries 110 spent, 2 crew, over; Boreas ok; Castor 0, 0, no crew; Deneb 35, 3, over
ordered
[["Aries", 110.0, 2, "over"], ["Boreas", 20.0, 1, "ok"], ["Castor", 0, 0, "no crew"], ["Deneb", 35.0, 3, "over"]]
--- check source absent | Does not use SUM(DISTINCT), which merges the two 40.00 bills
[Ss][Uu][Mm]\s*\(\s*[Dd][Ii][Ss][Tt][Ii][Nn][Cc][Tt]
--- check source absent | Does not type a mission's name
'(Aries|Boreas|Castor|Deneb)'

+++ problem | The coupon report
--- task
Return one row per coupon, with four columns:

- `coupon`: the order's `coupon` in capitals with spaces trimmed, or `'none'` when it is NULL or blank
- `orders`: how many orders used it, of any status
- `live_orders`: how many of those orders have `status` `'paid'` or `'shipped'`
- `live_revenue`: the sum of `qty * unit_price` over the live ones, rounded to 2 decimals

Sort by `orders`, highest first, then by `coupon`.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
WITH t AS (
  SELECT o.id, o.status,
         COALESCE(NULLIF(upper(trim(o.coupon)), ''), 'none') AS coupon,
         SUM(oi.qty * oi.unit_price) AS total
  FROM orders o
  JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  GROUP BY o.id
)
SELECT coupon,
       COUNT(*) AS orders,
       SUM(CASE WHEN status IN ('paid', 'shipped') THEN 1 ELSE 0 END) AS live_orders,
       ROUND(SUM(CASE WHEN status IN ('paid', 'shipped') THEN total ELSE 0 END), 2) AS live_revenue
FROM t
GROUP BY coupon
ORDER BY orders DESC, coupon;
--- check result | none 5 orders (3 live, 154.5), SPRING10 2 (2, 117.9), WELCOME5 1 (1, 58.0)
ordered
[["none", 5, 3, 154.5], ["SPRING10", 2, 2, 117.9], ["WELCOME5", 1, 1, 58.0]]
--- check source absent | Does not type a coupon code
'(SPRING10|WELCOME5)'
--- check source absent | Does not type the revenue
154\.5|117\.9

+++ problem | How fast referrals join
--- task
For every customer who **was** referred, return five columns:

- `name`
- `referrer`: the referrer's name
- `days_after`: how many days after the referrer's `joined` date this customer joined
- `speed`: `'fast'` if that is 36 days or fewer, `'slow'` otherwise
- `bought`: `1` if this customer has at least one order with `status` `'paid'` or `'shipped'`, `0` if not

Sort by `days_after`, then by `name`.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
SELECT c.name,
       r.name AS referrer,
       julianday(c.joined) - julianday(r.joined) AS days_after,
       CASE WHEN julianday(c.joined) - julianday(r.joined) <= 36 THEN 'fast' ELSE 'slow' END AS speed,
       EXISTS (
         SELECT 1 FROM orders o
         WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status IN ('paid', 'shipped')
       ) AS bought
FROM customers c
JOIN customers r ON r.id = c.referred_by
ORDER BY days_after, c.name;
--- check result | Ben 19 days fast, Cleo 36 fast (on the edge), Dev 37 slow, Finn 38 slow and has not bought
ordered
[["Ben", "Ada", 19.0, "fast", 1], ["Cleo", "Ada", 36.0, "fast", 1], ["Dev", "Ben", 37.0, "slow", 1], ["Finn", "Dev", 38.0, "slow", 0]]
--- check source absent | Does not type any name
'(Ada|Ben|Cleo|Dev|Finn)'
--- check source absent | Does not type the day counts
\b(19|37|38)\b

+++ problem | Customers who bought from every category
--- task
Return the `name` of every customer who has bought at least one product from **every** product category, in orders of any status. Sort by name.

The shop may add categories later, and the query must stay right when it does: do not type the categories or how many there are.
--- starter
-- Write your query here.
--- solution
SELECT c.name
FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
  SELECT 1 FROM products p
  WHERE NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM orders o
    JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    JOIN products q     ON q.id = oi.product_id
    WHERE o.customer_id = c.id AND q.category = p.category
  )
)
ORDER BY c.name;
--- check result | Ada and Ben
ordered
[["Ada"], ["Ben"]]
--- check source absent | Does not type a category
'(electronics|home|stationery)'
--- check source absent | Does not type the number of categories
=\s*3\b|>=\s*3\b

+++ question | Filter in ON
--- ask
The shop has 7 customers. Only Eve has a refunded order, and she has one. How many rows does this query return?

```sql
SELECT c.name, o.id
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o
  ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.status = 'refunded';
```
--- answer
7
seven
--- why
The condition is in the `ON`, so it only decides which orders match. A `LEFT JOIN` keeps every customer: Eve gets her refunded order, and the other six get one row each with NULLs. Moved into `WHERE`, the same condition would throw the NULL rows away and leave 1 row.

+++ question | NOT IN and NULL
--- ask
What does this return in SQLite?

```sql
SELECT 3 NOT IN (1, 2, NULL);
```
--- answer
NULL
--- why
`3 NOT IN (1, 2, NULL)` means `3 <> 1 AND 3 <> 2 AND 3 <> NULL`. The last part is unknown, so the whole condition is unknown, which SQLite shows as NULL. In a `WHERE`, unknown drops the row, which is why `NOT IN` with a NULL in the list returns nothing. `NOT EXISTS` has no such trap.

+++ question | Three counts
--- ask
The `customers` table has 7 rows. Two customers have a NULL `city`; the others live in London (2), Paris (2) and Berlin (1). What does this return? Write the three numbers in order, separated by commas.

```sql
SELECT COUNT(*), COUNT(city), COUNT(DISTINCT city) FROM customers;
```
--- answer
7, 5, 3
7,5,3
7 5 3
--- why
`COUNT(*)` counts rows: 7. `COUNT(city)` skips NULLs: 5. `COUNT(DISTINCT city)` counts each different non-NULL value once: London, Paris and Berlin, so 3.

+++ question | Deep pages
--- ask
A table of a million products has an index on `(price, name)`. An app pages through it 20 rows at a time, sorted by `price, name`. How does the work to fetch page `p` compare between `LIMIT 20 OFFSET (p - 1) * 20` and keyset pagination (`WHERE` the rows come after the last one shown, then `LIMIT 20`)?
--- choice correct
With `OFFSET` the database still walks past the (p − 1) × 20 skipped rows, so the work grows with `p`; keyset jumps straight to the bookmark in the index, so every page costs about the same.
--- choice
They cost the same: `LIMIT 20` makes both stop after 20 rows.
--- choice
`OFFSET` is cheaper, because skipped rows are never read.
--- choice
Keyset is slower, because its `WHERE` has to be checked against every row in the table.
--- why
`OFFSET` counts rows: to skip 100,000 of them the database must find them in order and throw them away, so page 5,000 costs far more than page 1. Keyset turns "where was I?" into a `WHERE` that the index can answer directly, so the cost stays close to the 20 rows returned, whatever the page.

+++ question | A subquery for every row
--- ask
`products` has `n` rows and no indexes. In the simple picture of how a correlated subquery runs (the inner query once for each outer row), how does the work for this query grow with `n`?

```sql
SELECT p.name
FROM products p
WHERE p.price > (SELECT AVG(x.price) FROM products x WHERE x.category = p.category);
```
--- choice
O(n): the table is read once.
--- choice
O(n log n): like sorting the table.
--- choice correct
O(n²): for each of the `n` outer rows, the inner query reads all `n` rows again.
--- choice
O(1): the average is worked out once and reused.
--- why
The inner query mentions `p.category`, so it cannot be worked out once: in idea it runs again for every outer row, and each run scans the whole table, n × n. A real database may find a quicker plan, for example by working out each category's average once, but the query as written describes n² work, and on big tables that is worth knowing.

+++ question | Where the double-count comes from
--- ask
This report gives Ben 116.00 paid, but he paid 58.00. His order 4 has 2 items and 2 payments. Which line causes the wrong total?

```sql
SELECT c.name, SUM(p.amount) AS paid
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o        ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi  ON oi.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN payments p ON p.order_id = o.id
GROUP BY c.id;
```
--- choice
`JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id`, because it should be a `LEFT JOIN`.
--- choice correct
`JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id`: the payments are then joined to every item row, so each payment is counted once per item.
--- choice
`LEFT JOIN payments p ON p.order_id = o.id`, because payments should be matched on the customer, not the order.
--- choice
`GROUP BY c.id`, because it should also group by `c.name`.
--- why
Nothing in the report uses `order_items`, but joining it turns order 4 into 2 rows, and each of those rows meets both payments: 2 × 2 = 4 rows, so 58.00 is added twice. Drop the join, or add each table up to one row per order before joining. The `GROUP BY c.id` is fine, because `c.name` depends on the primary key.

+++ question | Why count a column
--- ask
After `customers c LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id`, why does a report count orders with `COUNT(o.id)` rather than `COUNT(*)`?
--- choice
`COUNT(*)` is slower, because it reads every column.
--- choice correct
A customer with no orders still has one row, full of NULLs. `COUNT(*)` counts that row as 1; `COUNT(o.id)` skips the NULL and gives 0.
--- choice
`COUNT(o.id)` counts each order only once, even when orders repeat.
--- choice
`COUNT(*)` skips rows that contain any NULL.
--- why
`COUNT(*)` counts rows, whatever is in them, and a `LEFT JOIN` gives an unmatched customer exactly one row. `COUNT(column)` counts only the non-NULL values, and the right-hand table's `id` is NULL exactly when nothing matched. Counting each value once is `COUNT(DISTINCT ...)`, a different tool.

+++ question | The last day of April
--- ask
An order was placed at `'2024-04-30 18:00:00'`. Does `WHERE placed <= '2024-04-30'` keep it?
--- choice correct
No. The two strings start the same, and the timestamp is longer, so as text it sorts after `'2024-04-30'`.
--- choice
Yes. SQLite sees that both are dates and compares them as dates.
--- choice
Yes. `<=` includes the whole of the last day.
--- choice
It stops with an error, because a date and a timestamp cannot be compared.
--- why
SQLite stores these dates as text and compares them character by character. `'2024-04-30'` is a prefix of `'2024-04-30 18:00:00'`, and a prefix sorts first, so every moment of 30 April after midnight falls outside. End a range with `<` and the first moment you do not want: `placed < '2024-05-01'`.

+++ question | A union of the same column
--- ask
The cities of the 7 customers are London, Paris, NULL, London, Berlin, NULL and Paris. How many rows does this return?

```sql
SELECT city FROM customers
UNION
SELECT city FROM customers;
```
--- answer
4
four
--- why
`UNION` removes duplicate rows from the combined result, and for that job two NULLs count as the same value. What is left is London, Paris, Berlin and one NULL. With `UNION ALL` it would be all 14 rows.

+++ question | The first true WHEN
--- ask
An order totals 107.00. Which `size` does it get, and why?

```sql
CASE
  WHEN total >= 25  THEN 'medium'
  WHEN total >= 100 THEN 'large'
  ELSE 'small'
END AS size
```
--- choice correct
`'medium'`, because `CASE` stops at the first `WHEN` that is true, and 107 is already `>= 25`.
--- choice
`'large'`, because `CASE` picks the most specific condition that is true.
--- choice
`'large'`, because the last true `WHEN` wins.
--- choice
NULL, because two `WHEN`s are true at once.
--- why
`CASE` reads its `WHEN`s from top to bottom and returns the first one that is true. The `'large'` line can never be reached here, because anything `>= 100` is also `>= 25`. Put the narrowest condition first.

+++ question | A column SQLite picks for you
--- ask
What is wrong with this query in SQLite?

```sql
SELECT city, name, COUNT(*)
FROM customers
GROUP BY city;
```
--- choice correct
`name` is neither grouped nor aggregated. SQLite runs it anyway and shows one name per city from some row of the group, without saying which; other databases refuse the query.
--- choice
It stops with an error, because `name` is not in the `GROUP BY`.
--- choice
It returns one row per customer, because `name` is in the `SELECT`.
--- choice
`COUNT(*)` counts the different names in each city instead of the rows.
--- why
The London group holds two customers, but the row shows one name. SQLite calls this a bare column and picks a value from one row of the group. PostgreSQL rejects the query. Group by the column too, or put it inside an aggregate, so the answer means something.

+++ question | One month after 31 January
--- ask
What does this return in SQLite? 2024 is a leap year.

```sql
SELECT date('2024-01-31', '+1 month');
```
--- answer
2024-03-02
'2024-03-02'
--- why
`'+1 month'` adds one to the month number and keeps the day, which gives 31 February. That day does not exist, so SQLite counts on: 29 February is the last day, so the 31st is two days later, 2 March. For "the end of next month", use `'start of month'` first, then move.
